Hyperbolic P ​ ( Φ ) 2 P(\Phi)_{2} -model on the plane
Tadahiro Oh, Leonardo Tolomeo, Yuzhao Wang, and Guangqu Zheng

Abstract
. We study the hyperbolic Φ 2 k + 1 \Phi^{k+1}_{2} -model on the plane. By establishing coming down from infinity for the associated stochastic nonlinear heat equation (SNLH) on the plane, we first construct a Φ 2 k + 1 \Phi^{k+1}_{2} -measure on the plane as a limit of the Φ 2 k + 1 \Phi^{k+1}_{2} -measures on large tori. We then study the canonical stochastic quantization of the Φ 2 k + 1 \Phi^{k+1}_{2} -measure on the plane thus constructed, namely, we study the defocusing stochastic damped nonlinear wave equation forced by an additive space-time white noise (= the hyperbolic Φ 2 k + 1 \Phi^{k+1}_{2} -model) on the plane. In particular, by taking a limit of the invariant Gibbs dynamics on large tori constructed by the first two authors with Gubinelli and Koch (2022), we construct invariant Gibbs dynamics for the hyperbolic Φ 2 k + 1 \Phi^{k+1}_{2} -model on the plane. Our main strategy is to develop further the ideas from a recent work on the hyperbolic Φ 3 3 \Phi^{3}_{3} -model on the three-dimensional torus by the first two authors and Okamoto (2021), and to study convergence of the so-called enhanced Gibbs measures, for which coming down from infinity for the associated SNLH with positive regularity plays a crucial role. By combining wave and heat analysis together with ideas from optimal transport theory, we then conclude global well-posedness of the hyperbolic Φ 2 k + 1 \Phi^{k+1}_{2} -model on the plane and invariance of the associated Gibbs measure. As a byproduct of our argument, we also obtain invariance of the limiting Φ 2 k + 1 \Phi^{k+1}_{2} -measure on the plane under the dynamics of the parabolic Φ 2 k + 1 \Phi^{k+1}_{2} -model.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
35L71, 60H15, 35K05, 35R60

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Hyperbolic
\Phi^{k+1}_{2}
-model

We study the following stochastic damped nonlinear wave equation
(SdNLW) forced by an additive space-time white noise, posed on the plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}
:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{2}u+\partial_{t}u+(1-\Delta)u\,+u^{k}=\sqrt{2}\xi,

(1.1)

where
k\in 2\mathbb{N}+1
,
u
is a real-valued unknown,
and
\xi(x,t)
is a Gaussian space-time white noise on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}

with the space-time covariance given by

\mathbb{E}\big[\xi(x_{1},t_{1})\xi(x_{2},t_{2})\big]=\delta(x_{1}-x_{2})\delta(t_{1}-t_{2}).

With
\vec{u}=(u,\partial_{t}u)
,
define the energy
\mathcal{E}(\vec{u})
by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{E}(\vec{u})&=E(u)+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\partial_{t}u)^{2}dx\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\langle\nabla\rangle u|^{2}dx+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\partial_{t}u)^{2}dx+\frac{1}{k+1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u^{k+1}dx,\end{split}

(1.2)

where
E(u)
is given by

\displaystyle E(u)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\langle\nabla\rangle u|^{2}dx+\frac{1}{k+1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u^{k+1}dx.

(1.3)

Note that the energy
\mathcal{E}(\vec{u})
is
precisely the energy (= Hamiltonian)
for the (deterministic) nonlinear wave equation (NLW):

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{2}u+(1-\Delta)u+u^{k}=0.

(1.4)

Namely,
with
v=\partial_{t}u
, we can write NLW (
1.4
)
in the following Hamiltonian formulation:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}u\\
v\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\partial\mathcal{E}}{\partial u}\rule[-8.53581pt]{0.0pt}{5.69054pt}\\
\frac{\partial\mathcal{E}}{\partial v}\end{pmatrix}.

(1.5)

Similarly, we can write SdNLW (
1.1
) as

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}u\\
v\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\partial\mathcal{E}}{\partial u}\rule[-8.53581pt]{0.0pt}{5.69054pt}\\
\frac{\partial\mathcal{E}}{\partial v}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}0\\
-v+\sqrt{2}\xi\end{pmatrix}.

(1.6)

Consider a
Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}
of the form

\displaystyle``d\vec{\rho}(\vec{u})=\mathcal{Z}^{-1}e^{-\mathcal{E}(\vec{u})}d\vec{u}=d\rho\otimes d\mu_{0}(\vec{u})\text{''},

(1.7)

where
\vec{u}=(u,v)=(u,\partial_{t}u)
,
\rho
is a
\Phi^{k+1}_{2}
-measure on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
and
\mu_{0}
is the (spatial) white noise measure on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
By drawing an analogy to the finite-dimensional Hamiltonian dynamics,
we expect that the Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}
in (
1.7
)
is invariant under the NLW dynamics (
1.4
) on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Moreover, it is easy to see that the white noise measure
\mu_{0}
on
the second component
v=\partial_{t}u

is invariant under the
Ornstein-Uhlenbeck dynamics:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}v=-v+\sqrt{2}\xi.

(1.8)

Thus, by viewing the SdNLW dynamics (
1.6
)
as a superposition
of the NLW dynamics (
1.5
) and the
Ornstein-Uhlenbeck dynamics (
1.8
),
we expect the Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}
in (
1.7
)
to be invariant under the SdNLW dynamics (
1.1
).
Namely,
the SdNLW equation (
1.1
)
is the hyperbolic counterpart of the well-studied stochastic quantization equation
in the parabolic setting (= the parabolic
\Phi^{k+1}_{d}
-model
1
1

1

The subscript
d

denotes the dimension of the underlying space.
)

[
22
,
41
,
55
,
56
,
42
,
36
,
1
,
37
,
2
]
:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}X+(1-\Delta)X\,+X^{k}=\sqrt{2}\xi,

(1.9)

under which the
\Phi^{k+1}_{d}
-measure remains invariant.
For this reason, we also refer to (
1.1
)
as the
hyperbolic
\Phi^{k+1}_{d}
-model
.
The hyperbolic
\Phi^{k+1}_{d}
-model (
1.1
)
corresponds to
the so-called canonical stochastic quantization equation
2
2

2

Namely,
the ‘Hamiltonian’ stochastic quantization equation given as the Langevin equation
with the momentum
v=\partial_{t}u
.
The parabolic
\Phi^{k+1}
-model
is the stochastic gradient flow for the energy functional
E(u)
defined in (
1.3
).

for the
\Phi^{k+1}_{d}
-measure,
and thus is of importance in mathematical physics. See
[
82
]
.
Our main goal in this paper is to construct the global-in-time dynamics
at the Gibbs equilibrium for
the hyperbolic
\Phi^{k+1}_{2}
-model (
1.1
)
posed on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

1.2.
Review of the periodic problem

Following the previous works
[
76
,
38
]
,
Gubinelli, Koch, and the first two authors

[
40
]

studied SdNLW (
1.1
) on the two-dimensional torus
\mathbb{T}^{2}=(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})^{2}
.
By introducing
a proper renormalization (see (
1.25
) below; see also
[
76
,
38
]
),
they constructed global-in-time
invariant Gibbs dynamics for SdNLW (
1.1
) on
\mathbb{T}^{2}

via the so-called Bourgain’s invariant measure argument
[
10
,
11
]
.
See
[
76
]
for the construction of invariant Gibbs dynamics
for the deterministic NLW (
1.4
) on
\mathbb{T}^{2}
, preceding
[
40
]
.
See also
[
71
]
for the corresponding results (for both SdNLW (
1.1
) and NLW (
1.4
)) on a two-dimensional
compact Riemannian manifold without boundary.

Given
L>0
,
define a dilated torus
\mathbb{T}_{L}^{2}
by setting

\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}=(\mathbb{R}/L\mathbb{Z})^{2}.

Then, the aforementioned result in
[
40
]

applies to SdNLW posed on
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}
for any
L>0
.
Our main strategy for studying SdNLW (
1.1
)
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is then to take
a large torus limit
L\to\infty

of the
L
-periodic SdNLW dynamics on
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}
.
As such, we first provide a review of the

L
-periodic problem on the dilated torus
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}

in this subsection
(especially since the presentation in
[
40
]

is only for the
L=1
case).

Let us first introduce some notations.
Fix
L>0
and set

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{2}=(\mathbb{Z}/L)^{2}.

Given
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{2}
,
we set

\displaystyle e_{\lambda}^{L}(x)=\frac{1}{L}e^{2\pi i\lambda\cdot x}

(1.10)

for
x\in\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}
.
Then,
\{e_{\lambda}^{L}\}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{2}}

forms an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})
.
We define the Fourier transform
\widehat{f}(\lambda)
of a function
f
on
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}

by

\displaystyle\widehat{f}(\lambda)=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}}f(x)\overline{e_{\lambda}^{L}(x)}dx,\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{L},

with the associated Fourier series expansion:
3
3

3

Hereafter,
we may drop the inessential factor
2\pi
.

\displaystyle f(x)=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{2}}\widehat{f}(\lambda)e_{\lambda}^{L}(x).

We first go over the construction of the Gibbs measure on
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}
;
see
[
23
,
75
]
for details (with
L=1
).
See also
[
49
]

for the construction of analogous log-correlated Gibbs measures in the one-dimensional setting.
Given
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
let
\mu_{s,L}
denote
a Gaussian measure on
L
-periodic distributions
with the covariance operator
(1-\Delta_{\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}})^{-s}
,
formally defined by
4
4

4

We use
\mathcal{Z}_{s,L}
, etc. to denote various normalizing constants, which
may vary line by line.

\displaystyle\begin{split}d\mu_{s,L}(u)&=\mathcal{Z}_{s,L}^{-1}\exp\bigg(-\frac{1}{2}\|u\|_{{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})}^{2}\bigg)du\\
&=\mathcal{Z}_{s,L}^{-1}\prod_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{2}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\langle\lambda\rangle^{2s}|\widehat{u}(\lambda)|^{2}}d\widehat{u}(\lambda),\end{split}

(1.11)

where
\langle\,\cdot\,\rangle=(1+|\cdot|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}
and
\widehat{u}(\lambda)
,
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{L}
, denotes the Fourier transform of
u
on
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}
.
We note that

\mu_{1,L}
corresponds to the massive Gaussian free field
on
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}
,
while
\mu_{0,L}
corresponds to the white noise on
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}
.
We then set

\displaystyle\vec{\mu}_{L}=\mu_{L}\otimes\mu_{0,L},\quad\text{where}\quad\mu_{L}=\mu_{1,L}.

(1.12)

Given
s\in\mathbb{R}
, let

\vec{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})=H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})\times H^{s-1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}).

Then,
\vec{\mu}_{L}
in (
1.12
) is formally given by

\displaystyle d\vec{\mu}_{L}(u,v)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Z}_{L}^{-1}\exp\bigg(-\frac{1}{2}\|(u,v)\|_{\vec{H}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})}^{2}\bigg)dudv.

(1.13)

Namely,
the measure
\vec{\mu}_{L}
is defined as
the induced probability measure
under the map:

\omega\in\Omega\longmapsto\big(u(\omega),v(\omega)\big)\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})\times\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}),

where
u(\omega)=u_{L}(\omega)
and
v(\omega)=v_{L}(\omega)
are given by
the following Gaussian Fourier series:

u(\omega)=u_{L}(\omega)=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{L}}\frac{g_{L\lambda}(\omega)}{\langle\lambda\rangle}e_{\lambda}^{L}\quad\text{and}\quad v(\omega)=v_{L}(\omega)=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{L}}h_{L\lambda}(\omega)e_{\lambda}^{L}.

(1.14)

Here,

\{g_{n},h_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
denotes a family of independent standard complex-valued Gaussian random variables conditioned that
g_{-n}=\overline{g_{n}}
and
h_{-n}=\overline{h_{n}}
,

n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.
From (
1.14
),
it is easy to see that
\vec{\mu}_{L}=\mu_{1,L}\otimes\mu_{0,L}
is supported on

\vec{H}^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})\setminus\vec{H}^{0}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})

for
s<0
.

In view of
(
1.2
)
and (
1.13
),
the Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}_{L}
on
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}

is formally given by

\displaystyle d\vec{\rho}_{L}(u,\partial_{t}u)=\mathcal{Z}_{L}^{-1}e^{-\frac{1}{k+1}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}}u^{k+1}dx}d\vec{\mu}_{L}(u,\partial_{t}u).

(1.15)

Due to the roughness of the support of
\vec{\mu}_{L}
,
the interaction potential
\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}}u^{k+1}dx
in (
1.15
) is not well defined
and thus a renormalization is required to give a proper meaning to
the expression in (
1.15
).

Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathbf{P}_{N}

be the Dirichlet projection onto the frequencies
\{|\lambda|\leq N\}
.
Then, with
u
as in (
1.14
),
it follows from (
1.14
), (
1.10
), and a Riemann sum approximation that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sigma_{N,L}:\!&=\mathbb{E}\big[\big(\mathbf{P}_{N}u(x)\big)^{2}\big]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{2}\\
|\lambda|\leq N\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\langle\lambda\rangle^{2}}\frac{1}{L^{2}}\\
&\hskip 1.42262pt=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\\
|n|\leq LN\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\langle\frac{n}{L}\rangle^{2}}\frac{1}{L^{2}}\sim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathbf{1}_{|z|\leq N}\frac{dz}{1+|z|^{2}}\sim\log N\end{split}

(1.16)

for
N\gg 1
,
independent of
x\in\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}
,
which diverges to
\infty
as
N\to\infty
(for each fixed period
L>0
).
In particular,
u=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbf{P}_{N}u
is only a distribution
and thus, for any integer
\ell\geq 2
, the power
(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)^{\ell}

does not converge to any limit.
For each
x\in\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}
, we now define
the Wick power
:\!(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)^{\ell}(x)\!:\,=\,:\!(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)^{\ell}(x)\!:_{L}
by
5
5

5

Note that the definition (
1.17
)
of the Wick power depends on the period
L>0
.
We, however, suppress the subscript
L
from the Wick power,
when it is clear from the context.

\displaystyle:\!(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)^{\ell}(x)\!:\,=H_{\ell}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u(x);\sigma_{N,L}),

(1.17)

where
H_{\ell}(x;\sigma)
is the Hermite polynomial of degree
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}:=\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}

with a variance parameter
\sigma>0
.
Arguing as in
[
38
,
39
,
40
]
,
we can show that

:\!(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)^{\ell}\!:
converges, almost surely and
in
L^{p}(\Omega)
for any finite
p\geq 1
,
to a limit, denoted by

:\!u^{\ell}\!:
in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})
,
s<0
.
Then,
by defining
the truncated renormalized potential energy:

\displaystyle R_{N}^{L}(u)=\frac{1}{k+1}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}}:\!(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)^{k+1}(x)\!:dx,

(1.18)

a standard computation shows that
\{R_{N}^{L}(u)\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}

converges to some limit, denoted by
R^{L}(u)
,
in
L^{p}(d\mu_{L})
for any finite
p\geq 1
, as
N\to\infty
.
The convergence
of the truncated renormalized potential energy

\{R_{N}^{L}(u)\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}

together with Nelson’s estimate
implies that the truncated density

\{e^{-R^{L}_{N}(u)}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}

converges to
the limiting density

e^{-R^{L}(u)}
in
L^{p}(d\mu_{L})
for any finite
p\geq 1
,
as
N\to\infty
.
Hence, by defining the renormalized truncated Gibbs measure:

\displaystyle d\vec{\rho}_{N,L}(u,\partial_{t}u)=\mathcal{Z}_{N,L}^{-1}e^{-R_{N}^{L}(u)}d\vec{\mu}_{L}(u,\partial_{t}u),

(1.19)

we then conclude that
the renormalized truncated Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}_{N,L}
converges
in total variation
to the limiting Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}_{L}
given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}d\vec{\rho}_{L}(u,\partial_{t}u)&=\mathcal{Z}_{L}^{-1}e^{-R^{L}(u)}d\vec{\mu}_{L}(u,\partial_{t}u)\\
&=\mathcal{Z}_{L}^{-1}\exp\bigg(-\frac{1}{k+1}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}}:\!u^{k+1}(x)\!:dx\bigg)d\vec{\mu}_{L}(u,\partial_{t}u).\end{split}

(1.20)

Furthermore, for each fixed
0<L<\infty
,
the resulting Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}_{L}
is equivalent
6
6

6

Namely,

\vec{\rho}_{L}
and
\vec{\mu}_{L}
are mutually absolutely continuous.

to the base Gaussian measure
\vec{\mu}_{L}
.

Remark 1.1
.

The first marginal of the Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}_{L}

(namely, after integrating (
1.20
)
in
\partial_{t}u
)
is precisely the
\Phi^{k+1}_{2}
-measure
\rho_{L}
on

, given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}d\rho_{L}(u)&=\mathcal{Z}_{L}^{-1}e^{-R^{L}(u)}d\mu_{L}(u)\\
&=\mathcal{Z}_{L}^{-1}\exp\bigg(-\frac{1}{k+1}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}}:\!u^{k+1}(x)\!:dx\bigg)d\mu_{L}(u),\end{split}

(1.21)

where

\mu_{L}=\mu_{1,L}
is as in (
1.12
).
Note that
\rho_{L}
is the Gibbs measure associated with the energy functional

E(u)
in (
1.3
).
The Gibbs measure

.

Next, we discuss stochastic dynamics associated with the Gibbs measure

\vec{\rho}_{L}
in (
1.20
).
This process is known as
stochastic quantization
[
80
]
.
In the parabolic setting,
Da Prato and Debussche
[
22
]

studied the parabolic
\Phi^{k+1}_{2}
-model (
1.9
) on
\mathbb{T}^{2}
,
associated with the
\Phi^{k+1}_{2}
-measure
\rho_{L=1}
in (

).
With the Wick renormalization,
they constructed global-in-time invariant dynamics for (
1.9
) on
\mathbb{T}^{2}

with the initial data distributed by the
\Phi^{k+1}_{2}
-measure
\rho_{L=1}
. This result is readily applicable to
the parabolic
\Phi^{k+1}_{2}
-model (
1.9
) posed on
the dilated torus
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}
for any
L>0
.
In
[
55
]
, with an intricate use of weighted Besov spaces (see (
2.25
) below),
Mourrat and Weber extended this result
to the parabolic
\Phi^{k+1}_{2}
-model on the plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

We now consider the hyperbolic
\Phi^{k+1}_{2}
-model on
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}

for fixed
L>0
:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{2}u+\partial_{t}u+(1-\Delta)u\,+u^{k}=\sqrt{2}\xi_{L}

(1.22)

with the Gibbsian initial data distributed by the Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}_{L}

in (
1.20
),
where
\xi_{L}
is a space-time white noise on
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
In view of the equivalence of the Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}_{L}
in (
1.20
)
and the base Gaussian measure
\vec{\mu}_{L}
in (
1.12
)
for each fixed

) with the

.

Let
\Phi=\Phi_{L}
be the solution to the linear stochastic damped wave equation on
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}

with the Gaussian initial data distributed by
\vec{\mu}_{L}
in (
1.12
):

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}^{2}\Phi+\partial_{t}\Phi+(1-\Delta)\Phi=\sqrt{2}\xi_{L}\\
(\Phi,\partial_{t}\Phi)|_{t=0}=(\phi_{0},\phi_{1})\quad\text{with }\ \Law(\phi_{0},\phi_{1})=\vec{\mu}_{L}.\end{cases}

Here,
\Law(X)
of a random variable
X
denotes the law of
X
.
Define the linear damped wave propagator
\mathcal{D}(t)
by

\mathcal{D}(t)=e^{-\frac{t}{2}}\frac{\sin\Big(t\sqrt{\frac{3}{4}-\Delta}\Big)}{\sqrt{\frac{3}{4}-\Delta}}

(1.23)

as a Fourier multiplier operator.
Then, the stochastic convolution
\Phi
defined above can be expressed as

\displaystyle\Phi(t)=\big(\partial_{t}\mathcal{D}(t)+\mathcal{D}(t)\big)\phi_{0}+\mathcal{D}(t)\phi_{1}+\sqrt{2}\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{D}(t-t^{\prime})dW_{L}(t^{\prime}),

where
W_{L}
denotes a cylindrical Wiener process on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})
:

\displaystyle W_{L}(t)=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{2}}B_{\lambda}(t)e_{\lambda}^{L}

(1.24)

and

\{B_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{L}}

is defined by

B_{\lambda}(t)=\langle\xi_{L},\mathbf{1}_{[0,t]}\cdot e_{\lambda}^{L}\rangle_{\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}}
.
Here,
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}}
denotes
the duality pairing on
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
Then, we see that
\{B_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
is a family of mutually independent complex-valued
7
7

7

In particular,
B_{0}
is a standard real-valued Brownian motion.

Brownian motions conditioned that
B_{-\lambda}=\overline{B_{\lambda}}
,
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{L}
.
Note that
\text{Var}(B_{\lambda}(t))=t
,
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{L}
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
Given
(x,t)\in\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
we see that

\Phi_{N}(x,t)=\mathbf{P}_{N}\Phi(x,t)

is a mean-zero real-valued Gaussian random variable with variance

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\Phi_{N}^{2}(x,t)\big]=\sigma_{N,L}\sim\log N\longrightarrow\infty

as
N\to\infty
, where
\sigma_{N,L}
is as in (
1.16
).
As in (
1.17
), we define the Wick power
:\!\Phi_{N}^{\ell}(x,t)\!:
by setting

\displaystyle:\!\Phi_{N}^{\ell}(x,t)\!:\,=H_{\ell}(\Phi_{N}(x,t);\sigma_{N,L}).

(1.25)

Then,

:\!\Phi_{N}^{\ell}\!:
converges, almost surely and
in
L^{p}(\Omega)
for any finite
p\geq 1
,
to a limit, denoted by

:\!\Phi^{\ell}\!:
, in
C(\mathbb{R}_{+};H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}))
,
s<0
.
8
8

8

Here,
we endow the space

C(\mathbb{R}_{+};H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}))
with the compact-open topology in time,
namely with the topology of uniform convergence on compact intervals.

Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
consider the following truncated SdNLW on
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}
:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{2}u_{N}+\partial_{t}u_{N}+(1-\Delta)u_{N}+\mathbf{P}_{N}\big((\mathbf{P}_{N}u_{N})^{k}\big)=\sqrt{2}\xi_{L}.

(1.26)

Proceeding with the first order expansion (
[
50
,
11
,
22
]
):

u_{N}=\Phi_{N}+v_{N},

(1.27)

we see that
the remainder term
v_{N}=u_{N}-\Phi_{N}
satisfies

\partial_{t}^{2}v_{N}+\partial_{t}v_{N}+(1-\Delta)v_{N}+\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}{k\choose\ell}\mathbf{P}_{N}\big(\Phi_{N}^{\ell}v_{N}^{k-\ell}\big)=0

(1.28)

with the zero initial data.
As pointed out above,
the power
\Phi_{N}^{\ell}
does not converge to any limit as
N\to\infty
.
Furthermore,
a triviality is known for (
1.28
) (at least for
k=3
).
Namely,
the solution
u_{N}
to (
1.26
)
tends to 0 as we remove the regularization (
N\to\infty
); see
[
62
]
.
This triviality result necessitates the use of a renormalization.
Therefore,
we instead consider the following renormalized version of (
1.28
):

\partial_{t}^{2}v_{N}+\partial_{t}v_{N}+(1-\Delta)v_{N}+\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}{k\choose\ell}\mathbf{P}_{N}\big(:\!\Phi_{N}^{\ell}\!:v_{N}^{k-\ell}\big)=0

(1.29)

with the zero initial data.
By formally taking a limit as
N\to\infty
,
we then obtain the limiting equation:

\partial_{t}^{2}v+\partial_{t}v+(1-\Delta)v+\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}{k\choose\ell}:\!\Phi^{\ell}\!:v^{k-\ell}=0.

(1.30)

Given the almost sure space-time regularity of the Wick powers

\{\,:\!\Phi^{\ell}\!:\,\}_{\ell=1}^{k}
, standard deterministic analysis with the product estimates
(Lemma
2.8
)
and Sobolev’s inequality yields
local well-posedness of (
1.30
)
with the continuous map,
sending the enhanced data set to the solution:

\displaystyle(\Phi,:\!\Phi^{2}\!:,\dots,:\!\Phi^{k}\!:\,)

\displaystyle\in\big(C([0,T];H^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}))\big)^{\otimes k}

\displaystyle\longmapsto(v,\partial_{t}v)\in C([0,T];\vec{H}^{1-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}))

for some small
\varepsilon>0
.
This deterministic local well-posedness analysis also applies
to (
1.29
), uniformly in
N\in\mathbb{N}
.

In view of the decomposition (
1.27
),
the renormalized truncated SdNLW (
1.29
) for
v_{N}

corresponds to the following
the renormalized truncated SdNLW for
u_{N}=\Phi_{N}+v_{N}
:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{2}u_{N}+\partial_{t}u_{N}+(1-\Delta)u_{N}+\mathbf{P}_{N}\big(:\!(\mathbf{P}_{N}u_{N})^{k}\!:\big)=\sqrt{2}\xi_{L}.

(1.31)

Here, the renormalized nonlinearity in (
1.31
)
is interpreted as

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{N}\big(:\!(\mathbf{P}_{N}u_{N})^{k}\!:\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}_{N}\big(:\!(\Phi_{N}+\mathbf{P}_{N}v_{N})^{k}\!:\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}{k\choose\ell}\mathbf{P}_{N}\big(:\!\Phi_{N}^{\ell}\!:v_{N}^{k-\ell}\big).

Then,
the aforementioned local well-posedness of (
1.30
),
together with the convergence of
the truncated enhanced data set

\{\,:\!\Phi_{N}^{\ell}\!:\,\}_{\ell=1}^{k}

to the limiting enhanced data set

\{\,:\!\Phi^{\ell}\!:\,\}_{\ell=1}^{k}
, implies
that
u_{N}

converges almost surely to a stochastic
process
u=\Phi+v
,
where
v
satisfies (
1.30
).
It is in this sense that we say that
the renormalized SdNLW on the dilated torus
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}
:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{2}u+\partial_{t}u+(1-\Delta)u+:\!u^{k}\!:\,=\sqrt{2}\xi_{L}

(1.32)

is locally well-posed with the Gaussian initial data distributed by
\vec{\mu}_{L}
,
and hence with the Gibbsian initial data in view of the equivalence of
the Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}_{L}
and the base Gaussian measure
\vec{\mu}_{L}
.

Once the local-in-time dynamics is constructed,
Bourgain’s invariant measure argument
[
10
,
11
]

allows us to construct global-in-time dynamics
for the hyperbolic
\Phi^{k+1}_{2}
-model (
1.32
) on
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}

and to prove invariance of the Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}_{L}
in (
1.20
).
This argument is based essentially on the following three ingredients:

•

invariance of the truncated Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}_{N,L}
in (
1.19
)
under the truncated SdNLW dynamics (
1.31
),
which provides a probabilistic growth bound on the solution
u_{N}
, uniformly in
N\in\mathbb{N}
,

•

a PDE approximation argument
(analogous to the local well-posedness argument in the current setting),

•

convergence (in total variation) of the truncated Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}_{N,L}
in (
1.19
)
to the limiting Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}_{L}
in (
1.20
).

See
[
71
]
for full details of this argument.

1.3.
Main result

Our goal in this paper is to extend the construction of the invariant Gibbs dynamics
of the hyperbolic
\Phi^{k+1}_{2}
-model
on
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}
described in the previous subsection to the plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
The main idea is to apply
Bourgain’s invariant measure argument ‘in spirit’,
where we replace
the frequency truncation parameter
N\to\infty

by the growing period
L\to\infty
.

We first state our main result.
Given
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
1\leq p<\infty
,
and
\mu>0
,
let

W^{s,p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
be the weighted Sobolev space
defined in (
2.22
) below.
We set

\displaystyle\vec{W}^{s,p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})=W^{s,p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times W^{s-1,p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

We also set

\displaystyle\vec{H}_{\text{loc}}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2})=H_{\text{loc}}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H_{\text{loc}}^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

Here,
H_{\text{loc}}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
denotes the class of functions
u
belonging
to
H^{s}(K)
for each compact subset
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
where
the

H^{s}(K)
-norm is defined as the restriction norm onto the set
K
;
see (
2.3
) below.
In the following, we endow

C(\mathbb{R}_{+};\vec{H}_{\textup{loc}}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))

with the compact-open topology.
9
9

9

We say that
u\in C(\mathbb{R}_{+};\vec{H}_{\textup{loc}}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))

if
u\in C(\mathbb{R}_{+};\vec{H}_{\textup{loc}}^{s}(K)

for each compact subset
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Throughout the paper, we assume that
random initial data are independent of a stochastic forcing
(such as
\xi_{L}
and
\xi
).
Given
R>0
, let

B_{R}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:|x|\leq R\}
denotes the (closed) ball of radius
R
centered at the origin
and

\mathbf{C}_{R}
denote the cone given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{C}_{R}&=\{(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}:|x|+|t|\leq R\}\\
&=\{(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,R]:x\in B_{R-t}\}.\end{split}

(1.33)

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
k\in 2\mathbb{N}+1
.
There exists a subset

\mathcal{A}=\{L_{j}:j\in\mathbb{N}\}\subset\mathbb{N}

(
with
L_{j}<L_{j^{\prime}}
for
j<j^{\prime}
)

such that the following statements hold.

(i)

Let
s<0
, finite
p\geq 1
, and
\mu>0
.
Let
\vec{\rho}_{L}
be the Gibbs measure
on the dilated torus
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}
defined in (
1.20
).
When viewed as a probability measure on
the weighted Sobolev space
\vec{W}^{s,p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
the
L_{j}
-periodic Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}_{L_{j}}

converges
weakly to a limiting Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}_{\infty}

as
j\to\infty
.
The limiting Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}_{\infty}
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}

can be written as

\vec{\rho}_{\infty}=\rho_{\infty}\otimes\mu_{0,\infty},

where
\rho_{\infty}
is the
\Phi^{k+1}_{2}
-measure
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
constructed as a limit
of the
L_{j}
-periodic
\Phi^{k+1}_{2}
-measure
\rho_{L_{j}}
,
and
\mu_{0,\infty}
is the white noise measure on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

(ii)

The hyperbolic
\Phi^{k+1}_{2}
-model on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{2}u+\partial_{t}u+(1-\Delta)u\,+:\!u^{k}\!:\,=\sqrt{2}\xi

(1.34)

is globally well-posed
almost surely with respect to the Gibbsian initial data
distributed by the Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}_{\infty}
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
constructed in Part
(i)
.
Furthermore,
the Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}_{\infty}
is invariant under the resulting
hyperbolic
\Phi^{k+1}_{2}
-dynamics on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

More precisely,
the following statements hold.
There exist
a sequence

\{(u_{L_{j}},\partial_{t}u_{L_{j}})\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}

and
a
non-trivial stochastic process
(u,\partial_{t}u)

almost surely belonging to

C(\mathbb{R}_{+};\vec{H}_{\textup{loc}}^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))

for any
\varepsilon>0

such that

•

for each
j\in\mathbb{N}
,

(u_{L_{j}},\partial_{t}u_{L_{j}})

is the global-in-time solution to SdNLW (
1.32
) on
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L_{j}}

with

\Law\big((u_{L_{j}}(0),\partial_{t}u_{L_{j}}(0))\big)=\vec{\rho}_{L_{j}}
, constructed in
[
40
]
,

•

as
j\to\infty
,

(u_{L_{j}}(0),\partial_{t}u_{L_{j}}(0))

converges almost surely to
(u(0),\partial_{t}u(0))
,
distributed by the
limiting Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}_{\infty}
,
in

\vec{H}_{\textup{loc}}^{-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
,

•

given any
R>0
,

(u_{L_{j}},\partial_{t}u_{L_{j}})

converges in probability to
(u,\partial_{t}u)

on the cone
\mathbf{C}_{R}

(
more precisely, in
L^{\infty}([0,R];\vec{H}^{-\varepsilon}(B_{R-t})\textup{;}~see\eqref{space1})
)
,
as
j\to\infty
.

Furthermore, the law of
(u(t),\partial_{t}u(t))
for any
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}

is given by the renormalized Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}_{\infty}
.

As for the construction
of the limiting
\Phi^{k+1}_{2}
-measure
\rho_{\infty}
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
see also
[
84
,
3
]

and the references therein.
10
10

10

See also
[
27
]
which appeared after the
the first version of this paper.

Note that the assumption on the almost sure convergence of

(u_{L_{j}}(0),\partial_{t}u_{L_{j}}(0))

in Theorem
1.2
(ii)
indeed follows from the weak convergence
of
\vec{\rho}_{L_{j}}

to
\vec{\rho}_{\infty}

in Theorem
1.2
(i) and
the Skorokhod representation theorem
(Lemma
2.19
)
and therefore is not an additional assumption.

Stochastic nonlinear wave equations (SNLW)
have attracted extensive attention from both applied and theoretical
points of view;
see
[
24
, Chapter 13]
and
[
61
]
for the references therein.
In particular, over the last five years, we have seen a significant progress
in the well-posedness theory of
SNLW in the singular setting:
11
11

11

Some of the works mentioned below
are on SNLW without damping.

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{2}u+\partial_{t}u+(1-\Delta)u+\mathcal{N}(u)=\zeta,

where the noise
\zeta

is primarily taken to be a space-time white noise
\xi
.
Here,
\mathcal{N}(u)
denotes a nonlinearity
which may be
of a power-type

[
38
,
39
,
40
,
71
,
62
,
89
,
63
,
15
,
64
,
77
,
17
]

and trigonometric and exponential nonlinearities

[
69
,
72
,
70
,
79
]
.
We also mention the works

[
76
,
67
,
66
,
85
,
78
,
65
]

on the (deterministic)
nonlinear wave equations (
1.4
) with rough random initial data
and

[
25
,
26
,
61
]

on SNLW with
more singular (both in space and time) noises.
We point out that
the only known well-posedness result up to date
for singular SNLW posed on an unbounded domain
is the work
[
89
]
by the second author,
where he established

pathwise
global well-posedness
of the cubic SNLW on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with an additive space-time white noise forcing:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{2}u+(1-\Delta)u\,+u^{k}=\xi,

(1.35)

where
k=3
.
Note that a slight modification of the argument yields
pathwise global well-posedness
for SdNLW (
1.1
) on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
when
k=3
.

Theorem
1.2
provides
the second well-posedness result for singular SNLW on an unbounded domain.
Our construction of the global-in-time dynamics, however,
is quite different from that in
[
89
]
.
In particular, it is
not pathwise but is based on
a probabilistic argument, more precisely,
on Bourgain’s invariant measure argument ‘in spirit’.
We point out that
pathwise global well-posedness
of SdNLW (
1.1
) or SNLW (
1.35
)
for the (super-)quintic case
k\geq 5
remains
a challenging open question
even on the torus
\mathbb{T}^{2}
,
and therefore,
the situation for the stochastic wave equation is completely different
from SNLH (
1.9
) on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
where Mourrat and Weber
[
55
]

proved pathwise global well-posedness of (
1.9
) on
\mathbb{R}^{2}

for any
k\in 2\mathbb{N}+1
.

Thanks to the finite speed of propagation,
the argument in
[
38
,
40
]

yields local well-posedness of (
1.34
)
on the ball
B_{R}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
for each
R>0
; see Proposition
4.2
below.
As
R\to\infty
,
however, the local existence time shrinks to
0
.
In order to construct a solution to (
1.34
)
on some time interval
[0,\tau]
, uniformly on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
we need to make use of statistical ingredients,
and thus, establishing local well-posedness of (
1.34
),
uniformly
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, is essentially as difficult as
establishing its global well-posedness,
as already observed in
[
89
]
.

On the dilated torus
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}
,
the Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}_{L}

and the base Gaussian measure
\vec{\mu}_{L}
are equivalent for each finite
L>0
.
However, this equivalence of
\vec{\rho}_{L}
and
\vec{\mu}_{L}
is not uniform when
L\to\infty
,
since
the potential energy

R^{L}
, defined as the limit of
R^{L}_{N}
in (
1.18
),
grows like
\sim L
as
L\to\infty
.
Indeed from
(
1.18
), (
1.17
), and Lemma
2.13
with (
1.10
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\vec{\mu}_{L}}

\displaystyle\Big[\big(R^{L}(u)\big)^{2}\Big]=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\vec{\mu}_{L}}\Big[\big(R_{N}^{L}(u)\big)^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle=C_{k}\lim_{N\to\infty}\iint_{\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{L}}\Big[H_{k+1}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u(x);\sigma_{N,L})H_{k+1}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u(y);\sigma_{N,L})\Big]dxdy

\displaystyle=C_{k}\lim_{N\to\infty}\iint_{\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}}\Big(\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{L}}\big[\mathbf{P}_{N}u(x)\mathbf{P}_{N}u(y)\big]\Big)^{k+1}dxdy

\displaystyle=C_{k}\lim_{N\to\infty}\iint_{\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}}\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{L}\\
|\lambda|\leq N\end{subarray}}\frac{e_{\lambda}^{L}(x-y)}{\langle\lambda\rangle^{2}}\frac{1}{L}\bigg)^{k+1}dxdy

\displaystyle=C_{k}L^{2}\lim_{N\to\infty}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},\dots,n_{k+1}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\\
n_{1}+\cdots+n_{k+1}=0\\
|n_{j}|\leq LN\end{subarray}}\bigg(\prod_{j=1}^{k+1}\frac{1}{\langle\frac{n_{j}}{L}\rangle^{2}}\bigg)\frac{1}{L^{2k}}

\displaystyle\sim L^{2},

where the last step follows from a Riemann sum approximation.
See also Remark
1.4
(i).
This non-uniformity causes difficulty in studying the large torus limit
L\to\infty
.
In the one-dimensional case,
there is no need for a renormalization in constructing a Gibbs measure,
and
Bourgain
[
13
]
used the
Brascamp-Lieb concentration inequality
[
14
, Theorem 5.1]

to reduce the relevant analysis to that for the Gaussian case,
uniformly in the period
L\gg 1
.
In the current two-dimensional case,
due to the use of the renormalization,
the log-concavity needed for
the
Brascamp-Lieb concentration inequality
is not available, and thus
we need an alternative approach.

In this work,
the main statistical control
comes from the study on the so-called

enhanced Gibbs measures
,
namely the distributions
of the enhanced data sets.
This idea played a crucial role
in a recent work
[
64
]

on the hyperbolic
\Phi^{3}_{3}
-model on
the three-dimensional torus
\mathbb{T}^{3}
by the first two authors
and Okamoto.
Given
R>0
,
our goal is to construct a solution to (
1.34
)
on the cone
\mathbf{C}_{R}
in (
1.33
)
as a limit of the solution
u_{L}
to the
L
-periodic problem (
1.32
);
see (
4.2
) below.
When
L\gg R
,
we see that the
L
-periodic spatial white noise

\zeta_{L}
defined in (
3.53
)
and
the
L
-periodic space-time white noise
\xi_{L}

defined in (
4.4
)
agree on the cone
\mathbf{C}_{R}
with
the spatial white noise

\zeta
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
(see Definition
3.4
)
and the space-time white noise on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
, respectively;
see (
4.6
) below.
Therefore, by restricting our attention to the cone
\mathbf{C}_{R}
,
we conclude from
the finite speed of propagation
and the observation above
that the difference
of the
L
-periodic
problem (
4.2
)
for different values of
L\geq 1

appears
only in the first component

u_{0,L}
of the initial data
with

\Law(u_{0,L})=\rho_{L}
,
where
\rho_{L}
is the
L
-periodic
\Phi^{k+1}_{2}
-measure in (
1.21
).
This essentially reduces
the convergence problem on the cone
\mathbf{C}_{R}

of the
L
-periodic
problem (
4.2
) to
studying convergence properties
of the enhanced Gibbs measure

\displaystyle\nu_{L}=\Law(\Xi_{0}(u_{0,L})),

(1.36)

where
\Xi_{0}(u_{0,L})
denotes
the enhanced data set,
12
12

12

Namely, the Wick powers of the associated linear solution.
emanating from the first component

u_{L}|_{t=0}=u_{0,L}
of the initial data
with
\Law(u_{0,L})=\rho_{L}
;
see (
4.36
) and (
4.12
).
As mentioned above,
by restricting our attention to the cone
\mathbf{C}_{R}
,
when
L\gg R
,
the second enhanced data set
\Xi_{1}(u_{1},\xi)

in (
4.13
) (see also (
4.10
)),
involving the second component
u_{1}

of the initial data and the space-time white noise forcing
\xi
,
does
not
depend on
L
, which simplifies some analysis.
As for the
second enhanced data set
\Xi_{1}(u_{1},\xi)
,
its
mapping properties

(viewing its elements as (random) multiplication operators) play an important role;
see Definition
4.1
and Proposition
4.9
.

Let us briefly describe four main steps of the proof of Theorem
1.2
.

\bullet

Step 1:

Coming down from infinity
for the associated SNLH on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

In this first step, we establish
coming down from infinity
(namely, an estimate on a solution
independent of initial data)
for
SNLH
(
3.5
)
in (i) weighted Lebesgue spaces
L^{p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
(Proposition
3.1
)
and (ii) weighted Sobolev spaces
W^{s,p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

of positive regularities (Proposition
3.3
).
The coming down from infinity in weighted Lebesgue
spaces
yields tightness
of the
L
-periodic Gibbs measures
\vec{\rho}_{L}
,
which then allows us to extract a sequence
\{\vec{\rho}_{L}\}_{L\in\mathcal{A}}

converging weakly to a limiting Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}_{\infty}
;
see Subsection
3.3
.
On the other hand, the coming down from infinity
in weighted Sobolev spaces of positive regularities
plays a crucial role in Step 3.

In recent years, coming down from infinity has been studied
in the context of (singular) SNLH; see, for example,
[
92
,
53
,
54
]
.
See the introduction in
[
53
]

for a further discussion.
In the case of weighted Lebesgue spaces (Proposition
3.1
),
our argument
follows closely that in
[
92
]

and aims to establish a certain differential inequality (see Lemma
3.2
).
Due to the use of the weight, however, the argument is more complicated
and requires a careful decomposition of the physical space into unit cubes,
combined with the Littlewood-Paley decomposition; see (
3.16
)-(
3.26
).
In the case of weighted Sobolev spaces of positive regularities
(Proposition
3.3
),
our argument is based on a Gronwall-type argument
with the coming down from infinity for weighted Lebesgue spaces.
We present details in Section
3
.

\bullet

Step 2:
Local well-posedness and stability of SdNLW (
1.34
)
on the cone
\mathbf{C}_{R}
.

This second step
is entirely deterministic, by viewing initial data
(u_{0},u_{1})

and a forcing
\xi
as given deterministic spatial / space-time distributions, and follows from a slight modification
of the local well-posedness argument in
[
40
]
.
Here, stability refers
to that with respect to the enhanced data set

\Xi_{0}(u_{0})
in
(
4.12
),
emanating from
the first component
u_{0}
of the initial data.
See Subsection
4.1
for details.

\bullet

Step 3:

Convergence of the enhanced Gibbs measures.

In this step and Step 4, we restrict our attention to
L\in\mathcal{A}\subset\mathbb{N}

constructed in Step 1. This is due to the non-uniqueness of the limiting Gibbs measure
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
; see Remark
1.4
(ii).

Our main goal in this step is to prove convergence of
\{\nu_{L}\}_{L\in\mathcal{A}}

to a natural limit

\displaystyle\nu_{\infty}=\Law(\Xi_{0}(u_{0}))

(1.37)

with
\Law(u_{0})=\rho_{\infty}
constructed in Step 1.
Here, the mode of convergence
is weak convergence as well as convergence in
the Wasserstein-1 metric.
The proof is broken into two parts,
where we first establish tightness
of
\{\nu_{L}\}_{L\in\mathcal{A}}
(Proposition
4.4
) and then show that the limit is
indeed unique, given by
\nu_{\infty}
in (
1.37
)
(Proposition
4.6
).

The (first) enhanced data set

\Xi_{0}(u_{0,L})

consists of the Wick powers

:\!(\mathcal{S}(t)u_{0,L})^{\ell}\!:
,
\ell=1,\dots,k
,
of the linear solution

\mathcal{S}(t)u_{0,L}=(\partial_{t}\mathcal{D}(t)+\mathcal{D}(t))u_{0,L}

with
\Law(u_{0,L})=\rho_{L}
,
where
\rho_{L}
is the
L
-periodic
\Phi^{k+1}
-measure in (
1.21
).
Hence,
in view of the invariance of
\rho_{L}
under the parabolic
\Phi^{k+1}_{2}
-model,
instead of studying
\Xi_{0}(u_{0,L})
,
it suffices to study
\Xi_{0}(X_{L}(1))
,
where
X_{L}
is the solution to the
L
-periodic
SNLH (
3.42
) (namely, the parabolic
\Phi^{k+1}_{2}
-model)
with the Gibbsian initial data
\Law(X_{0,L})=\rho_{L}
.
As a result, the argument involves an intricate combination of wave and heat analysis
(see, in particular, the proof of Proposition
4.6
)
as well as the coming down from infinity in weighted Sobolev spaces
of positive regularities.
See Subsection
4.2
for details.

\bullet

Step 4:
Global well-posedness and invariance of the limiting Gibbs measure.

We first prove well-posedness of the hyperbolic
\Phi^{k+1}_{2}
-model
on the cone
\mathbf{C}_{R}
for each
R>0
.
In view of the global well-posedness of the
L
-periodic hyperbolic
\Phi^{k+1}_{2}
-model
(
4.2
),
the local well-posedness and stability results established in Step 2
allow us to reduce the problem
to estimating the size of the first enhanced data set
\Xi_{0}(u_{0,L})
,
studying the mapping properties of the second enhanced data set

\Xi_{1}(u_{2},\xi)
,
and convergence in the Wasserstein-1 metric
of the enhanced Gibbs measure
\nu_{L}
to
\nu_{\infty}

established in Step 3.
Invariance of the limiting Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}_{\infty}

then follows from the weak convergence of
\{\vec{\rho}_{L}\}_{L\in\mathcal{A}}
to
\vec{\rho}_{\infty}

(Theorem
1.2
(i)),
the convergence in law, as
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}_{2}\times\mathbb{R}_{+})
-valued random variables, of the solution
u_{L}
,
L\in\mathcal{A}
,
to the
L
-periodic hyperbolic
\Phi^{k+1}_{2}
-model (
4.2
)
to the solution
u
to the hyperbolic
\Phi^{k+1}_{2}
-model (
4.1
) on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
which follows as a corollary of the global well-posedness
(Remark
4.12
).

In view of the finite speed of propagation,
when we work on the cone
\mathbf{C}_{R}
,
the idea of breaking the enhanced data set
into two groups
\Xi_{0}(u_{0,L})
in (
4.12
)
and
\Xi_{1}(u_{1},\xi)
in (
4.13
),
where the latter is independent of
L\gg R
,
seems new but is natural.
Let us make a brief comparison to the work

[
64
]
on the hyperbolic

\Phi^{3}_{3}
-model by the first two authors and Okamoto.
What is common is the use of the enhanced Gibbs measures
(= the laws of the enhanced data sets
of the
L
-periodic (or frequency-truncated) Gibbs measures),
while, in the current paper,
we only consider the enhanced Gibbs measure
emanating from the first component of the initial data
for the reason explained above.
In this work,
we reduce various problems to those
for the associated stochastic heat equation,
which leads to an interesting mixture of wave and heat analysis.
Lastly, we remark that, in a recent remarkable preprint
[
17
]

resolving a challenging open problem on well-posedness of the hyperbolic
\Phi^{4}_{3}
-model,
a mixture of wave and heat analysis also played an important role
(but in a different context from our analysis).

Remark 1.3
.

(i) A slight modification of the proof of Theorem
1.2

applies to the deterministic NLW (
1.4
) on
\mathbb{R}^{2}

with the Gibbsian initial data, thus yielding
global well-posedness of (the renormalized version of) (
1.4
)
with
\Law(u(0),\partial_{t}u(0))=\vec{\rho}_{\infty}

and invariance of
\vec{\rho}_{\infty}
under the resulting dynamics.
13
13

13

In
a recent preprint
[
4
]
, Barashkov and Laarne independently obtained
global well-posedness and invariance of the Gibbs measure
the deterministic NLW (
1.4
) on
\mathbb{R}^{2}

(with
k=3
).

See
[
51
]
for the one-dimensional case.
We also mention
[
13
,
94
,
19
,
20
,
46
,
16
,
68
]

for works on the
construction of invariant Gibbs dynamics
for Hamiltonian PDEs
on unbounded domains.

(ii) In this paper, we only consider the defocusing case
k\in 2\mathbb{N}+1
.
If (a)
k\in 2\mathbb{N}+2
or (b)
k\in 2\mathbb{N}+1

but with the
-
sign on the potential energy
\frac{1}{k+1}\int u^{k+1}dx
in (
1.2
),
namely, the focusing case,
then it is known that the associated (renormalized) Gibbs measure
on the two-dimensional torus
\mathbb{T}^{2}
is not normalizable
even with a taming by a power of the Wick-ordered
L^{2}
-norm; see

[
73
]
. See also
[
18
]
.
We refer interested readers to
[
48
,
74
,
63
,
64
]

on the (non-)construction of focusing Gibbs measures on the general
d
-dimensional torus.

When
k=2
,
it is possible to construct the Gibbs measure on
\mathbb{T}^{2}
with a taming by a power
of the Wick-ordered
L^{2}
-norm
(see
[
12
,
73
]
)
and the associated invariant Gibbs dynamics
for the hyperbolic
\Phi^{3}_{2}
-model on
\mathbb{T}^{2}
;
see Remark 1.8 in
[
76
]
.
See also
[
64
]
for the three-dimensional case.
Due to the non-defocusing nature of the problem,
however, we expect a certain triviality phenomenon to take place in taking
a large torus limit of the
L
-periodic
\Phi^{3}_{2}
-measure,
just as in the one-dimensional focusing case

[
81
,
91
]
.
14
14

14

See a recent preprint

[
83
]
on the triviality of the
\Phi^{3}_{2}
-measure on the plane.

(iii) In
[
88
,
90
]
, the second author
proved ergodicity of the Gibbs measure
for the hyperbolic
\Phi^{k+1}_{d}
-model posed on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
when
d=1,2
.
See also
[
29
]
.
It is of interest to study the corresponding problem on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Remark 1.4
.

(i)
As mentioned above, the equivalence
of the
L
-periodic
\Phi^{k+1}_{2}
-measure
\rho_{L}

and the base Gaussian free field
\mu_{L}
on
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}

is not uniform.
In fact, it is expected that the limiting
\Phi^{k+1}_{2}
-measure

\rho_{\infty}
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
constructed in Theorem
1.2
(i)
and the base Gaussian measure (= the large torus limit of
\mu_{L}
)
are mutually singular.
We will address this issue
in a forthcoming work.

(ii)
The limiting Gibbs measure
\rho_{\infty}

constructed in Theorem
1.2
(i)
in principle depends on the sequence
L\in\mathcal{A}

and there is no uniqueness statement
for the limiting Gibbs measure.
See
[
30
,
31
,
32
]
.
See also the discussion at the end of Section 2
in
[
3
]
.

Remark 1.5
.

Let
\rho_{\infty}
be the limiting
\Phi^{k+1}
-measure
in Theorem
1.2
(i)
constructed as a limit of
\{\rho_{L}\}_{L\in\mathcal{A}}
.
Consider
the parabolic
\Phi^{k+1}_{2}
-model (
3.1
) on
\mathbb{R}^{2}

with the Gibbsian initial data
\Law(X_{0})=\rho_{\infty}
.
Then, it follows from
the proof of Proposition
4.6

(see Remark
4.7
)
that
\rho_{\infty}

is invariant under
the parabolic
\Phi^{k+1}_{2}
-dynamics on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
While this fact is not difficult to prove,
to the authors’ knowledge, it is not explicitly written
and thus we decided to mention it here.

2.
Preliminary

2.1.
Notations

By
A\lesssim B
, we mean
A\leq CB

for some constant
C>0
.
We use
A\sim B

to mean

A\lesssim B
and
B\lesssim A
.
We write
A\ll B
, if there is some small
c>0

such that
A\leq cB
.
We may use subscripts to denote dependence on external parameters; for example,

A\lesssim_{\delta}B
means
A\leq C(\delta)B
.
We use
a-

(and
a+
) to denote
a-\varepsilon
(and
a+\varepsilon
, respectively)
for arbitrarily small
\varepsilon>0
.
If this notation appears in an estimate,
then an implicit constant
is allowed to depend on
\varepsilon>0
(and it usually diverges as
\varepsilon\to 0
).
We also use the notation
p=\infty-

to denote a sufficiently large number
p\gg 1
, depending on the context.
For conciseness of notation,
we set

a\vee b=\max(a,b)
.

Throughout this paper, we fix a rich enough probability
space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
, on which all the random objects
are defined.
The realization
\omega\in\Omega
is often omitted in the writing.
Given a random variable
X
, we denote by
\Law(X)

the law of
X
.

In the remaining part of the paper, we only work with
real-valued functions / distributions.
Given a time-differentiable function,
we set

\vec{u}=(u,\partial_{t}u)
.

We
define the Fourier transform of a function
f
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}

by setting

\widehat{f}(\eta)=\mathcal{F}(f)(\eta)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x)e^{-2\pi i\eta\cdot x}dx

with the inverse Fourier transform given by

\mathcal{F}^{-1}(f)(\eta)=\widehat{f}(-\eta)
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
1\leq p\leq\infty
.
We define the
L^{2}
-based Sobolev space
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

by the norm:

\displaystyle\|f\|_{H^{s}}=\|\langle\nabla\rangle^{s}f\|_{L^{2}}=\|\langle\eta\rangle^{s}\widehat{f}(\eta)\|_{L^{2}},

where
\langle\,\cdot\,\rangle=(1+|\cdot|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}
.
We also define the
L^{p}
-based Sobolev space
W^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

by the norm:

\displaystyle\|f\|_{W^{s,p}}=\|\langle\nabla\rangle^{s}f\|_{L^{p}}=\big\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}[\langle\eta\rangle^{s}\widehat{f}(\eta)]\big\|_{L^{p}}.

(2.1)

When
p=2
, we have
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=W^{s,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
We recall the following interpolation result
which follows from the Littlewood-Paley characterization of Sobolev norms via the square function
and Hölder’s inequality;
let
s,s_{1},s_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
and
p,p_{1},p_{2}\in(1,\infty)

such that
s=\theta s_{1}+(1-\theta)s_{2}
and
\frac{1}{p}=\frac{\theta}{p_{1}}+\frac{1-\theta}{p_{2}}

for some
0<\theta<1
.
Then, we have

\|u\|_{W^{s,p}}\lesssim\|u\|_{W^{s_{1},p_{1}}}^{\theta}\|u\|_{W^{s_{2},p_{2}}}^{1-\theta}.

(2.2)

Let
X(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be a function space on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such as
the Sobolev space
W^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

defined above or the weighted Sobolev / Besov spaces defined below.
Given a (nice) subset
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
we define the localized version of a function space
X(\mathbb{R}^{d})

by the restriction norm:

\displaystyle\|f\|_{X(K)}=\inf\big\{\|g\|_{X(\mathbb{R}^{d})}:\,f\equiv g\text{ on }K\big\}.

(2.3)

In dealing with a space of space-time functions,
we often use short-hand notations such as

L^{q}_{T}H^{s}_{x}

for
L^{q}([0,T];H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
.
Given a space
X(\mathbb{R}^{d})
of functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
we set
L^{\infty}([0,R];X(B_{R-t}))

by

\displaystyle L^{\infty}([0,R];X(B_{R-t}))=\big\{u\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathring{\mathbf{C}}_{R}):t\in[0,R]\mapsto\|u(t)\|_{X(B_{R-t})}\text{ is in $L^{\infty}$}\big\},

(2.4)

where

\mathring{\mathbf{C}}_{R}
denotes the interior of the cone

\mathbf{C}_{R}
and

X(B_{R-t})
is defined by the restriction norm as in (
2.3
).
Here,
B_{R}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}

denotes the (closed) ball of radius
R
centered at the origin
and
\mathbf{C}_{R}
denotes the cone as in (
1.33
)
(but in
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
).

Let
\mathcal{D}(t)
denote the linear damped wave propagator defined in
(
1.23
).
We then set

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(t)=\partial_{t}\mathcal{D}(t)+\mathcal{D}(t).

(2.5)

Namely,
u=\mathcal{S}(t)u_{0}
satisfies

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}^{2}u+\partial_{t}u+(1-\Delta)=0\\
(u,\partial_{t}u)|_{t=0}=(u_{0},0).\end{cases}

Let
p_{t}
denote the standard heat kernel on
\mathbb{R}^{2}

given by
p_{t}(x)=\frac{1}{4\pi t}e^{-\frac{|x|^{2}}{4t}}
.
Then,
let
P(t)
denote the linear heat propagator given by

\displaystyle P(t)f=e^{t(\Delta-1)}f=e^{-t}(p_{t}*f).

(2.6)

We now introduce the Littlewood-Paley projector,
which is adapted to weighted Sobolev and Besov spaces
defined in the next subsection.
Recall the following definition
[
55
, Definition 1]
.
15
15

15

Definition 2.1 in the arXiv version.

Given
\theta_{0}\geq 1
,
the Gevrey class

\mathcal{G}^{\theta_{0}}\subset C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})

of order
\theta_{0}

consists of functions
f
satisfying
the following;
given any compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
there exists
C_{K}>0
such that

\sup_{x\in K}|\partial^{\alpha}f(x)|\leq(C_{K})^{|\alpha|+1}(\alpha!)^{\theta_{0}}

for any multi-index
\alpha
.
We use
\mathcal{G}^{\theta_{0}}_{c}
to denote
the set of compactly supported functions in
\mathcal{G}^{\theta_{0}}
.

Let
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}:=\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
.
Let us now introduce the
Littlewood-Paley projector

\mathbf{Q}_{k}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, defined by Gevrey class multipliers.
As seen in
[
55
]
, such a choice is suitable
for weighted spaces with a stretched exponential weight;
see (
2.10
).
As in Section 3 of
[
55
]
, let

\displaystyle\chi_{0},\chi_{1}\in\mathcal{G}^{\theta_{0}}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d};[0,1])

(2.7)

with

\displaystyle\supp\chi_{0}\subset\big\{|\xi|\leq\tfrac{4}{3}\big\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\supp\chi_{1}\subset\big\{\tfrac{3}{4}\leq|\xi|\leq\tfrac{8}{3}\big\}

such that

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\chi_{k}(\xi)\equiv 1

on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
\chi_{k}(\xi)=\chi_{1}(2^{1-k}\xi)
for
k\geq 2
.
We then define the Littlewood-Paley projector
\mathbf{Q}_{k}
by

\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}_{k}(f)=\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\chi_{k}\widehat{f})=\eta_{k}*f,

(2.8)

where
\eta_{k}
is defined by

\displaystyle\eta_{k}=\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\chi_{k})

(2.9)

for
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
Namely, we have
\eta_{k}(x)=2^{dk}\eta(2^{k}x)
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
where

\eta(x)=2^{-d}\eta_{1}(2^{-1}x)
.
We also recall
Proposition 1 in
[
55
]
16
16

16

Proposition 2.2 in the arXiv version.

that

\displaystyle|\eta(x)|\lesssim e^{-c|x|^{\frac{1}{\theta_{0}}}}.

(2.10)

2.2.
Weighted Sobolev and Besov spaces

In this subsection, we introduce weighted Sobolev and Besov spaces
and discuss their basic properties.
We first recall the definition of the stretched exponential weight introduced in
[
55
]
.
Let
\theta_{0}
be as in (
2.7
), appearing in the definition
of the Littlewood-Paley
projector
\mathbf{Q}_{k}
in (
2.8
).
Given
0<\delta<\frac{1}{\theta_{0}}
(
\leq 1
)
and
\mu>0
,
we define the weight
w_{\mu}(x)

by setting

\displaystyle w_{\mu}(x)=e^{-\mu\langle x\rangle^{\delta}}.

(2.11)

It is easy to check that
the weight
w_{\mu}
is
w_{-\mu}
-moderate
in the sense:

\displaystyle w_{\mu}(x+y)\leq w_{-\mu}(x)w_{\mu}(y)

(2.12)

for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
;
see
[
55
, (2.6)]
.
In particular,
it follows from (
2.12
) that

\displaystyle w_{\mu}(x)\lesssim w_{\mu}(n)

(2.13)

for any
x\in Q_{n}:=n+\big[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\big)^{2}
.
Let
0<\alpha<1
.
Then, recall from
[
55
, (3.25)]
17
17

17

(3.24) in the arXiv version.

that we have

\displaystyle|\mathbf{Q}_{k}w_{\mu}(x)|\lesssim 2^{-\alpha k}w_{\mu}(x)

(2.14)

for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
In the remaining part of the paper, we fix
0<\delta<\frac{1}{\theta_{0}}
in (
2.11
)
and we often drop the dependence on
\delta
in various estimates.

Let
1\leq p<\infty
and
\mu>0
.
Then, the weighted Lebesgue space

L^{p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is defined by the norm:

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L^{p}_{\mu}}=\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|f(x)|^{p}w_{\mu}(x)dx\bigg)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

(2.15)

While it is possible to introduce a weighted Sobolev space
by the
L^{p}_{\mu}
-norm of
\langle\nabla\rangle^{s}f
(see (
2.31
) below),
we use a slightly different definition by first introducing spatial localization.

Let
\phi:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
be a smooth even function such that
\phi
is non-increasing on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

\phi=1
on
\big[-\frac{5}{4},\frac{5}{4}\big]
, and
\phi=0
on
(-\infty,-\frac{8}{5}\big]\cup\big[\frac{8}{5},\infty)
.
Given
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
we define
\phi_{j}
by

\displaystyle\phi_{0}(x)=\phi(|x|)\qquad{\rm and}\qquad\phi_{j}(x)=\phi\big(\tfrac{|x|}{2^{j}}\big)-\phi\big(\tfrac{|x|}{2^{j-1}}\big),\ \ j\in\mathbb{N}.

(2.16)

Then, it is easy to check that

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\phi_{j}(x)=1\,\,\,\,\text{for any $x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}$},

(2.17)

and

\displaystyle\supp\phi_{0}\subset\big\{|x|\leq\tfrac{8}{5}\}\quad\text{and}\quad\supp\phi_{j}\subset\big\{\tfrac{5}{8}\cdot 2^{j}\leq|x|\leq\tfrac{8}{5}\cdot 2^{j}\},\ \ j\in\mathbb{N}.

(2.18)

By convention, we set
\phi_{-1}=0
.
Given
s\geq 0
and
1\leq p\leq\infty
,
it follows from (
2.16
) that

\displaystyle\|\phi_{j}\|_{W^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\sim 2^{\frac{d}{p}j}.

(2.19)

We also define slightly fattened cutoff functions
\widetilde{\phi}_{j}
,
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, by setting

\displaystyle\widetilde{\phi}_{j}=\phi_{j-1}+\phi_{j}+\phi_{j+1},\ \ j\in\mathbb{N}.

(2.20)

Then, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{\phi}_{j}\phi_{j}=\phi_{j}.

(2.21)

for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.

Given
1\leq p<\infty
and
\mu>0
,
we define the weighted Sobolev space

W^{s,p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
by the norm:

\displaystyle\|f\|_{W^{s,p}_{\mu}}=\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}w_{\mu}(2^{j})\|\phi_{j}f\|_{W^{s,p}}^{p}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{p}},

(2.22)

where
W^{s,p}=W^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
denotes the usual
L^{p}
-based Sobolev space on
\mathbb{R}^{d}

defined in (
2.1
).
When
p=2
, we set

H^{s}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=W^{s,2}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

The following embedding follows from the definition (
2.22
)
with Lemma
2.8
and (
2.19
):

W^{s,p_{1}}_{\mu_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset W^{s,p_{2}}_{\mu_{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\quad\text{ for $1\leq p_{2}\leq p_{1}<\infty$
and $\mu_{2}\geq\mu_{1}>0$.}

From (
2.22
) and (
2.15
) with (
2.11
) and (
2.18
),
there exists
a_{1}>0
such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|f\|_{W^{s,p}_{\mu}}&\leq\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}w_{\frac{1}{p}\mu}(2^{j})\|\phi_{j}f\|_{W^{s,p}}\leq\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{\mu}{p}2^{j\delta}}\|\phi_{j}f\|_{W^{s,p}},\\
\|\phi_{j}f\|_{W^{s,p}}&\leq e^{\frac{\mu}{p}}e^{\frac{\mu}{p}2^{j\delta}}\|f\|_{W^{s,p}_{\mu}},\\
\|\phi_{j}f\|_{L^{p}}&\leq e^{\frac{\mu}{p}a_{1}2^{j\delta}}\|f\|_{L^{p}_{\mu}}\end{split}

(2.23)

for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
Similar bounds hold when we replace
\phi_{j}
by
\widetilde{\phi}_{j}
.

Let
1\leq q\leq p<\infty
.
By applying Sobolev’s inequality (with
\frac{s}{d}\geq\frac{1}{q}-\frac{1}{p}
)
and the embedding
\ell^{q}(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})\subset\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|f\|_{W^{0,p}_{\mu}}&=\big\|w_{\frac{\mu}{p}}(2^{j})\|\phi_{j}f\|_{L^{p}}\big\|_{\ell^{p}_{j}(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})}\\
&\lesssim\big\|w_{\frac{\mu}{p}}(2^{j})\|\phi_{j}f\|_{W^{s,q}}\big\|_{\ell^{q}_{j}(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})}\\
&=\|f\|_{W^{s,q}_{\frac{q}{p}\mu}}.\end{split}

(2.24)

Remark 2.1
.

We point out that when
s=0
,
the
W^{0,p}_{\mu}
-norm defined in (
2.22
)
is not equivalent to the
L^{p}_{\mu}
-norm defined in (
2.15
).
See also Remark
2.4
below.

We also recall the definition of the weighted Besov space

B^{s,\mu}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

from
[
55
, Section 3]
.
Given
s\in\mathbb{R}
,

1\leq p,q\leq\infty
, and
\mu\geq 0
,
we define
the weighted Besov space

B^{s,\mu}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

as the completion of
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
under the norm:

\displaystyle\|f\|_{B^{s,\mu}_{p,q}}=\Big\|2^{sk}\|\mathbf{Q}_{k}f\|_{L^{p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\Big\|_{\ell^{q}_{k}(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})}.

(2.25)

See also
[
36
]
.
When
\mu=0
,
the weighted Besov space

B^{s,\mu}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

reduces to the usual
Besov space

B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
We recall the following embeddings
for (unweighted) Sobolev and Besov spaces:

\displaystyle\|f\|_{B^{s}_{p,\infty}}\lesssim\|f\|_{W^{s,p}}\lesssim\|f\|_{B^{s}_{p,1}}\lesssim\|f\|_{B^{s+\varepsilon}_{p,\infty}}

(2.26)

for any
\varepsilon>0
.

We first establish the following compact embedding for weighted Besov spaces,
which plays a crucial role in proving Theorem
1.2
(i).

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
1\leq p<\infty
.
Then, given any
s>s^{\prime}
and
\mu<\mu^{\prime}
,
the embedding

W^{s,p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow W^{s^{\prime},p}_{\mu^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

is compact.

Proof.

Let
\{f_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a bounded sequence in
W^{s,p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
Then it follows from (
2.22
) and (
2.18
) that for any
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
the sequence
\{\phi_{j}f_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

is a bounded sequence in
W^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

with a bounded support:

\big\{|x|\leq\tfrac{8}{5}\cdot 2^{j}\}
,

j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.

In the following, we implement a diagonal argument.
Let
j=0
.
Then, by Rellich’s lemma (with
s^{\prime}<s
),
there exists a subsequence

\{\phi_{0}f_{n_{k}^{(0)}}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}

which is convergent in

W^{s^{\prime},p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
Then,
for
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
by Rellich’s lemma,
we can choose a subsequence

\{n_{k}^{(j)}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}

of
\{n_{k}^{(j-1)}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}

such that

\{\phi_{j}f_{n_{k}^{(j)}}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is convergent in

W^{s^{\prime},p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
Now, consider the sequence

\{\phi_{j}f_{n_{k}^{(k)}}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
.
Then, it is clear from the construction that, for each
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,

\phi_{j}f_{n_{k}^{(k)}}

converges to some
F_{j}
in

W^{s^{\prime},p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
as
k\to\infty
.

By setting

F=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}F_{j},

we claim that
F\in W^{s^{\prime},p}_{\mu^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

for any
\mu^{\prime}>\mu

and
that

f_{n_{k}^{(k)}}
converges to
F
in
W_{\mu^{\prime}}^{s^{\prime},p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

for
\mu^{\prime}>2^{\delta}\mu
.

First, note that
we have

\displaystyle\|\phi_{j}f\|_{W^{s^{\prime},p}}\lesssim\|f\|_{W^{s^{\prime},p}},

(2.27)

uniformly in
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
When
s^{\prime}=0
, (
2.27
) follows from Hölder’s inequality.
When
s^{\prime}<0
, it follows from Lemma
2.8
(ii)
and (
2.19
)
that

\displaystyle\|\phi_{j}f\|_{W^{s^{\prime},p}}\lesssim\|\phi_{j}\|_{W^{-s^{\prime},\infty}}\|f\|_{W^{s^{\prime},p}}\lesssim\|f\|_{W^{s^{\prime},p}}.

When
s^{\prime}>0
,
the fractional Leibniz rule (see Lemma
2.8
(i) below),
(
2.19
),
and Sobolev’s inequality yield

\displaystyle\|\phi_{j}f\|_{W^{s^{\prime},p}}\lesssim\|\phi_{j}\|_{W^{s^{\prime},\infty}}\|f\|_{W^{s^{\prime},p}}\lesssim\|f\|_{W^{s^{\prime},p}}.

This proves the bound (
2.27
).
Then,
from (
2.23
),
(
2.18
),
(
2.27
),

w_{\mu^{\prime}}(2^{j})=w_{\mu}(2^{j})w_{\mu^{\prime}-\mu}(2^{j})
,
and the uniform bound on the
W^{s,p}_{\mu}
-norm
of
f_{n_{k}^{(k)}}
,
we have

\displaystyle\|F\|_{W_{\mu^{\prime}}^{s^{\prime},p}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\|F_{j}\|_{W_{\mu^{\prime}}^{s^{\prime},p}}\leq\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}w_{\frac{1}{p}\mu^{\prime}}(2^{\ell})\|\phi_{\ell}F_{j}\|_{W^{s^{\prime},p}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}w_{\frac{1}{p}\mu^{\prime}}(2^{\ell})\lim_{k\to\infty}\|\phi_{\ell}\phi_{j}f_{n_{k}^{(k)}}\|_{W^{s^{\prime},p}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\sum_{i=-1}^{1}w_{\frac{1}{p}\mu^{\prime}}(2^{\ell})\lim_{k\to\infty}\|\phi_{\ell}\phi_{\ell+i}f_{n_{k}^{(k)}}\|_{W^{s^{\prime},p}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}w_{\frac{1}{p}\mu^{\prime}}(2^{\ell})\lim_{k\to\infty}\|\phi_{\ell}f_{n_{k}^{(k)}}\|_{W^{s,p}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}w_{\frac{1}{p}(\mu^{\prime}-\mu)}(2^{\ell})\cdot\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\Big(w_{\frac{1}{p}\mu}(2^{\ell})\|\phi_{\ell}f_{n_{k}^{(k)}}\|_{W^{s,p}}\Big)

\displaystyle<\infty,

provided that
s^{\prime}<s
and
\mu^{\prime}>\mu
.

Next, we show convergence. First, note that, as a limit of

\phi_{j}f_{n_{k}^{(k)}}
,
we have
F_{j}(x)=0
whenever
\phi_{j}(x)=0
.
Thus, in view of (
2.18
), we can write
F_{j}=\phi_{j}G_{j}

with a well-defined function
G_{j}=\mathbf{1}_{\phi_{j}\neq 0}\cdot F_{j}/\phi_{j}

and thus, we have

\displaystyle f_{n_{k}^{(k)}}-F=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\phi_{j}(f_{n_{k}^{(k)}}-G_{j}).

(2.28)

We set
F_{-1}=G_{-1}=0
.

From (
2.22
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|f_{n_{k}^{(k)}}-F\|_{W^{s^{\prime},p}_{\mu^{\prime}}}^{p}&=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}w_{\mu^{\prime}}(2^{j})\|\phi_{j}(f_{n_{k}^{(k)}}-F)\|_{W^{s^{\prime},p}}^{p}.\end{split}

(2.29)

Then, from (
2.28
) with (
2.18
)
followed by (
2.27
) we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}w_{\mu^{\prime}}(2^{j})\|\phi_{j}(f_{n_{k}^{(k)}}-F)\|_{W^{s^{\prime},p}}^{p}&=w_{\mu^{\prime}}(2^{j})\bigg\|\phi_{j}\sum_{i=-1}^{1}\phi_{j+i}(f_{n_{k}^{(k)}}-G_{j+i})\bigg\|_{W^{s^{\prime},p}}^{p}\\
&\lesssim w_{\mu^{\prime}}(2^{j})\bigg\|\sum_{i=-1}^{1}\phi_{j+i}(f_{n_{k}^{(k)}}-G_{j+i})\bigg\|_{W^{s^{\prime},p}}^{p}\\
&\lesssim\sum_{\ell=j-1}^{j+1}w_{\mu^{\prime}}(2^{\ell-1})\|\phi_{\ell}(f_{n_{k}^{(k)}}-G_{\ell})\|_{W^{s^{\prime},p}}^{p}\\
&=\sum_{\ell=j-1}^{j+1}w_{\mu^{\prime}}(2^{\ell-1})\|\phi_{\ell}f_{n_{k}^{(k)}}-F_{\ell}\|_{W^{s^{\prime},p}}^{p}.\end{split}

(2.30)

For each
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
the right-hand side of (
2.30
) tends to
0
as
k\to\infty
.
Namely, for each
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
the summand on the right-hand side of (
2.29
) tends to
0
as
k\to\infty
.
On the other hand, in view of the uniform bound
on the
W^{s,p}_{\mu}
-norm of
f_{n_{k}^{(k)}}

and the fact that
F\in W^{s^{\prime},p}_{\mu^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we have

\displaystyle w_{\mu^{\prime}}(2^{j})

\displaystyle\|\phi_{j}(f_{n_{k}^{(k)}}-F)\|_{W^{s^{\prime},p}}^{p}

\displaystyle\lesssim w_{\mu^{\prime}}(2^{j})\|\phi_{j}f_{n_{k}^{(k)}}\|_{W^{s^{\prime},p}}^{p}+w_{\mu^{\prime}}(2^{j})\|\phi_{j}F\|_{W^{s^{\prime},p}}^{p},

which is summable in
j
.
Therefore, by the dominated convergence theorem
applied to (
2.29
),
we conclude that

f_{n_{k}^{(k)}}
converges to
F
in
W^{s^{\prime},p}_{\mu^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

as
k\to\infty
.
This concludes the proof of Lemma
2.2
.
∎

There seems to be no embedding relation
analogous to (
2.26
)
for weighted Sobolev spaces
W^{s,p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
in (
2.22
)
and weighted Besov spaces.
With a slight loss in
s
and
\mu
, however,
we have the following embeddings
for weighted Sobolev spaces and weighted Besov spaces.
We present the proof in Appendix
A
.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
1\leq p<\infty
, and
\mu>0
.
Then, there exist
c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that

\displaystyle\|f\|_{W^{s,p}_{\mu}}\lesssim\|f\|_{B^{s^{\prime},\mu^{\prime}}_{p,1}}

for any
s^{\prime}>s
and
0<\mu^{\prime}<c_{1}\mu
,
and

\displaystyle\|f\|_{B^{s,\mu}_{p,\infty}}\lesssim\|f\|_{W^{s,p}_{\mu^{\prime}}}

for any
0<\mu^{\prime}<c_{2}\mu
.

Remark 2.4
.

Another way to define a weighted Sobolev space
would be
to define a space
\mathbf{W}^{s,p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
via the norm:

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathbf{W}^{s,p}_{\mu}}=\|\langle\nabla\rangle^{s}f\|_{L^{p}_{\mu}}.

(2.31)

When
s=0
, we have
\mathbf{W}^{0,p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})=L^{p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.
Furthermore, this weighted Sobolev space
\mathbf{W}^{s,p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is compatible with the weighted Besov space
defined in (
2.25
) in the following sense:

\displaystyle\|f\|_{B^{s,\mu}_{p,\infty}}\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathbf{W}^{s,p}_{\mu}}\lesssim\|f\|_{B^{s,\mu}_{p,1}},

(2.32)

where the first inequality follows from Young’s inequality
with
[
55
, Lemma 1]
18
18

18

Lemma 2.6 in the arXiv version.

while the second inequality follows from Minkowski’s inequality.
It follows from Lemma
2.3

and (
2.32
) that

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathbf{W}^{s-\varepsilon,p}_{\mu_{1}}}\lesssim\|f\|_{W^{s,p}_{\mu}}\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathbf{W}^{s+\varepsilon,p}_{\mu_{2}}}.

for any
\varepsilon>0
and
\mu_{1}\gg\mu\gg\mu_{2}>0
.

In this paper,
we use the weighted Sobolev space
W^{s,p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
defined in (
2.22
)
since it is more convenient to work with

W^{s,p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
in
establishing coming down from infinity
in a weighted Sobolev spaces of positive regularities.

See Remark
2.7
for a discussion
on the bounded property of the linear damped wave propagator on
\mathbf{W}^{s,2}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

2.3.
Linear estimates on weighted Sobolev spaces

In this subsection, we establish
linear estimates
for the linear heat propagator and the linear damped wave propagator
on weighted Sobolev spaces.

Lemma 2.5
.

There exist small
C_{0},c>0

such that
for
any
s\geq 0
,
1\leq p<\infty
,

\mu>0
,
and
0<\mu^{\prime}<C_{0}\mu
, we have

\displaystyle\|P(t)f\|_{W^{s,p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim t^{-\frac{s}{2}}e^{-ct}\|f\|_{L^{p}_{\mu^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

(2.33)

for any
t>0
,
where
P(t)=e^{t(\Delta-1)}
is as in (
2.6
).

Proof.

We first introduce a cutoff function
\phi_{m}^{t}
in terms of the self-similar variable
\frac{x}{t^{\frac{1}{2}}}

for the (homogeneous) heat equation
\partial_{t}u-\Delta u=0

by setting

\displaystyle\phi_{m}^{t}(x):=\phi_{m}\bigg(\frac{x}{t^{\frac{1}{2}}}\bigg)

(2.34)

for
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
Then, given
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
it follows
from (
2.17
), (
2.18
), and (
2.34
) that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \phi_{j}(P(t)f)&=e^{-t}\sum_{m,m^{\prime}=0}^{\infty}\phi_{j}\big[(\phi_{m}^{t}p_{t})\ast(\phi_{m^{\prime}}f)\big]\\
&=e^{-t}\sum_{(m,m^{\prime})\in\Lambda_{t,j}}\phi_{j}\big[(\phi_{m}^{t}p_{t})\ast(\phi_{m^{\prime}}f)\big],\end{aligned}

(2.35)

where
\Lambda_{t,j}\subset(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})^{2}
is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Lambda_{t,j}=\Big\{(m,m^{\prime})\in(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})^{2}:\ &\tfrac{5}{8}\cdot 2^{m^{\prime}}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\{m^{\prime}\geq 1\}}\leq\tfrac{8}{5}\cdot 2^{j}+\tfrac{8}{5}\cdot 2^{m}t^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&\text{and }\,\tfrac{5}{8}\cdot 2^{m}t^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\{m\geq 1\}}\leq\tfrac{8}{5}\cdot 2^{j}+\tfrac{8}{5}\cdot 2^{m^{\prime}}\Big\}.\end{split}

(2.36)

In view of (
2.34
),
a direct computation shows that

\displaystyle\|\phi^{t}_{m}p_{t}\|_{L^{1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\exp(-c_{1}4^{m}),

\displaystyle\|\phi^{t}_{m}p_{t}\|_{W^{1,1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim t^{-\frac{1}{2}}\exp(-c_{1}4^{m})

for some
c_{1}>0
.
Hence, by the interpolation (
2.2
), we have

\displaystyle\|\phi^{t}_{m}p_{t}\|_{W^{s,1}}\lesssim t^{-\frac{s}{2}}\exp(-c_{1}4^{m})

(2.37)

for any
t>0
and
0<s<1
.
We point out that (
2.37
)
also holds for any
s\geq 0
.
Then,
from (
2.6
), (
2.23
), (
2.35
),
the fractional Leibniz rule (Lemma
2.8
(i) below),
(
2.16
),
Young’s inequality,
and (
2.37
),
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|P(t)f\|_{W^{s,p}_{\mu}}&\leq e^{-t}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{\mu}{p}2^{j\delta}}\|\phi_{j}(p_{t}\ast f)\|_{W^{s,p}}\\
&\leq e^{-t}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{\mu}{p}2^{j\delta}}\sum_{(m,m^{\prime})\in\Lambda_{t,j}}\|\phi_{j}\|_{W^{s,\infty}}\|(\phi^{t}_{m}p_{t})\ast(\phi_{m^{\prime}}f)\|_{W^{s,p}}\\
&\lesssim e^{-t}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{\mu}{p}2^{j\delta}}\sum_{(m,m^{\prime})\in\Lambda_{t,j}}\|\phi^{t}_{m}p_{t}\|_{W^{s,1}}\|\phi_{m^{\prime}}f\|_{L^{p}}\\
&\lesssim t^{-\frac{s}{2}}e^{-t}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{\mu}{p}2^{j\delta}}\sum_{(m,m^{\prime})\in\Lambda_{t,j}}e^{-c_{1}4^{m}}\|f\|_{L^{p}_{\mu^{\prime}}}e^{\frac{\mu^{\prime}}{p}a_{1}2^{m^{\prime}\delta}}.\end{split}

(2.38)

Hence, it remains to estimate

\displaystyle A_{t}:=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{\mu}{p}2^{j\delta}}\sum_{(m,m^{\prime})\in\Lambda_{t,j}}e^{-c_{1}4^{m}}e^{\frac{\mu^{\prime}}{p}a_{1}2^{m^{\prime}\delta}}.

First, we consider the case
0<t\lesssim 1
.
By summing over
m^{\prime}
with (
2.36
),
we have

\displaystyle A_{t}\lesssim\sum_{j,m=0}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{\mu}{p}2^{j\delta}}e^{-c_{1}4^{m}}e^{\frac{\mu^{\prime}}{p}c_{2}(2^{j\delta}+2^{m\delta}t^{\frac{\delta}{2}})}\lesssim 1,

(2.39)

provided that
\mu>c_{2}\mu^{\prime}
.
Next, we consider the case
t\gg 1
.
Note that
4^{m}\sim 2^{m\delta}t^{\frac{\delta}{2}}

implies

\displaystyle 2^{m\delta}t^{\frac{\delta}{2}}\sim t^{\frac{\delta}{2-\delta}}\ll t

(2.40)

for
t\gg 1
since
\delta<1
.
By first summing over
m^{\prime}
as in (
2.39
)
and then summing over
m
,

\displaystyle A_{t}\lesssim\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}e^{(-\frac{\mu}{p}+\frac{\mu^{\prime}}{p}c_{2})2^{j\delta}}e^{c_{3}t^{\frac{\delta}{2}\frac{2}{2-\delta}}}.

(2.41)

Therefore, the desired bound (
2.33
) follows
from (
2.38
), (
2.39
), and (
2.41
)
with (
2.40
).
∎

Next, we establish
estimates for
the linear damped wave operator.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{R}
.
Then, there exists
C_{0}>0
such that

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{D}(t)f\|_{H^{s}_{\mu}}\lesssim e^{-\frac{t}{4}}\|f\|_{H^{s-1}_{\mu^{\prime}}}

(2.42)

and

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{S}(t)f\|_{H^{s}_{\mu}}\lesssim e^{-\frac{t}{4}}\|f\|_{H^{s}_{\mu^{\prime}}}

(2.43)

for
\mu>C_{0}\mu^{\prime}>0
.
Here,
\mathcal{D}(t)
and
\mathcal{S}(t)
are as in (
1.23
) and (
2.5
).

Proof.

In view of (
2.17
), write

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|\phi_{j}\mathcal{D}(t)f\|_{H^{s}}=\bigg\|\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\phi_{j}\mathcal{D}(t)(\phi_{\ell}f)\bigg\|_{H^{s}}.\end{aligned}

(2.44)

In the following, we only consider
j,\ell\geq 1

but a similar argument holds for the case
j=0
or
\ell=0
.
Thanks to the finite speed of propagation and (
2.18
),
we have

\displaystyle\supp\mathcal{D}(t)(\phi_{\ell}f)\subset\big\{\tfrac{5}{8}\cdot 2^{\ell}-t\leq|x|\leq\tfrac{8}{5}\cdot 2^{\ell}+t\}.

As a result,

\phi_{j}\mathcal{D}(t)(\phi_{\ell}f)\neq 0

only when

\displaystyle\tfrac{5}{8}\cdot 2^{j}\leq\tfrac{8}{5}\cdot 2^{\ell}+t\qquad{\rm and}\qquad\tfrac{5}{8}\cdot 2^{\ell}-t\leq\tfrac{8}{5}\cdot 2^{j}.

(2.45)

From the triangle inequality, we have
\langle\xi\rangle^{s}\lesssim\langle\xi_{1}\rangle^{|s|}\langle\xi_{2}\rangle^{s}

for
\xi=\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}
.
Then, from Young’s inequality on the Fourier side, we have

\displaystyle\|fg\|_{H^{s}}\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathcal{F}L^{|s|,1}}\|g\|_{H^{s}},

(2.46)

where the Fourier-Lebesgue norm is defined by

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathcal{F}L^{s,1}}=\|\langle\xi\rangle^{s}\widehat{f}(\xi)\|_{L^{1}_{\xi}}.

(2.47)

Note that from (
2.16
), we have

\displaystyle\|\phi_{j}\|_{\mathcal{F}L^{s,1}}\lesssim_{s}1,

(2.48)

uniformly in
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.

Given
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
t\geq 0
,
define
\Gamma_{j,t}
by

\Gamma_{j,t}=\big\{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}:\ell\text{ satisfies \eqref{range1}}\big\}.

Then, we deduce from
(
2.44
),
(
2.46
)
(
1.23
), and
(
2.23
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|\phi_{j}\mathcal{D}(t)f\|_{H^{s}}&\lesssim e^{-\frac{t}{2}}\sum_{\ell\in\Gamma_{j,t}}\|\phi_{\ell}f\|_{H^{s-1}}\\
&\lesssim e^{-\frac{t}{2}}\bigg(\sum_{\ell\in\Gamma_{j,t}}e^{\frac{\mu^{\prime}}{2}2^{\ell\delta}}\bigg)\|f\|_{H^{s-1}_{\mu^{\prime}}}\\
&\lesssim e^{-\frac{t}{2}}e^{c_{0}\mu^{\prime}(2^{j\delta}+t^{\delta})}\|f\|_{H^{s-1}_{\mu^{\prime}}},\end{aligned}

(2.49)

where the implicit constant is independent of
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
Then, from (
2.23
) and (
2.49
), we have

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{D}(t)f\|_{H^{s}_{\mu}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{\mu}{2}2^{j\delta}}\|\phi_{j}\mathcal{D}(t)f\|_{H^{s}}

\displaystyle\lesssim e^{-\frac{t}{2}}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{\mu}{2}2^{j\delta}}e^{c_{0}\mu^{\prime}(2^{j\delta}+t^{\delta})}\|f\|_{H^{s-1}_{\mu^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim e^{-\frac{t}{2}}e^{c_{0}t^{\delta}}\|f\|_{H^{s-1}_{\mu^{\prime}}}\lesssim e^{-\frac{t}{4}}\|f\|_{H^{s-1}_{\mu^{\prime}}},

provided that
\mu>2c_{0}\mu^{\prime}
.
This proves (
2.42
).
Recalling that

\mathcal{S}(t)=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{D}(t)+e^{-\frac{t}{2}}\cos\Big(t\sqrt{\tfrac{3}{4}-\Delta}\Big),

the second bound (
2.43
) follows from an analogous
computation.
∎

Remark 2.7
.

(i)
Let
w_{\mu}
be as in (
2.11
).
Then, for any
0<\delta\leq 2
and
\mu>0
,
the Fourier transform of the weight
w_{\mu}
is positive.
Indeed, it follows from
Lemma 5 in
[
28
]

on completely monotonic functions that

w_{\mu}(x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-t\langle x\rangle^{2}}d\alpha_{\mu}(t)=e^{-t}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-t|x|^{2}}d\alpha_{\mu}(t),

where
\alpha_{\mu}(t)
is bounded and non-decreasing and the integral converges for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Hence, as a superposition of the Gaussians
e^{-t|x|^{2}}
,
we conclude that the Fourier transform of the weight
w_{\mu}
is positive.

(ii) Let
\mathbf{W}^{s,2}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be the weighted Sobolev space defined in (
2.31
) with
p=2
.
Then, an analogue of Lemma
2.6

also holds for
\mathbf{W}^{s,2}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
Using the positivity of the Fourier transform of the weight
w_{\frac{\mu}{2}}
, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{D}(t)f\|_{\mathbf{W}^{s,2}_{\mu}}

\displaystyle=\|\langle\nabla\rangle^{s}\mathcal{D}(t)f\|_{L^{2}_{\mu}}=\Big\|\big(\langle\,\cdot\,\rangle^{s}\widehat{\mathcal{D}(t)f}\big)*\widehat{w}_{\frac{\mu}{2}}(\xi)\Big\|_{L^{2}_{\xi}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\Big\|\big(\langle\,\cdot\,\rangle^{s-1}|\widehat{f}|\big)*\widehat{w}_{\frac{\mu}{2}}(\xi)\Big\|_{L^{2}_{\xi}}=\|f\|_{\mathbf{W}^{s-1,2}_{\mu}}.

A similar computation holds for
\mathcal{S}(t)
.
Note that, unlike Lemma
2.6
,
there is no loss in the coefficient
\mu
of the weight.

2.4.
Product estimates

We first state the product estimate on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Lemma 2.8
.

(i)

Let
s\geq 0
.
Suppose that

1<p_{j},q_{j}\leq\infty
and
1\leq r<\infty

such that
\frac{1}{p_{j}}+\frac{1}{q_{j}}=\frac{1}{r}
,
j=1,2
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\|fg\|_{W^{s,r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim\Big(\|f\|_{L^{p_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|g\|_{W^{s,q_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\|f\|_{W^{s,p_{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|g\|_{L^{q_{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\Big).

(2.50)

(ii)

Let
s>0
.
Suppose that

(ii.a)

1<p\leq\infty
and
1<q,r<\infty
,

(ii.b)

1<p=r\leq\infty
and
q=\infty
, or

(ii.c)

1<p=q^{\prime}\leq\infty
and
r=1

such that

\frac{1}{r}\leq\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}\leq\frac{1}{r}+\frac{s}{d}

and

q,r^{\prime}\geq p^{\prime}
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\|fg\|_{W^{-s,r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim\|f\|_{W^{-s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|g\|_{W^{s,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

(2.51)

As for the fractional Leibniz rule (
2.50
)
see
[
44
,
21
]

for
p_{j},q_{j}<\infty

and

[
58
, (2.1)]

(for the one-dimensional case which can be easily extended
to higher dimensions); see also
[
35
]
.
As for the second estimate (
2.51
),
see
[
38
,
6
]
.
The estimates (
2.50
) and (
2.51
) indeed hold
for wider ranges of indices;
see
[
6
]
for a further discussion.

Remark 2.9
.

Note that, given a (nice) subset
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
the estimates (
2.50
) and (
2.51
)
also hold on the localized version of Sobolev spaces
W^{s,p}(K)

defined in (
2.3
).
Given
f
and
g
on
K
,
let
{\bf f}
and
{\bf g}

be extensions onto
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Then, from (
2.3
) and (
2.50
), we have

\displaystyle\|fg\|_{W^{s,r}(K)}

\displaystyle\leq\|{\bf f}{\bf g}\|_{W^{s,r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\Big(\|{\bf f}\|_{L^{p_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|{\bf g}\|_{W^{s,q_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\|{\bf f}\|_{W^{s,p_{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|{\bf g}\|_{L^{q_{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\Big).

By taking infima over extensions

{\bf f}
and
{\bf g}
, we then obtain

\displaystyle\|fg\|_{W^{s,r}(K)}

\displaystyle\ \lesssim\Big(\|f\|_{L^{p_{1}}(K)}\|g\|_{W^{s,q_{1}}(K)}+\|f\|_{W^{s,p_{2}}(K)}\|g\|_{L^{q_{2}}(K)}\Big).

A similar comment applies to all the preliminary estimates
presented in this section.

Next, we state a bi-parameter version
of the fractional Leibniz rule.

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
0\leq s\leq 1
,
1<p_{1},p_{2}\leq\infty
,
and
1\leq p<\infty

with
\frac{1}{p}=\frac{1}{p_{1}}+\frac{1}{p_{2}}
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\|\langle\nabla_{x}\rangle^{s}\langle\nabla_{y}\rangle^{s}(fg)\|_{L^{p}_{x,y}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}\lesssim\|\langle\nabla_{x}\rangle^{s}\langle\nabla_{y}\rangle^{s}f\|_{L^{p_{1}}_{x,y}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}\|\langle\nabla_{x}\rangle^{s}\langle\nabla_{y}\rangle^{s}g\|_{L^{p_{2}}_{x,y}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}

(2.52)

for any functions
f=f(x,y)
and
g=g(x,y)
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{y}
.

Proof.

Let
0\leq s\leq 1
and
1<p<\infty
.
Let
f=f(x,y)
and
g=g(x,y)
be functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{y}
.
Then, by the Marcinkiewicz multiplier theorem
(Theorem 6.2.4 in
[
33
]
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\langle\nabla_{x}\rangle^{s}\langle\nabla_{y}\rangle^{s}(fg)\|_{L^{p}_{x,y}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}&\lesssim\|fg\|_{L^{p}_{x,y}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}+\big\||\nabla_{x}|^{s}(fg)\big\|_{L^{p}_{x,y}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}\\
&\quad+\big\||\nabla_{y}|^{s}(fg)\big\|_{L^{p}_{x,y}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}+\big\||\nabla_{x}|^{s}|\nabla_{y}|^{s}(fg)\big\|_{L^{p}_{x,y}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}.\end{split}

(2.53)

Indeed, noting that

\displaystyle\text{RHS of }\eqref{bi1}\sim\big\|(1+|\nabla_{x}|^{s})(1+|\nabla_{y}|^{s})(fg)\big\|_{L^{p}_{x,y}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})},

the bound (
2.53
) follows
since the multiplier
m(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})
,
(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, defined by

\displaystyle m(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=\frac{\langle\eta_{1}\rangle^{s}\langle\eta_{2}\rangle^{s}}{(1+|\eta_{1}|^{s})(1+|\eta_{2}|^{s})}

is a Marcinkiewicz multiplier.

By Hölder’s inequality and the usual fractional Leibniz rule (Lemma
2.8
(i)),
the first three terms on the right-hand side of (
2.53
) are bounded
by the right-hand side of (
2.52
).
As for the last term on the right-hand side of (
2.53
),
it follows from the bi-parameter fractional Leibniz rule (
[
57
, (61) on p. 295]
;
see also
[
58
, (3.3)]
)
that

\displaystyle\big\||\nabla_{x}|^{s}|\nabla_{y}|^{s}(fg)\big\|_{L^{p}_{x,y}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\big\||\nabla_{x}|^{s}|\nabla_{y}|^{s}f\big\|_{L^{p_{1}}_{x,y}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}\|g\|_{L^{p_{2}}_{x,y}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}

\displaystyle\quad+\|f\big\|_{L^{p_{1}}_{x,y}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}\big\||\nabla_{x}|^{s}|\nabla_{y}|^{s}g\big\|_{L^{p_{2}}_{x,y}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}

\displaystyle\quad+\big\||\nabla_{x}|^{s}f\big\|_{L^{p_{1}}_{x,y}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}\big\||\nabla_{y}|^{s}g\big\|_{L^{p_{2}}_{x,y}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}
