\displaystyle\quad+\big\||\nabla_{y}|^{s}f\big\|_{L^{p_{1}}_{x,y}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}\big\||\nabla_{x}|^{s}g\big\|_{L^{p_{2}}_{x,y}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})},

which is once again bounded
by the right-hand side of (
2.52
).
This concludes the proof of Lemma
2.10
.
∎

We extend the product estimates in Lemma
2.8
to weighted Sobolev spaces.

Lemma 2.11
.

(i)

Let
s\geq 0
and
\mu>0
.
Suppose that

1<p_{j},q_{j}\leq\infty
and
1\leq r<\infty

such that
\frac{1}{p_{j}}+\frac{1}{q_{j}}=\frac{1}{r}
,
j=1,2
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\|fg\|_{W^{s,r}_{\mu}}\lesssim\|f\|_{W^{0,p_{1}}_{\mu}}\|g\|_{W^{s,q_{1}}_{\frac{\mu}{2^{\delta}}}}+\|f\|_{W^{s,p_{2}}_{\mu}}\|g\|_{W^{0,q_{2}}_{\frac{\mu}{2^{\delta}}}}.

(2.54)

(ii)

Let
s>0
and
\mu>0
,
and let
1\leq p,q,r\leq\infty
be as in Lemma
2.8
(ii).
Then, we have

\displaystyle\|fg\|_{W^{-s,r}_{\mu}}\lesssim\|f\|_{W^{-s,p}_{\mu}}\|g\|_{W^{s,q}_{\frac{\mu}{2^{\delta}}}}.

(2.55)

Proof.

(i)
From (
2.22
)
(
2.21
),
the fractional Leibniz rule (Lemma
2.8
(i)),
and Hölder’s inequality (in
j
) with
\frac{1}{r}=\frac{1}{p_{1}}+\frac{1}{q_{1}}
, (
2.20
),
and (
2.11
),
we have

\displaystyle\|fg\|_{W^{s,r}_{\mu}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}w_{\mu}(2^{j})\|\phi_{j}fg\|_{W^{s,r}}^{r}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{r}}=\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}w_{\mu}(2^{j})\|\phi_{j}f\cdot\widetilde{\phi}_{j}g\|_{W^{s,r}}^{r}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{r}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}w_{\mu}(2^{j})\|\phi_{j}f\|_{L^{p_{1}}}^{r}\|\widetilde{\phi}_{j}g\|_{W^{s,q_{1}}}^{r}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{r}}

\displaystyle\quad+\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}w_{\mu}(2^{j})\|\phi_{j}f\|_{W^{s,p_{2}}}^{r}\|\widetilde{\phi}_{j}g\|_{L^{q_{2}}}^{r}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{r}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f\|_{W^{0,p_{1}}_{\mu}}\|g\|_{W^{s,q_{1}}_{\frac{\mu}{2^{\delta}}}}+\|f\|_{W^{s,p_{2}}_{\mu}}\|g\|_{W^{0,q_{2}}_{\frac{\mu}{2^{\delta}}}},

which yields (
2.54
).

(ii) By Lemma
2.8
(ii), we have

\|\phi_{j}f\cdot\widetilde{\phi}_{j}g\|_{W^{-s,r}}\lesssim\|\phi_{j}f\|_{W^{-s,p}}\|\widetilde{\phi}_{j}g\|_{W^{s,q}}.

Then, (
2.55
) follows from applying
Hölder’s inequality as in Part (i).
∎

2.5.
Tools from stochastic analysis

In the following, we review some basic facts on
the Hermite polynomials
and the Wiener chaos estimate.
See, for example,
[
47
,
60
]
.

We define the
k
th Hermite polynomials

H_{k}(x;\sigma)
with variance
\sigma
via the following generating function:

e^{tx-\frac{1}{2}\sigma t^{2}}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{k}}{k!}H_{k}(x;\sigma)

for
t,x\in\mathbb{R}
and
\sigma>0
.
When
\sigma=1
, we set
H_{k}(x)=H_{k}(x;1)
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle H_{k}(x;\sigma)=\sigma^{\frac{k}{2}}H_{k}(\sigma^{-\frac{1}{2}}x).

(2.56)

It is well known that
\big\{H_{k}/\sqrt{k!}\big\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}

form an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-x^{2}/2}dx)
.
The following identity:

\displaystyle H_{k}(x+y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\begin{pmatrix}k\\
\ell\end{pmatrix}x^{k-\ell}H_{\ell}(y),

which, together with (
2.56
), yields

\displaystyle\begin{split}H_{k}(x+y;\sigma)&=\sigma^{\frac{k}{2}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\begin{pmatrix}k\\
\ell\end{pmatrix}\sigma^{-\frac{k-\ell}{2}}x^{k-\ell}H_{\ell}(\sigma^{-\frac{1}{2}}y)\\
&=\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\begin{pmatrix}k\\
\ell\end{pmatrix}x^{k-\ell}H_{\ell}(y;\sigma).\end{split}

(2.57)

Let
(H,B,\mu)
be an abstract Wiener space.
Namely,
\mu
is a Gaussian measure on a separable Banach space
B

with
H\subset B
as its Cameron-Martin space.
Given a complete orthonormal system
\{e_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subset B^{*}
of
H^{*}=H
,
we define a polynomial chaos of order

k
to be an element of the form
\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}H_{k_{j}}(\langle x,e_{j}\rangle)
,
where
x\in B
,
k_{j}\neq 0
for only finitely many
j
’s,
k=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}k_{j}
,

H_{k_{j}}
is the Hermite polynomial of degree
k_{j}
,
and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle=\vphantom{|}_{B}\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{B^{*}}
denotes the
B
–
B^{*}
duality pairing.
We then
denote the closure of
polynomial chaoses of order
k

under
L^{2}(B,\mu)
by
\mathcal{H}_{k}
.
The elements in
\mathcal{H}_{k}

are called homogeneous Wiener chaoses of order
k
.
Then, we have the following Ito-Wiener decomposition:

L^{2}(B,\mu)=\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{H}_{k}.

See Theorem 1.1.1 in
[
60
]
.
See also
[
43
,
9
]
.
We also set

\mathcal{H}_{\leq k}=\bigoplus_{j=0}^{k}\mathcal{H}_{j}

for
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

We now state the Wiener chaos estimate,
which is a consequence of Nelson’s hypercontractivity
[
59
]
.
See, for example,
[
84
, Theorem I.22]
.
See also
[
86
, Proposition 2.4]
.

Lemma 2.12
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
Then, we have

\Big(\mathbb{E}\big[|X|^{p}\big]\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq(p-1)^{\frac{k}{2}}\Big(\mathbb{E}\big[|X|^{2}\big]\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

for any random variable
X\in\mathcal{H}_{k}
and any
2\leq p<\infty
.

Next, we recall the following orthogonal property of Wick powers;
see
[
60
, Lemma 1.1.1]
.

Lemma 2.13
.

Let
Y_{1},Y_{2}
be two real-valued, mean-zero, and jointly Gaussian random variables with variances

\sigma_{1}=\mathbb{E}[Y_{1}^{2}]>0
and
\sigma_{2}=\mathbb{E}[Y_{2}^{2}]>0
.
Then, for
k,m\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[H_{k}(Y_{1};\sigma_{1})H_{m}(Y_{2};\sigma_{2})\big]=\mathbf{1}_{k=m}\cdot k!\big(\mathbb{E}[Y_{1}Y_{2}]\big)^{k}.

The following lemma allows us to compute
the regularity of a stochastic term
by testing it against a test function.
See
[
87
, Proposition A.3.3]

for the proof.

Lemma 2.14
.

Let
X\in\mathcal{H}_{\leq k}
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
19
19

19

This means that

\langle X,\varphi\rangle\in\mathcal{H}_{\leq k}

for any test function
\varphi
.

Let
R\geq 1
.
Suppose that there exist
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
and
2\leq q<\infty

such that

\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|\langle X,\varphi\rangle\big|^{2}\Big]\leq A^{2}\|\varphi\|_{W^{\sigma,q^{\prime}}}^{2}

for any test function
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}
supported on a ball
B
of radius 1
with
B\subset B_{2R}
,
where
\frac{1}{q^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{q}=1
.
Then, given any small
\varepsilon>0
and any finite
p\geq\frac{4}{\varepsilon}
, we have

\mathbb{E}\Big[\|X\|_{W^{-\frac{2}{q}-\sigma-\varepsilon,\infty}(B_{R})}^{p}\Big]\lesssim_{\sigma,q,R}A^{p}.

\bullet

Wasserstein-1 metric and weak convergence.

Let
(X,d)
be a Polish space (separable complete metric space).
Then, the Wasserstein-1 metric
for two probability measures
\mu,\nu

on
X
is defined by

\displaystyle d_{\rm Wass}(\mu,\nu)=\inf_{\mathfrak{p}\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)}\int_{X\times X}d(x,y)d\mathfrak{p}(x,y).

(2.58)

Here,
\Pi(\mu,\nu)
is the set of probability measures
\mathfrak{p}

on
X\times X

whose first and second marginals are given by
\mu
and
\nu
,
namely,

\displaystyle\int_{y\in X}d\mathfrak{p}(x,y)=d\mu(x)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\int_{x\in X}d\mathfrak{p}(x,y)=d\nu(y).

(2.59)

The Wasserstein-1 metric is also known as the
Kantorovich-Rubinstein distance;
see the
Kantorovich-Rubinstein theorem (
[
93
, Theorem 1.14]
)
which provides the dual characterization of (
2.58
).
We point out that the infimum in (
2.58
)
is indeed attained; see
[
93
, Theorem 1.3]
.

In general, convergence in the Wasserstein-1 metric
is stronger than weak convergence.
We recall the following characterization of convergence
in the Wasserstein-1 metric;
see
[
93
, Theorem 7.12]
.

Lemma 2.15
.

Let
(X,d)
be a Polish space.
Given a sequence of probability measures

\{\mu_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
on
X
and a probability measure
\mu
on
X
,
the sequence
\{\mu_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

converges to
\mu
in the Wasserstein-1 metric as
n\to\infty

if and only if

(i)

\mu_{n}
converges weakly to
\mu
as
n\to\infty
, and

(ii)

for some

(
and thus for any
)

x_{0}\in X
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\int_{d(x,x_{0})\geq R}d(x,x_{0})d\mu_{n}(x)=0.

(2.60)

Remark 2.16
.

When the metric
d
on
X
is bounded,
the condition (
2.60
) trivially holds,
and thus
convergence in the Wasserstein-1 metric coincides
with weak convergence.
See also Remark 7.13 (iii) in
[
93
]
.

Lastly,
we recall the Prokhorov theorem
and the Skorokhod representation theorem.
We first recall the definition of tightness.

Definition 2.17
.

Let
\mathcal{J}
be a nonempty index set.
A family

\{\mu_{n}\}_{n\in\mathcal{J}}
of probability measures
on a metric space
\mathcal{M}
is said to be tight
if, for every
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a compact set
K_{\varepsilon}\subset\mathcal{M}

such that
\sup_{n\in\mathcal{J}}\mu_{n}(K_{\varepsilon}^{c})\leq\varepsilon
.
We say that
\{\mu_{n}\}_{n\in\mathcal{J}}
is relatively compact, if every sequence
in
\{\mu_{n}\}_{n\in\mathcal{J}}
contains a weakly convergent subsequence.

We now
recall the following
Prokhorov theorem from
[
8
]
.

Lemma 2.18

(Prokhorov theorem)
.

If a sequence of probability measures
on a metric space
\mathcal{M}
is tight, then
it is relatively compact.
If in addition,
\mathcal{M}
is separable and complete, then relative compactness is
equivalent to tightness.

Lastly, we recall the following Skorokhod representation
theorem from
[
5
, Chapter 31]
.

Lemma 2.19

(Skorokhod representation theorem)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a complete
separable metric space
(
i.e. a Polish space
)
.
Suppose that
a sequence

\{\mu_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

of probability measures
on
\mathcal{M}
converges weakly to a probability measure
\mu

as
n\to\infty
.
Then, there exist a probability space
(\widetilde{\Omega},\widetilde{\mathcal{F}},\widetilde{\mathbb{P}})
,
and random variables
X_{n},X:\widetilde{\Omega}\to\mathcal{M}

such that

\displaystyle\Law(X_{n})=\mu_{n}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Law(X)=\mu,

and
X_{n}
converges
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}
-almost surely to
X
as
n\to\infty
.

3.
Coming down from infinity

In this section, we study the following stochastic nonlinear heat equation (SNLH) on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}X+(1-\Delta)X+:\!X^{k}\!:\,=\sqrt{2}\xi\\
X|_{t=0}=X_{0},\end{cases}

(3.1)

where
k\in 2\mathbb{N}+1
and

\xi
is a Gaussian space-time white noise on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Let
Z
denote the
solution to the following linear equation:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}Z+(1-\Delta)Z=\sqrt{2}\xi\\
Z|_{t=0}=0.\end{cases}

(3.2)

Namely,
Z
is the stochastic convolution,
formally given by

\displaystyle Z(t)=\sqrt{2}\int_{0}^{t}P(t-t^{\prime})\xi(dt^{\prime}),

where
P(t)=e^{t(\Delta-1)}
is the linear heat propagator.
Then, with the first order expansion

\displaystyle X=Y+Z,

(3.3)

the remainder term
Y=X-Z
satisfies

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}Y+(1-\Delta)Y+\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}{k\choose\ell}:\!Z^{\ell}\!:\,Y^{k-\ell}=0\\
Y|_{t=0}=X_{0},\end{cases}

(3.4)

where

:\!Z^{\ell}\!:
denotes the Wick-renormalized power of
Z
.
See Section 5 in
[
55
]
for a further discussion.
We say that
X
is a solution to (
3.1
)
if it satisfies (
3.3
) together with (
3.2
)
and (
3.4
).
Our main goal in this section is to establish
coming down from infinity for a solution
Y
to (
3.4
)
in weighted Sobolev spaces of positive regularities
(Proposition
3.3
),
which will play a crucial role in Subsection
4.2
.
For this purpose, we consider

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}Y+(1-\Delta)Y+\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}Z^{(\ell)}Y^{k-\ell}=0\\
Y|_{t=0}=X_{0},\end{cases}

(3.5)

where
Z^{(0)}=1
and
Z^{(\ell)}
,
\ell=1,\dots,k
,
are given space-time distributions.
We
first establish
coming down from infinity for a solution
Y
to (
3.5
)
in weighted Lebesgue spaces
in Subsection
3.1
.
In Subsection
3.2
,
we then
establish
coming down from infinity
in weighted Sobolev spaces of positive regularities
(Proposition
3.3
).
As a corollary to the coming down from infinity
in weighted Lebesgue spaces,
we construct a limiting Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}_{\infty}

on the plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}

(Theorem
1.2
(i));
see Subsection
3.3
.
We point out that
while the construction of a limiting Gibbs measure on
\mathbb{R}^{2}

requires only
the coming down from infinity
in weighted Lebesgue spaces
(Proposition
3.1
),
the coming down from infinity
in weighted Sobolev spaces of positive regularity
(Proposition
3.3
)
plays an essential role in the construction
of the enhanced Gibbs measure
as well as
global-in-time dynamics on
\mathbb{R}^{2}

presented in Section
4
.

In the following discussion,
the regularity of the initial data
X_{0}
in (
3.5
) does not
play an important role
(which is precisely the point of coming down from infinity)
as long as a solution
X=Y+Z
exists.
For this reason, we do not precisely state the regularity
of the initial data
X_{0}
in
Propositions
3.1
and
3.3
.
For our application to (
3.1
)
(and its
L
-periodic counterpart (
3.42
)),
it suffices to take
X_{0}\in B^{-\varepsilon,\mu}_{p_{0},\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

for some
\varepsilon>0
,
p_{0}\gg 1
, and
\mu>0

so that a global solution is guaranteed to exist; see
[
55
]
.
In particular, the initial data distributed by the
L
-periodic
\Phi^{k+1}_{2}
-measure

\rho_{L}
in (
1.21
) almost surely satisfies this regularity condition.

3.1.
Coming down from infinity on weighted Lebesgue spaces

In this subsection,
our main goal is to prove the following coming down from infinity
for a solution
Y
to (
3.5
)
in the weighted Lebesgue space
L^{p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.
As compared to the previous work
[
92
]

on
\mathbb{T}^{2}
,
we need to proceed with more care,
using a decomposition of the physical space into unit cubes
and the Littlewood-Paley decomposition; see (
3.16
)-(
3.26
).

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
k\in 2\mathbb{N}+1
and
T>0
.
Let
Y
be a solution to (
3.5
)
on the time interval
[0,T]
.
Then,
given any finite
p\geq 1
and
\mu>0
,
there exist
\lambda>1
, small
\varepsilon>0
, finite
p_{0}\gg 1
, and
0<\mu_{0}<\mu
such that

\displaystyle\|Y(t)\|_{L^{p}_{\mu}}\leq C\bigg\{t^{-\frac{1}{(\lambda-1)p}}\vee\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\|Z^{(\ell)}(t)\|_{B^{-\varepsilon,\mu_{0}}_{p_{0},p_{0}}}^{\frac{p_{0}}{\lambda p}}\bigg\}

(3.6)

for
0<t\leq T
,
where the constant
C
is independent of the initial condition
X_{0}

in (
3.5
) and
T>0
.

For the later use, we set

\displaystyle C_{\bar{Z},p,\mu}(t)=\sup_{0\leq t^{\prime}\leq t}\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\|Z^{(\ell)}(t^{\prime})\|_{B^{-\varepsilon,\mu_{0}}_{p_{0},p_{0}}}^{\frac{p_{0}}{\lambda p}}.

(3.7)

Before proceeding to the proof of Proposition
3.1
,
we first recall the following key lemma; see Lemma 3.8 in
[
92
]
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
F:[0,T]\to[0,\infty)
be a differentiable function.
Suppose that there exist
\lambda>1
and
c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}F(t)+c_{1}F^{\lambda}(t)\leq c_{2}

(3.8)

for any
0\leq t\leq T
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle F(t)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{F(0)}{\big(1+tF^{\lambda-1}(0)(\lambda-1)\frac{c_{1}}{2}\big)^{\frac{1}{\lambda-1}}}\vee\bigg(\frac{2c_{2}}{c_{1}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{\lambda}}

\displaystyle\leq\Bigg\{t^{-\frac{1}{\lambda-1}}\bigg((\lambda-1)\frac{c_{1}}{2}\bigg)^{-\frac{1}{\lambda-1}}\Bigg\}\vee\bigg(\frac{2c_{2}}{c_{1}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{\lambda}}

for any
0<t\leq T
.

Lemma
3.2
shows that in order to establish coming down from infinity,
it suffices to establish a bound of the form (
3.8
).

We now present the proof of Proposition
3.1
.

Proof of Proposition
3.1
.

By Hölder’s inequality, it suffices to consider
p\in 2\mathbb{N}
.
Fix
p\in 2\mathbb{N}
.
Then, using the equation (
3.5
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \frac{1}{p}\partial_{t}\|Y(t)\|_{L^{p}_{\mu}}^{p}&=\langle\partial_{t}Y(t),Y^{p-1}(t)w_{\mu}\rangle_{L^{2}_{x}}\\
&=\bigg\langle\Delta Y(t)-Y(t)-\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}Z^{(\ell)}(t)Y^{k-\ell}(t),Y^{p-1}(t)w_{\mu}\bigg\rangle_{L^{2}_{x}}.\end{aligned}

(3.9)

For simplicity of notation, we will drop the time dependence in the following computations.

From (
2.11
) with
0<\delta<1
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle\Delta Y,Y^{p-1}w_{\mu}\rangle_{L^{2}}&=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\nabla Y\cdot\nabla(Y^{p-1}w_{\mu})dx\\
&=-(p-1)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla Y|^{2}Y^{p-2}w_{\mu}dx\\
&\hphantom{X}+\mu\delta\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\nabla Y\cdot x)Y^{p-1}\langle x\rangle^{\delta-2}w_{\mu}dx\\
&=:-(p-1)K_{t}+B_{0}.\end{split}

(3.10)

Since
p
is even, we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \langle-Y,Y^{p-1}w_{\mu}\rangle_{L^{2}}&=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Y^{p}w_{\mu}dx\leq 0.\end{aligned}

(3.11)

As for the contribution from the last term in (
3.9
),
recalling that
Z^{(0)}=1
, we write

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \bigg\langle&-\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}Z^{(\ell)}Y^{k-\ell},Y^{p-1}w_{\mu}\bigg\rangle_{L^{2}}\\
&=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Y^{p+k-1}w_{\mu}dx-\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Y^{p+k-\ell-1}Z^{(\ell)}w_{\mu}dx\\
&=:-L_{t}-\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}B_{\ell}.\end{aligned}

(3.12)

Since
p\in 2\mathbb{N}
and
k\in 2\mathbb{N}+1
,
we have
K_{t}
and
L_{t}
are non-negative.
In the following, we control the terms
B_{\ell}
,
\ell=0,\dots,k
,
by these non-negative terms.
For this reason, we set

\displaystyle M_{t}=K_{t}+L_{t}.

(3.13)

We first treat
B_{0}
in (
3.10
).
In view of the fast decay of the weight
w_{\mu}
,
it follows from Hölder’s inequality that

\displaystyle\|Y\|_{L^{p}_{\mu}}^{p}\leq C_{\mu,\delta}\|Y\|_{L^{p+k-1}_{\mu}}^{p}=C_{\mu,\delta}L_{t}^{\frac{p}{p+k-1}}\leq C_{\mu,\delta}M_{t}^{\frac{p}{p+k-1}}.

(3.14)

Then, by recalling
0<\delta<1

and applying
Cauchy-Schwarz’s inequality
and Young’s inequality with (
3.14
)
and (
3.13
),
we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} |B_{0}|&\leq\mu\delta\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla Y|\cdot|Y|^{p-1}\langle x\rangle^{\delta-1}w_{\mu}dx\\
&\leq C_{\mu,\delta}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla Y||Y|^{\frac{p-2}{2}}\cdot|Y|^{\frac{p}{2}}w_{\mu}dx\\
&\leq C_{\mu,\delta}K_{t}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|Y\|_{L^{p}_{\mu}}^{\frac{p}{2}}\leq\tfrac{1}{100}K_{t}+C^{\prime}_{\mu,\delta}\|Y\|_{L^{p}_{\mu}}^{p}\\
&\leq\tfrac{1}{100}M_{t}+C^{\prime\prime}_{\mu,\delta}.\end{aligned}

(3.15)

Next, we consider
B_{\ell}
,
\ell=1,\dots,k
.
Let
Q=\big[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\big)^{2}
be the unit cube in
\mathbb{R}^{2}

and set
Q_{n}=n+Q
.
Fix small
\varepsilon>0

and
r>1
sufficiently close to
1
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}|B_{\ell}|&=\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Y^{p+k-\ell-1}Z^{(\ell)}w_{\mu}dx\bigg|\leq\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\bigg|\int_{Q_{n}}Y^{p+k-\ell-1}Z^{(\ell)}w_{\mu}dx\bigg|\\
&\leq\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\|Y^{p+k-\ell-1}w_{\mu}\|_{W^{\varepsilon,r}(Q_{n})}\|Z^{(\ell)}\|_{W^{-\varepsilon,r^{\prime}}(Q_{n})},\end{split}

(3.16)

where
\frac{1}{r}+\frac{1}{r^{\prime}}=1
.

From (
2.26
)
and the Littlewood-Paley decomposition
with the uniform boundedness on
L^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

of the Littlewood-Paley projector
\mathbf{Q}_{j}
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\|Y^{p+k-\ell-1}w_{\mu}\|_{W^{\varepsilon,r}(Q_{n})}\lesssim\|Y^{p+k-\ell-1}w_{\mu}\|_{B^{2\varepsilon}_{r,\infty}(Q_{n})}\\
&\hphantom{X}=\sup_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}2^{2\varepsilon j}\|\mathbf{Q}_{j}(Y^{p+k-\ell-1}w_{\mu})\|_{L^{r}(Q_{n})}\\
&\hphantom{X}\leq\sup_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}2^{2\varepsilon j}\sum_{j_{1},j_{2}=0}^{\infty}\big\|\mathbf{Q}_{j}\big(\mathbf{Q}_{j_{1}}(Y^{p+k-\ell-1})\mathbf{Q}_{j_{2}}w_{\mu}\big)\big\|_{L^{r}(Q_{n})}\\
&\hphantom{X}\lesssim\sum_{j_{1}\geq j_{2}+2}2^{2\varepsilon j_{1}}\|\mathbf{Q}_{j_{1}}(Y^{p+k-\ell-1})\mathbf{Q}_{j_{2}}w_{\mu}\|_{L^{r}(Q_{n})}\\
&\hphantom{XX}+\sum_{j_{1}<j_{2}+2}2^{2\varepsilon j_{2}}\|\mathbf{Q}_{j_{1}}(Y^{p+k-\ell-1})\mathbf{Q}_{j_{2}}w_{\mu}\|_{L^{r}(Q_{n})}\\
&\hphantom{X}=:\hskip 1.42262pt\text{I}\hskip 1.42262pt+\text{I \hskip-7.96677pt I}.\end{split}

(3.17)

As for the first term
I
, it follows from
(
2.14
) and
(
2.13
) that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hskip 1.42262pt\text{I}\hskip 1.42262pt&\lesssim\sum_{j_{1}\geq j_{2}+2}2^{2\varepsilon j_{1}}2^{-\frac{1}{2}j_{2}}w_{\mu}(n)\|\mathbf{Q}_{j_{1}}(Y^{p+k-\ell-1})\|_{L^{r}(Q_{n})}\\
&\lesssim w_{\mu}(n)\|Y^{p+k-\ell-1}\|_{W^{3\varepsilon,r}(Q_{n})}.\end{split}

(3.18)

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\text{I \hskip-7.96677pt I}&\lesssim\sum_{j_{1}<j_{2}+2}2^{(2\varepsilon-\frac{1}{2})j_{2}}w_{\mu}(n)\|\mathbf{Q}_{j_{1}}(Y^{p+k-\ell-1})\|_{L^{r}(Q_{n})}\\
&\lesssim w_{\mu}(n)\|Y^{p+k-\ell-1}\|_{W^{3\varepsilon,r}(Q_{n})}.\end{split}

(3.19)

In the following, we estimate
\|Y^{p+k-\ell-1}\|_{W^{3\varepsilon,r}(Q_{n})}
.
Define
M_{t}(Q_{n})

by

\displaystyle M_{t}(Q_{n})=\int_{Q_{n}}|\nabla Y|^{2}Y^{p-2}+Y^{p+k-1}dx.

(3.20)

By the interpolation (
2.2
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\|Y^{p+k-\ell-1}\|_{W^{3\varepsilon,r}(Q_{n})}\lesssim\|Y^{p+k-\ell-1}\|_{L^{r}(Q_{n})}^{1-3\varepsilon}\|Y^{p+k-\ell-1}\|_{W^{1,r}(Q_{n})}^{3\varepsilon}\\
&\quad\sim\|Y^{p+k-\ell-1}\|_{L^{r}(Q_{n})}^{1-3\varepsilon}\|\nabla(Y^{p+k-\ell-1})\|_{L^{r}(Q_{n})}^{3\varepsilon}+\|Y^{p+k-\ell-1}\|_{L^{r}(Q_{n})}.\end{split}

(3.21)

By Hölder’s inequality
and by choosing
r>1
sufficiently close to
1
,
we have

\displaystyle\|Y^{p+k-\ell-1}\|_{L^{r}(Q_{n})}=\|Y\|_{L^{(p+k-\ell-1)r}(Q_{n})}^{p+k-\ell-1}\leq M_{t}(Q_{n})^{\frac{p+k-\ell-1}{p+k-1}}

(3.22)

for any

\ell=1,\dots,k
.
On the other hand, by Hölder’s inequality,
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\nabla(Y^{p+k-\ell-1})\|_{L^{r}(Q_{n})}&\sim\|\nabla Y\cdot|Y|^{\frac{p-2}{2}}Y^{\frac{p}{2}+k-\ell-1}\|_{L^{r}(Q_{n})}\\
&\leq M(Q_{n})^{\frac{1}{2}}\|Y^{\frac{p}{2}+k-\ell-1}\|_{L^{\frac{2r}{2-r}}(Q_{n})}\\
&\leq M(Q_{n})^{\frac{1}{2}}\big\||Y|^{\frac{p}{2}}\big\|_{L^{q}(Q_{n})}^{1+\frac{2}{p}(k-\ell-1)},\end{split}

(3.23)

where

q=\frac{2}{p}\cdot\frac{2r}{2-r}\big(\frac{p}{2}+k-\ell-1\big)
.
By Sobolev’s inequality,
20
20

20

When
\ell=k
, we have
q\leq 2
for
r>1

sufficiently close to
1
, and thus there is no need to apply Sobolev’s inequality.
the interpolation (
2.2
),
and Young’s inequality followed by Hölder’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\big\||Y|^{\frac{p}{2}}\big\|_{L^{q}(Q_{n})}&\lesssim\big\||Y|^{\frac{p}{2}}\big\|_{L^{2}(Q_{n})}+\big\||Y|^{\frac{p-2}{2}}\nabla Y\big\|_{L^{2}(Q_{n})}\\
&\leq M_{t}(Q_{n})^{\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{p}{p+k-1}}+M_{t}(Q_{n})^{\frac{1}{2}}.\end{split}

(3.24)

Hence, from (
3.21
), (
3.22
), (
3.23
), and (
3.24
),
we obtain

\displaystyle\|Y^{p+k-\ell-1}\|_{W^{3\varepsilon,r}(Q_{n})}\lesssim M_{t}(Q_{n})^{1-\theta}+1

(3.25)

for some
\theta>0
, provided that
\varepsilon>0
is sufficiently small,
where the implicit constant is independent of
\ell=1,\dots,k
.

Hence, putting
(
3.16
), (
3.17
),
(
3.18
), (
3.19
),
and (
3.25
)
together
and applying Young’s inequality with (
2.26
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}|B_{\ell}|&\lesssim\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}w_{\mu}(n)\Big(M_{t}(Q_{n})^{1-\theta}+1\Big)\|Z^{(\ell)}\|_{W^{-\varepsilon,r^{\prime}}(Q_{n})}\\
&\leq\varepsilon_{0}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}w_{\mu}(n)M_{t}(Q_{n})+C_{\varepsilon_{0}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}w_{\mu}(n)\|Z^{(\ell)}\|_{W^{-\varepsilon,r^{\prime}}(Q_{n})}^{\frac{1}{\theta}}+C_{1}\\
&\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}M_{t}+C_{\varepsilon_{0}}\|Z^{(\ell)}\|_{B^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{2},\mu_{0}}_{p_{0},p_{0}}}^{p_{0}}+C_{2}\end{split}

(3.26)

for some small
\varepsilon_{0}>0
, finite
p_{0}\gg 1
,
and
\mu_{0}<\mu
.
Here, the last step follows from
(
3.13
), (
3.20
), and (
2.13
)
for the first term,
while it follows from
(
2.25
) and (
2.13
) for the second term.

Therefore, from
(
3.9
),
(
3.10
),
(
3.11
),
(
3.12
),
(
3.15
), and (
3.26
),
we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}\partial_{t}\|Y(t)\|_{L^{p}_{\mu}}^{p}+c_{0}M_{t}\lesssim\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\|Z^{(\ell)}(t)\|_{B^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{2},\mu_{0}}_{p_{0},p_{0}}}^{p_{0}}.

Finally, in view of
(
3.14
),
there exists
\lambda>1
such that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \frac{1}{p}\partial_{t}\|Y(t)\|_{L^{p}_{\mu}}^{p}+c_{1}\|Y(t)\|_{L^{p}_{\mu}}^{\lambda p}\lesssim\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\|Z^{(\ell)}(t)\|_{B^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{2},\mu_{0}}_{p_{0},p_{0}}}^{p_{0}}\end{aligned}

(3.27)

for any
0\leq t\leq T
.
Finally, the desired bound (
3.6
) follows
from (
3.27
) and Lemma
3.2
.
This concludes the proof of Proposition
3.1
.
∎

3.2.
Coming down from infinity on weighted Sobolev spaces
of positive regularities

In this subsection, we establish the following coming down from infinity
for a solution
Y
to (
3.5
) in a weighted Sobolev space
of positive regularity.

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
k\in 2\mathbb{N}+1
and
T>0
.
Let
Y
be a solution to (
3.5
)
on the time interval
[0,T]
.
Then,
given any

0<s<1
,
finite
p\geq 1
, and
\mu>0
,
there exist
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}>1
, small
\theta>0
, finite
q_{0},q\gg 1
,
and
0<\mu^{\prime},\mu_{1}<\mu
such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|Y(t)\|_{W^{s,p}_{\mu}}&\leq C(T)\bigg\{t^{-\frac{s}{2}}\Big(t^{-\frac{1}{(\lambda^{\prime}-1)p}}\vee C_{\bar{Z},p,\mu^{\prime}}(t)\Big)\\
&\hphantom{lXXXX}+t^{-\frac{1}{\lambda-1}}\vee\big(C_{\bar{Z},q_{0},\mu}(t)\big)^{q_{0}}+Q_{\bar{Z},\theta,q,\mu_{1}}(t)+1\bigg\}\end{split}

(3.28)

for
0<t\leq T
,
where the constant
C(T)
is independent of the initial condition
X_{0}

in (
3.5
).
Here,
C_{\bar{Z},p,\mu}
is as in (
3.7
)
and

\displaystyle Q_{\bar{Z},\theta,q,\mu_{1}}(t)=\sup_{0\leq t^{\prime}\leq t}\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\|Z^{(\ell)}(t^{\prime})\|_{B^{-\frac{\theta}{2},\mu_{1}}_{q,q}}^{q}.

(3.29)

The proof of Proposition
3.3

is based on a Gronwall-type argument,
utilizing the already established coming down from infinity
in weighted Lebesgue spaces
(Proposition
3.1
).
While the idea is straightforward,
an actual implementation requires
careful analysis via a spatial decomposition
(analogous to the proof of Lemma
2.5
),
for which we find our definition (
2.22
)
of the weighted Sobolev spaces
W^{s,p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
more convenient
(than
\mathbf{W}^{s,p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
defined in (
2.31
)).

Proof.

Fix
0<r<t
.
Then, from
(
3.5
) and
(
2.23
),
we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\|Y(t)\|_{W^{s,p}_{\mu}}\leq\|P(t-r)Y(r)\|_{W^{s,p}_{\mu}}+\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\int_{r}^{t}\|P(t-t^{\prime})(Z^{(\ell)}Y^{k-\ell})(t^{\prime})\|_{W^{s,p}_{\mu}}dt^{\prime}\\
&\leq\|P(t-r)Y(r)\|_{W^{s,p}_{\mu}}+\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{\mu}{p}2^{j\delta}}\int_{r}^{t}\|\phi_{j}P(t-t^{\prime})(Z^{(\ell)}Y^{k-\ell})(t^{\prime})\|_{W^{s,p}}dt^{\prime}.\end{aligned}

(3.30)

Let
F_{k.\ell}=Z^{(\ell)}Y^{k-\ell}
.
We proceed as in the proof of Lemma
2.5

and estimate

\|\phi_{j}P(t-t^{\prime})F_{k,\ell}\|_{W^{s,p}}.

With (
2.35
), the fractional Leibniz rule (Lemma
2.8
(i)),
Young’s inequality, and (
2.37
),
we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|\phi_{j}P(t)F_{k,\ell}\|_{W^{s,p}}&\lesssim e^{-t}\sum_{(m,m^{\prime})\in\Lambda_{t,j}}\big\|\phi_{j}\big[(\phi^{t}_{m}p_{t})\ast(\phi_{m^{\prime}}F_{k,\ell})\big]\big\|_{W^{s,p}}\\
&\lesssim e^{-t}\sum_{(m,m^{\prime})\in\Lambda_{t,j}}\|\phi_{j}\|_{W^{s,\infty}}\big\|(\phi^{t}_{m}p_{t})\ast(\phi_{m^{\prime}}F_{k,\ell})\big\|_{W^{s,p}}\\
&\lesssim e^{-t}\sum_{(m,m^{\prime})\in\Lambda_{t,j}}\|\phi^{t}_{m}p_{t}\|_{W^{s+\theta,1}}\|\phi_{m^{\prime}}F_{k,\ell}\|_{W^{-\theta,p}}\\
&\lesssim t^{-\frac{s+\theta}{2}}e^{-t}\sum_{(m,m^{\prime})\in\Lambda_{t,j}}e^{-c_{1}4^{m}}\|\phi_{m^{\prime}}F_{k,\ell}\|_{W^{-\theta,p}}\end{aligned}

(3.31)

for any
\theta>0
, where
\phi^{t}_{m}
and

\Lambda_{t,j}
are as in (
2.34
) and (
2.36
).
In the following, we choose
\theta>0
sufficiently small
such that
s+\theta<1
.

From
(
2.21
),
Lemma
2.8
(ii) (with finite
q\gg 1
),
the fractional Leibniz rule (Lemma
2.8
(i) with
q_{0}\gg 1
),
the interpolation (
2.2
),
and Hölder’s inequality with (
2.18
),
we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|\phi_{m^{\prime}}F_{k,\ell}\|_{W^{-\theta,p}}&=\|(\widetilde{\phi}_{m^{\prime}}Y)^{k-\ell}\cdot\phi_{m^{\prime}}Z^{(\ell)}\|_{W^{-\theta,p}}\\
&\lesssim\|(\widetilde{\phi}_{m^{\prime}}Y)^{k-\ell}\|_{W^{\theta,p-}}\|\phi_{m^{\prime}}Z^{(\ell)}\|_{W^{-\theta,q}}\\
&\lesssim\|\widetilde{\phi}_{m^{\prime}}Y\|_{L^{q_{0}}}^{k-\ell-1}\|\widetilde{\phi}_{m^{\prime}}Y\|_{W^{\theta,p}}\|\phi_{m^{\prime}}Z^{(\ell)}\|_{W^{-\theta,q}}\\
&\lesssim\|\widetilde{\phi}_{m^{\prime}}Y\|_{L^{q_{0}}}^{k-\ell-1}\|\widetilde{\phi}_{m^{\prime}}Y\|_{W^{s,p}}^{\frac{\theta}{s}}\|\widetilde{\phi}_{m^{\prime}}Y\|_{L^{p}}^{1-\frac{\theta}{s}}\|\phi_{m^{\prime}}Z^{(\ell)}\|_{W^{-\theta,q}}\\
&\lesssim\|\widetilde{\phi}_{m^{\prime}}Y\|_{L^{q_{0}}}^{k-\ell-1}\|\widetilde{\phi}_{m^{\prime}}Y\|_{W^{s,p}}^{\frac{\theta}{s}}\\
&\quad\times 2^{(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{q_{0}})(1-\frac{\theta}{s})2m^{\prime}}\|\widetilde{\phi}_{m^{\prime}}Y\|_{L^{q_{0}}}^{1-\frac{\theta}{s}}\|\phi_{m^{\prime}}Z^{(\ell)}\|_{W^{-\theta,q}},\end{aligned}

(3.32)

provided that
0<\theta\leq s
.
Then, it follows from (
3.32
) with
(
2.23
) (for
\widetilde{\phi}_{m^{\prime}}
defined in (
2.20
))
that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|\phi_{m^{\prime}}F_{k,\ell}\|_{W^{-\theta,p}}&\lesssim 2^{(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{q_{0}})(1-\frac{\theta}{s})2m^{\prime}}e^{\frac{\mu}{q_{0}}a_{1}(k-\ell-\frac{\theta}{s})2^{(m^{\prime}+1)\delta}}\|Y\|_{L^{q_{0}}_{\mu}}^{k-\ell-\frac{\theta}{s}}\\
&\quad\times e^{\frac{\mu}{p}\frac{\theta}{s}2^{(m^{\prime}+1)\delta}}\|Y\|_{W^{s,p}_{\mu}}^{\frac{\theta}{s}}\,e^{\frac{\mu}{q}2^{m^{\prime}\delta}}\|Z^{(\ell)}\|_{W^{-\theta,q}_{\mu}}.\end{aligned}

(3.33)

Note that, given any small
\kappa_{0}>0
,
there exists
C(p,q_{0},s,\theta,\kappa_{0})>0

such that

\displaystyle 2^{(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{q_{0}})(1-\frac{\theta}{s})2m^{\prime}}\leq C(p,q_{0},s,\theta,\kappa_{0})e^{\kappa_{0}2^{m^{\prime}\delta}}

(3.34)

for any
m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
Hence, from
(
3.31
) and (
3.33
) with (
3.34
),
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{\mu}{p}2^{j\delta}}\|\phi_{j}P(t)F_{k,\ell}\|_{W^{s,p}}

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim t^{-\frac{s+\theta}{2}}e^{-t}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{\mu}{p}2^{j\delta}}\sum_{(m,m^{\prime})\in\Lambda_{t,j}}e^{-c_{1}4^{m}}e^{\widetilde{\mu}2^{m^{\prime}\delta}}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\|Y\|_{L^{q_{0}}_{\mu}}^{k-\ell-\frac{\theta}{s}}\|Y\|_{W^{s,p}_{\mu}}^{\frac{\theta}{s}}\|Z^{(\ell)}\|_{W^{-\theta,q}_{\mu}},

where
\widetilde{\mu}
is given by

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mu}=\frac{\mu}{q_{0}}a_{1}\bigg(k-\ell-\frac{\theta}{s}\bigg)2^{\delta}+\frac{\mu}{p}\frac{\theta}{s}2^{\delta}+\frac{\mu}{q}+\kappa_{0}.

By choosing sufficiently large
q_{0},q\gg 1
,
0<\theta\ll s
,
and sufficiently small
\kappa_{0}>0
,
we have
p\widetilde{\mu}\ll\mu
.
Then, by summing over
m^{\prime}
with
(
2.36
) (as in the proof of Lemma
2.5
),
then summing over
m
(with (
2.40
); see also (
2.41
)),
and finally summing over
j
with
p\widetilde{\mu}\ll\mu
,
we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{\mu}{p}2^{j\delta}}\|\phi_{j}P(t)F_{k,\ell}\|_{W^{s,p}}\\
&\quad\lesssim t^{-\frac{s+\theta}{2}}e^{-t}\sum_{j,m=0}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{\mu}{p}2^{j\delta}}e^{-c_{1}4^{m}}e^{c_{0}\widetilde{\mu}(2^{j\delta}+2^{m\delta}t^{\frac{\delta}{2}})}\\
&\quad\quad\times\|Y\|_{L^{q_{0}}_{\mu}}^{k-\ell-\frac{\theta}{s}}\|Y\|_{W^{s,p}_{\mu}}^{\frac{\theta}{s}}\|Z^{(\ell)}\|_{W^{-\theta,q}_{\mu}}\\
&\quad\lesssim t^{-\frac{s+\theta}{2}}e^{-ct}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}e^{-(\frac{\mu}{p}-c_{0}\widetilde{\mu})2^{j\delta}}\|Y\|_{L^{q_{0}}_{\mu}}^{k-\ell-\frac{\theta}{s}}\|Y\|_{W^{s,p}_{\mu}}^{\frac{\theta}{s}}\|Z^{(\ell)}\|_{W^{-\theta,q}_{\mu}}\\
&\quad\lesssim t^{-\frac{s+\theta}{2}}e^{-ct}\|Y\|_{L^{q_{0}}_{\mu}}^{k-\ell-\frac{\theta}{s}}\|Y\|_{W^{s,p}_{\mu}}^{\frac{\theta}{s}}\|Z^{(\ell)}\|_{W^{-\theta,q}_{\mu}}\end{split}

(3.35)

for any
t>0
.

Therefore, from (
3.30
), (
3.35
),
Young’s inequality, and Lemma
2.3

(with some
0<\mu_{1}<\mu
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|Y(t)\|_{W^{s,p}_{\mu}}&\lesssim\|P(t-r)Y(r)\|_{W^{s,p}_{\mu}}\\
&\quad+\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\int_{r}^{t}(t-t^{\prime})^{-\frac{s+\theta}{2}}\|Y(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{q_{0}}_{\mu}}^{k-\ell-\frac{\theta}{s}}\|Y(t^{\prime})\|_{W^{s,p}_{\mu}}^{\frac{\theta}{s}}\|Z^{(\ell)}(t^{\prime})\|_{W^{-\theta,q}_{\mu}}dt^{\prime}\\
&\lesssim\|P(t-r)Y(r)\|_{W^{s,p}_{\mu}}+\int_{r}^{t}(t-t^{\prime})^{-\frac{s+\theta}{2}}\|Y(t^{\prime})\|_{W^{s,p}_{\mu}}dt^{\prime}\\
&\quad+\int_{r}^{t}(t-t^{\prime})^{-\frac{s+\theta}{2}}\bigg(\|Y(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{q_{0}}_{\mu}}^{q_{0}}+\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\|Z^{(\ell)}(t^{\prime})\|_{B^{-\frac{\theta}{2},\mu_{1}}_{q,q}}^{q}+1\bigg)dt^{\prime}.\end{split}

(3.36)

From Proposition
3.1

with (
3.7
),
there exists
\lambda=\lambda(q_{0},\mu)>1
such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|Y(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{q_{0}}_{\mu}}&\lesssim(t^{\prime})^{-\frac{1}{(\lambda-1)q_{0}}}\vee C_{\bar{Z},q_{0},\mu}(t^{\prime})\\
&\leq r^{-\frac{1}{(\lambda-1)q_{0}}}\vee C_{\bar{Z},q_{0},\mu}(t)\end{split}

(3.37)

for
r\leq t^{\prime}\leq t
.
Then, from (
3.36
),
Lemma
2.5
, (
3.37
), and (
3.29
), we
have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|Y(t)\|_{W^{s,p}_{\mu}}&\lesssim(t-r)^{-\frac{s}{2}}\|Y(r)\|_{L^{p}_{\mu^{\prime}}}\\
&\quad+C(T)\Big(r^{-\frac{1}{\lambda-1}}\vee\big(C_{\bar{Z},q_{0},\mu}(t)\big)^{q_{0}}+Q_{\bar{Z},\theta,q,\mu_{1}}(t)+1\Big)\\
&\quad+\int_{r}^{t}(t-t^{\prime})^{-\frac{s+\theta}{2}}\|Y(t^{\prime})\|_{W^{s,p}_{\mu}}dt^{\prime}.\end{split}

(3.38)

Let

\displaystyle\begin{split}B(t,r)&=(t-r)^{-\frac{s}{2}-}\|Y(r)\|_{L^{p}_{\mu^{\prime}}}\\
&\quad+C(T)\Big(r^{-\frac{1}{\lambda-1}}\vee\big(C_{\bar{Z},q_{0},\mu}(t)\big)^{q_{0}}+Q_{\bar{Z},\theta,q,\mu_{1}}(t)+1\Big).\end{split}

(3.39)

Then, recalling that
s+\theta<1
,
it follows from (
3.38
) and Cauchy-Schwarz’s inequality that

\displaystyle\|Y(t)\|_{W^{s,p}_{\mu}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq CB(t,r)^{2}+C(T)\int_{r}^{t}\|Y(t^{\prime})\|_{W^{s,p}_{\mu}}^{2}dt^{\prime}.

Hence, by Gronwall’s inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle\|Y(t)\|_{W^{s,p}_{\mu}}\leq C(T)B(t,r)

(3.40)

for any
r\leq t\leq T
.
Finally, by choosing
r=\frac{t}{2}
,
it follows from (
3.40
),
(
3.39
) and Proposition
3.1
that

\displaystyle\|Y(t)\|_{W^{s,p}_{\mu}}

\displaystyle\leq C(T)\bigg\{t^{-\frac{s}{2}}\Big(t^{-\frac{1}{(\lambda^{\prime}-1)p}}\vee C_{\bar{Z},p,\mu^{\prime}}(t)\Big)

\displaystyle\hphantom{lXXXX}+t^{-\frac{1}{\lambda-1}}\vee\big(C_{\bar{Z},q_{0},\mu}(t)\big)^{q_{0}}+Q_{\bar{Z},\theta,q,\mu_{1}}(t)+1\bigg\}

for some
\lambda^{\prime}=\lambda(p,\mu^{\prime})>1
.
This concludes the proof of Proposition
3.3
.
∎

3.3.
Construction of the Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}_{\infty}
on the plane

We conclude this section by presenting the proof of Theorem
1.2
(i).

Let
X
be a global-in-time solution to (
3.1
) on
\mathbb{R}^{2}

constructed in
[
55
]
,
satisfying the decomposition
X=Y+Z
in (
3.3
)
with
Y
and
Z
satisfying (
3.4
)
and (
3.2
), respectively.
Fix
s<0
, finite
p\geq 1
, and
\mu>0
.
Then, by Lemma
2.3
,
(
2.32
) with
s=0
, and Proposition
3.1
,
we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|X(t)\|_{W^{s,p}_{\mu}}&\lesssim\|Z(t)\|_{W^{s,p}_{\mu}}+\|Y(t)\|_{L^{p}_{\mu^{\prime}}}\\
&\lesssim\|Z(t)\|_{B^{s+\varepsilon,\mu^{\prime\prime}}_{p,p}}+\bigg\{t^{-\frac{1}{(\lambda-1)p}}\vee\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\|:\!Z^{\ell}(t)\!:\|_{B^{-\varepsilon,\mu_{0}}_{p_{0},p_{0}}}^{\frac{p_{0}}{\lambda p}}\bigg\}\end{aligned}

(3.41)

for a suitable choice of parameters
\mu^{\prime}
,
\mu^{\prime\prime}
,
\lambda
,
p_{0}
,
\varepsilon
, and
\mu_{0}
,
depending on
p
and
\mu
,
where
the implicit constant is independent of the initial data
X_{0}
.

Given
L\geq 1
,
let

\xi_{L}
denote the (spatially)
L
-periodic space-time white noise
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}

obtained by first restricting the space-time white noise
\xi
on

\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
, appearing in (
3.1
),
onto
\big[-\frac{L}{2},\frac{L}{2}\big]^{2}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}

and then extending it periodically (with the spatial period
L
)
onto
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
See Section 5 in
[
55
]

for a further discussion.
See also (
4.4
) and (
4.5
) below.
We now consider the following SNLH
with the
L
-periodic space-time white noise:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}X_{L}+(1-\Delta)X_{L}+:\!X_{L}^{k}\!:\,=\sqrt{2}\xi_{L}\\
X_{L}|_{t=0}=X_{0,L},\end{cases}

(3.42)

where the initial data
X_{0,L}
is assumed to be
L
-periodic.
This is
the parabolic
\Phi^{k+1}_{2}
-model on
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}

studied in
[
22
]
(with
L=1
).
Write
X_{L}
as

\displaystyle X_{L}=Y_{L}+Z_{L},

(3.43)

where

Z_{L}
satisfies

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}Z_{L}+(1-\Delta)Z_{L}=\sqrt{2}\xi_{L}\\
Z_{L}|_{t=0}=0\end{cases}

(3.44)

and
Y_{L}
satisfies

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}Y_{L}+(1-\Delta)Y_{L}+\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}{k\choose\ell}:\!Z_{L}^{\ell}\!:\,Y_{L}^{k-\ell}=0\\
Y_{L}|_{t=0}=X_{0,L}.\end{cases}

(3.45)

Then, by applying Lemma
2.3
and Proposition
3.1

once again
we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|X_{L}(t)\|_{W^{s,p}_{\mu}}&\lesssim\|Z_{L}(t)\|_{W^{s,p}_{\mu}}+\|Y_{L}(t)\|_{L^{p}_{\mu^{\prime}}}\\
&\lesssim\|Z_{L}(t)\|_{B^{s+\varepsilon,\mu^{\prime\prime}}_{p,p}}+\bigg\{t^{-\frac{1}{(\lambda-1)p}}\vee\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\|:\!Z_{L}^{\ell}(t)\!:\|_{B^{-\varepsilon,\mu_{0}}_{p_{0},p_{0}}}^{\frac{p_{0}}{\lambda p}}\bigg\}\end{aligned}

(3.46)

where
the implicit constant is independent of the initial data
X_{0,L}

and of the period
L\geq 1
.
Moreover, it follows from

[
55
, Theorem 5.1]
21
21

21

Theorem 5.4 in the arXiv version.

that the
p
th moment of the right-hand side of (
3.46
) (and of (
3.41
))
is bounded, uniformly in
L\geq 1
.

Let
\rho_{L}
denote the
L
-periodic
\Phi^{k+1}_{2}
-measure on
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}
,
appearing in (
1.21
).
By taking
\Law(X_{0,L})=\rho_{L}
in (
3.42
)
(and we assume that
X_{0,L}
is independent of the noise
\xi_{L}
),
it is known

[
22
]
that

\displaystyle\Law(X_{L}(t))=\rho_{L}

(3.47)

for any
t\geq 0
.
Moreover, from (
3.46
)
and the observation mentioned right after (
3.46
), we have

\displaystyle\sup_{L\geq 1}\mathbb{E}\Big[\|X_{L}(1)\|_{W^{s,p}_{\mu}}^{p}\Big]\lesssim 1.

(3.48)

Now, given
s<0
and
\mu>0
,
let
s<s^{\prime}<0
and
0<\mu^{\prime}\ll\mu
.
Then, given
M>0
, set

\displaystyle K_{M}=\big\{f\in W^{s,p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2}):\|f\|_{W^{s^{\prime},p}_{\mu^{\prime}}}\leq M\big\}.

Then, it follows from Chebyshev’s inequality
and (
3.48
)
that, given
\varepsilon>0
,
there exists
M>0
such that

\displaystyle\rho_{L}\big(K_{M}^{c}\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\Big(\|X_{L}(1;\omega)\|_{W^{s^{\prime},p}_{\mu^{\prime}}}>M\Big)\lesssim M^{-p}<\varepsilon,

uniformly in
L\geq 1
.
On the other hand,
it follows from Lemma
2.2

that

K_{M}
is compact in
W^{s,p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.
Therefore, from
the Prokhorov theorem (Lemma
2.18
), we conclude that
\{\rho_{L}\}_{L\in\mathbb{N}}

is tight
and hence admits a
subsequence
\{\rho_{L_{j}}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}

which converges weakly to
a limiting
\Phi^{k+1}_{2}
-measure
\rho_{\infty}
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}

as
j\to\infty
.

Next, we prove weak convergence of the
L
-periodic white noise measure
\mu_{0,L}

defined in (
1.11
) (with
s=0
)
to the limiting white noise measure on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Formally,
a (spatial) white noise
\zeta
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}

is a centered Gaussian distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{2}

with covariance

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\zeta(x_{1})\zeta(x_{2})\big]=\delta(x_{1}-x_{2}).

(3.49)

The expression (
3.49
) is merely formal
but we can make it rigorous by testing it against a test function.

Definition 3.4
.

A (spatial) white noise
\zeta
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}

is a family of centered Gaussian random variables

\{\zeta(\varphi):\varphi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\}

such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\zeta(\varphi)^{2}\big]=\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{E}\big[\zeta(\varphi_{1})\zeta(\varphi_{2})\big]=\langle\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Given
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\eta_{k}
be as in (
2.9
).
Then, a direct computation with Lemma
2.12
shows

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[|\zeta(\eta_{k})|^{p}\big]\lesssim_{p}\Big(\mathbb{E}\big[\zeta(\eta_{k})^{2}\big]\Big)^{\frac{p}{2}}=\|\eta_{k}\|_{L^{2}}^{p}\sim 2^{kp}

(3.50)

for any finite
p\geq 1
.
Hence, we conclude from
(the time-independent version of) Lemma 9 in
[
55
]
22
22

22

Lemma 5.2 in the arXiv version.

that the white noise

\zeta
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
belongs almost surely to

B^{s,\mu}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for any
s<-1
,
\mu>0
,
1\leq p<\infty
, and
1\leq q\leq\infty
,
with the following bound:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\|\zeta\|_{B^{s,\mu}_{p,q}}^{r}\big]\leq C(r,s,\mu,p,q)<\infty

(3.51)

for any finite
r\geq 1
.
Then, by Lemma
2.3
,
we see that
the white noise

\zeta
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
also belongs almost surely to
the weighted Sobolev space
W^{s,p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for any
s<-1
,
1\leq p<\infty
,
and
\mu>0
,
with a bound:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\|\zeta\|_{W^{s,p}_{\mu}}^{r}\big]\leq C(r,s,\mu,p)<\infty

(3.52)

for any finite
r\geq 1
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
\zeta
be a white noise on
\mathbb{R}^{2}

as in Definition
3.4

and set
\mu_{0,\infty}=\Law(\zeta)
.
Then, given any
s<-1
, finite
p\geq 1
, and
\mu>0
,
by viewing the
L
-periodic white noise measure
\mu_{0,L}

defined in (
1.11
) with
s=0

as a measure on
W^{s,p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
the sequence
\{\mu_{0,L}\}_{L\in\mathbb{N}}

converges weakly
to
\mu_{0,\infty}

as
L\to\infty
.

By putting the weak convergence of a subsequence
\{\rho_{L_{j}}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}

with Lemma
3.5
,
we conclude that
as probability measures on
\vec{W}^{s,p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})=W^{s,p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times W^{s-1,p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

with
s<0
, finite
p\geq 1
, and
\mu>0
,
the
L_{j}
-periodic Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}_{L_{j}}=\rho_{L_{j}}\otimes\mu_{0,L_{j}}

in (
1.20
)
converges weakly to a limiting
Gibbs measure

\vec{\rho}_{\infty}=\rho_{\infty}\otimes\mu_{0,\infty}

on
\mathbb{R}^{2}

as
j\to\infty
.
This proves Theorem
1.2
(i).

Remark 3.6
.

In view of the embedding
between weighted Sobolev spaces and weighted Besov spaces
stated in Lemma
2.3
,
the weak convergence of
the
L_{j}
-periodic Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}_{L_{j}}
to
the limiting Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}_{\infty}

also holds
as probability measures
on the weighted Besov space
B^{s,\mu}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times B^{s-1,\mu}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

for any
s<0
, finite
p\geq 1
,
1\leq q\leq\infty
,
and
\mu>0
.

We conclude this section by presenting the proof of Lemma
3.5
.

Proof of Lemma
3.5
.

As we have seen in Section
1
,
an
L
-periodic white noise on
the dilated torus
\mathbb{T}_{L}^{2}
is given by
the second Gaussian Fourier series for
v_{L}
in (
1.14
)
such that
\Law(v_{L})=\mu_{0,L}
.
While it is possible to work with
v_{L}
in (
1.14
),
by viewing it as an
L
-periodic distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
and show that
the
L
-periodic white noise measure
\mu_{0,L}=\Law(v_{L})

converges weakly to the white noise measure
\mu_{0,\infty}=\Law(\zeta)
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
it is more convenient to work with
the
L
-periodized version
\zeta_{L}
of the white noise
\zeta
.
Define
\zeta_{L}
on
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}
by setting

\displaystyle\zeta_{L}=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{L}}\widehat{\zeta}_{L}(\lambda)e_{\lambda}^{L},

(3.53)

where
e_{\lambda}^{L}
is as in (
1.10
)
and
\widehat{\zeta}_{L}(\lambda)
is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widehat{\zeta}_{L}(\lambda)&=\int_{[-\frac{L}{2},\frac{L}{2})^{2}}\zeta(x)\overline{e_{\lambda}^{L}(x)}dx=\zeta\Big(\mathbf{1}_{[-\frac{L}{2},\frac{L}{2})^{2}}\overline{e_{\lambda}^{L}}\Big)\\
:\!&=\zeta\Big(\mathbf{1}_{[-\frac{L}{2},\frac{L}{2})^{2}}\Re(e_{\lambda}^{L})\Big)-i\zeta\Big(\mathbf{1}_{[-\frac{L}{2},\frac{L}{2})^{2}}\Im(e_{\lambda}^{L})\Big).\end{split}

(3.54)

Then, by viewing
\zeta_{L}
as an
L
-periodic distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
we see that
\Law(\zeta_{L})=\mu_{0,L}
.
Indeed, it follows from Definition
3.4

and the orthonormality of
\{e_{\lambda}^{L}\}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{L}}

on
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}=\big[-\frac{L}{2},\frac{L}{2}\big)^{2}

that
\{\widehat{\zeta}_{L}(\lambda)\}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{L}}

forms
a family of independent standard complex-valued Gaussian random variables conditioned that

\widehat{\zeta}_{L}(-\lambda)=\overline{\widehat{\zeta}_{L}(\lambda)}
,

\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{L}
.
Hence, from (
1.14
) and (
3.53
),
we conclude that
\Law(\zeta_{L})=\Law(v_{L})=\mu_{0,L}
.
Before proceeding further, we point out that
by repeating the computation (
3.50
)
for
\zeta_{L}
,
we see that the bounds (
3.51
) and (
3.52
)
also hold for
\zeta_{L}
, uniformly in
L\geq 1
.

By definition,
\zeta_{L}
agrees with
\zeta
on
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}=\big[-\frac{L}{2},\frac{L}{2}\big)^{2}
.
Now, fix
s<-1
,
1\leq p<\infty
, and
\mu>0
.
Then, given finite
r\geq 1
, it follows from (
3.52
)
for
\zeta
and
\zeta_{L}
that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\Big[\|\zeta-\zeta_{L}\|_{W^{s,p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{p}\big]&=\mathbb{E}\Big[\|\zeta-\zeta_{L}\|_{W^{s,p}_{\mu}((\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L})^{c})}^{p}\big]\\
&\leq C(r,s,\mu^{\prime},p)\cdot w_{\mu-\mu^{\prime}}(c_{0}L)\end{split}

(3.55)

for any
0<\mu^{\prime}<\mu
,
where
c_{0}>0
is an absolute constant independent of
L\geq 1

(and the other parameters).
Then, summing over
L\in\mathbb{N}
,
we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg(\sum_{L=1}^{\infty}\|\zeta-\zeta_{L}\|^{p}_{W^{s,p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{L=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\Big[\|\zeta-\zeta_{L}\|^{p}_{W^{s,p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\Big]

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{L=1}^{\infty}w_{\mu-\mu^{\prime}}(c_{0}L)<\infty,

since
\mu>\mu^{\prime}
.
This implies immediately that

\sum_{L=1}^{\infty}\|\zeta-\zeta_{L}\|^{p}_{W^{s,p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
is finite almost surely,
which in particular implies that

\zeta_{L}
converges almost surely to
\zeta
in
W^{s,p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
as
L\to\infty

(with
L\in\mathbb{N}
).
Therefore, we conclude that

\mu_{0,L}=\Law(\zeta_{L})
converges weakly to
\mu_{0,\infty}=\Law(\zeta)

as
L\to\infty
(with
L\in\mathbb{N}
).
This conclude the proof of Lemma
3.5
.
∎

4.
Global well-posedness and invariance
of the Gibbs measure

In this section, we present the proof of Theorem
1.2
(ii).
Our main goal is to construct global-in-time dynamics
for the hyperbolic
\Phi^{k+1}_{2}
-model on
\mathbb{R}^{2}

with the Gibbsian initial data:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}^{2}u+\partial_{t}u+(1-\Delta)u\,+:\!u^{k}\!:\,=\sqrt{2}\xi\\
(u,\partial_{t}u)|_{t=0}=(u_{0},u_{1})\quad\text{with }\Law(u_{0},u_{1})=\vec{\rho}_{\infty},\end{cases}

(4.1)

where
\vec{\rho}_{\infty}=\rho_{\infty}\otimes\mu_{0,\infty}
is the Gibbs measure on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, constructed
as a limit of the
L_{j}
-periodic Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}_{L_{j}}=\rho_{L_{j}}\otimes\mu_{0,L_{j}}

in the previous section.
For simplicity of notation,
we set

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}=\{L_{j}:j\in\mathbb{N}\}\subset\mathbb{N}

and only consider the values of
L\in\mathcal{A}

in the following, unless otherwise specified.

Consider the
L
-periodic hyperbolic
\Phi^{k+1}_{2}
-model on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}^{2}u_{L}+\partial_{t}u_{L}+(1-\Delta)u_{L}\,+:\!u_{L}^{k}\!:\,=\sqrt{2}\xi_{L}\\
(u_{L},\partial_{t}u_{L})|_{t=0}=(u_{0,L},u_{1,L})\quad\text{with }\Law(u_{0,L},u_{1,L})=\vec{\rho}_{L},\end{cases}

(4.2)

where
\xi_{L}
is an
L
-periodic space-time white noise
(see (
4.4
) and (
4.5
) below) and
\vec{\rho}_{L}=\rho_{L}\otimes\mu_{0,L}

is the
L
-periodic Gibbs measure in (
1.20
); see also Remark
1.1
.
As mentioned in Section
1
,
the Cauchy problem
(
4.2
) is globally well-posed
and the Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}_{L}
is invariant under the resulting dynamics.
In the following, we
construct
a solution to (
4.1
)
as a limit of the solutions
u_{L}
to (
4.2
),
L\in\mathcal{A}
.

Let us first introduce some notations.
Let
\Psi
be the solution to the following linear stochastic damped wave equation:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}^{2}\Psi+\partial_{t}\Psi+(1-\Delta)\Psi=\sqrt{2}\xi\\
(\Psi,\partial_{t}\Psi)|_{t=0}=(0,0).\end{cases}

(4.3)

With
\mathcal{D}(t)
as in (
1.23
), we formally have

\displaystyle\Psi(t)=

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{D}(t-t^{\prime})\xi(dt^{\prime}).

Let
L\geq 1
.
Given a spatial white noise
\zeta
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
we let
\zeta_{L}
denote the

L
-periodized version of
\zeta
defined in (
3.53
) and (
3.54
).
Similarly, let
\xi_{L}

be
the
L
-periodized version (in space) of the space-time white noise
\xi
.
Namely, we define
\xi_{L}
on
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{L}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\cong\big[-\frac{L}{2},\frac{L}{2}\big)^{2}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
by setting

\displaystyle\xi_{L}=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{L}}\widehat{\xi}_{L}(\lambda)e_{\lambda}^{L},

(4.4)

where
e_{\lambda}^{L}
is as in (
1.10
)
and
\widehat{\xi}_{L}(\lambda)
is
given by

\displaystyle\widehat{\xi}_{L}(\lambda)=\int_{[-\frac{L}{2},\frac{L}{2})^{2}}\xi(x)\overline{e_{\lambda}^{L}(x)}dx=\xi\Big(\mathbf{1}_{[-\frac{L}{2},\frac{L}{2})^{2}}\,\overline{e_{\lambda}^{L}}\Big)

(4.5)

with the equality understood in the sense of temporal distributions.
Alternatively,
\widehat{\xi}_{L}(\lambda)
is
given as the (distributional) time derivative of

\displaystyle\widehat{B}_{L}(\lambda,t)=\xi\Big(\mathbf{1}_{[0,t]}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{[-\frac{L}{2},\frac{L}{2})^{2}}(x)\,\overline{e_{\lambda}^{L}(x)}\Big),

where the right-hand side is the action of
\xi
on a function in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}_{+})
.
It is then easy to check that
\{\widehat{\xi}_{L}(\lambda)\}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{L}}
forms
a family of independent temporal white noises
conditioned that
\widehat{\xi}_{L}(-\lambda)=\overline{\widehat{\xi}_{L}(\lambda)}
,
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{L}
.
Compare this with (
1.24
).
By the
L
-periodic extension in space,
we then view
\xi_{L}
as a space-time distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

In the following,
we construct a solution
u
to (
4.1
)
on the cone
\mathbf{C}_{R}
defined in (
1.33
)
for each
R>0

as a limit of the solution
u_{L}
to (
4.2
).
Fix
R>0
.
Then, thanks to the finite speed of propagation,
we have

\displaystyle\zeta_{L}=\zeta\quad\text{and}\quad\xi_{L}=\xi\quad\text{on the cone $\mathbf{C}_{R}$},

(4.6)

provided that
L\geq 2R
.
In particular, on the cone
\mathbf{C}_{R}
, the equation (
4.2
)
agrees (in law) with

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}^{2}u_{L}+\partial_{t}u_{L}+(1-\Delta)u_{L}\,+:\!u_{L}^{k}\!:\,=\sqrt{2}\xi\\
(u_{L},\partial_{t}u_{L})|_{t=0}=(u_{0,L},u_{1})\quad\text{with }\Law(u_{0,L})=\rho_{L},\end{cases}

(4.7)

where
\xi
and
u_{1}
with
\Law(u_{1})=\mu_{0,\infty}

are as in (
4.1
).
Namely, the difference between (
4.1
)
and (
4.7
) appears only in
the first component of the initial data.
This motivates the following decomposition of a solution.

We write a solution
u
to (
4.1
) as

\displaystyle\begin{split}u(t)&=\Phi(t)+v(t)\\
:\!&=\mathcal{S}(t)u_{0}+\mathcal{D}(t)u_{1}+\Psi(t)+v(t),\end{split}

(4.8)

where
\mathcal{S}(t)
,
\mathcal{D}(t)
, and
\Psi(t)
are as in (
2.5
),
(
1.23
), and (
4.3
),
and
v
satisfies the following equation:

\displaystyle\begin{split}v(t)&=-\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{D}(t-t^{\prime}):\!u^{k}(t^{\prime})\!:dt^{\prime}\\
&=-\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}{k\choose\ell}\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{D}(t-t^{\prime})\big(\!:\!\Phi^{\ell}(t^{\prime})\!:v^{k-\ell}(t^{\prime})\big)dt^{\prime}.\end{split}

(4.9)

We also set

\displaystyle\Phi_{0}(t)=\Phi_{0}(u_{0})(t)=\mathcal{S}(t)u_{0}\quad\text{and}\quad\Phi_{1}(t)=\Phi_{1}(u_{1},\xi)(t)=\mathcal{D}(t)u_{1}+\Psi(t)

(4.10)

such that

\Phi(u_{0},u_{1},\xi)=\Phi_{0}(u_{0})+\Phi_{1}(u_{1},\xi).

Then, by the independence of
u_{0}
and
(u_{1},\xi)
in (
4.1
),
23
23

23

More precisely,
recall that the product of independent homogenous Wiener chaoses is a homogenous Wiener chaos
of the order given by the sum of the two.

we have

\displaystyle:\!\Phi^{\ell}(t)\!:\,\,=\,\,:\!\Phi^{\ell}(u_{0},u_{1},\xi)(t)\!:\,\,=\sum_{m=0}^{\ell}{\ell\choose m}:\!\Phi_{0}^{m}(u_{0})(t)\!:\,:\!\Phi_{1}^{\ell-m}(u_{1},\xi)(t)\!:\,,

and thus
we can rewrite (
4.9
) as

\displaystyle\begin{split}v(t)&=-\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\sum_{m=0}^{\ell}{k\choose\ell}{\ell\choose m}\\
&\hphantom{XXX}\times\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{D}(t-t^{\prime})\big(\!:\!\Phi_{0}^{m}(u_{0})(t^{\prime})\!:\,:\!\Phi_{1}^{\ell-m}(u_{1},\xi)(t^{\prime})\!:v^{k-\ell}(t^{\prime})\big)dt^{\prime}.\end{split}

(4.11)

In view of (
4.11
),
we define enhanced data sets
\Xi_{0}
and
\Xi_{1}

by setting

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Xi_{0}(\phi)&=\big(\Phi_{0}(\phi),:\!\Phi_{0}^{2}(\phi)\!:\,,\dots,\,:\!\Phi_{0}^{k}(\phi)\!:\big)\\
&=\big(\mathcal{S}(t)\phi,:\!(\mathcal{S}(t)\phi)^{2}\!:\,,\dots,\,:\!(\mathcal{S}(t)\phi)^{k}\!:\big)\end{split}

(4.12)

and

\displaystyle\Xi_{1}(u_{1},\xi)=\big(\Phi_{1},:\!\Phi_{1}^{2}\!:\,,\dots,\,:\!\Phi_{1}^{k}\!:\big),

(4.13)

where

\Phi_{1}=\Phi_{1}(u_{1},\xi)
is as in (
4.10
).

We now introduce distances / sizes by which we measure these enhanced data sets.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
k\in 2\mathbb{N}+1
.
Let
0<\varepsilon\ll 1
, finite
p\gg 1
, and
\mu>0

(to be chosen later).

(i)
We first introduce a space
\mathbb{W}

for the first enhanced data set
\Xi_{0}(\phi)

in (
4.12
)
by setting

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}=\big(C(\mathbb{R}_{+};W^{-\varepsilon,p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\big)^{\otimes k}.

We endow
\mathbb{W}
with the following bounded metric:

\displaystyle\begin{split}d_{\mathbb{W}}(f,g)&=d_{\mathbb{W}}\big((f_{1},\dots,f_{k}),(g_{1},\dots,g_{k})\big)\\
&=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{\ell}}\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{k}\|f_{j}-g_{j}\|_{C([0,2^{\ell}];W^{-\varepsilon,p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))}}{1+\sum_{j=1}^{k}\|f_{j}-g_{j}\|_{C([0,2^{\ell}];W^{-\varepsilon,p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))}},\end{split}

(4.14)

which induces the compact-open topology (in time)
on
\mathbb{W}
.
Then, the metric space

(\mathbb{W},d_{\mathbb{W}})
is a bounded Polish space.
24
24

24

Recall that
the space of continuous functions from
a separable metric space
X
to another separable metric space
Y

with the compact-open topology is separable; see
[
52
]
.
See also the paper
[
45
, Corollary 3.3]
.

Given
R>0
,
we also set

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}(R)=\big(L^{\infty}([0;R];W^{-\varepsilon,p}(B_{R}))\big)^{\otimes k}

(4.15)

with the norm given by

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathbb{W}(R)}=\|(f_{1},\dots,f_{k})\|_{\mathbb{W}(R)}=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\|f_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}([0;R];W^{-\varepsilon,p}(B_{R}))}.

(ii)
Let
\Xi_{0}=(\Xi_{01},\dots,\Xi_{0k})\in\mathbb{W}(R)
.
Given
R>0
, let
\|\Xi_{1}\|_{\Xi_{0},R}
denote the smallest constant
K_{1}\geq 1

such that the following inequality holds for
1\leq j,\ell\leq k

with
j+\ell\leq k
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{0}^{T}\big\|:\!\Phi_{1}^{j}(t)\!:\Xi_{0\ell}(t)\|_{W^{-2(k+1)\varepsilon,p}(B_{R})}^{p}dt\leq K_{1}\int_{0}^{T}\|\Xi_{0\ell}(t)\|_{W^{-\varepsilon,p}(B_{R})}^{p}dt\end{split}

(4.16)

for any
0<T\leq R
.

(iii) Let

\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}=(\widetilde{\Xi}_{01},\dots,\widetilde{\Xi}_{0k})\in\mathbb{W}(R)
.
Given
R>0
, let
K_{2},K_{3}\geq 1
be the smallest constants
such that the following inequalities hold
for
1\leq j,\ell\leq k

with
j+\ell\leq k
:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \int_{0}^{T}\big\|:\!\Phi_{1}^{j}(t)\!:\widetilde{\Xi}_{0\ell}(t)\|_{W^{-2(k+1)\varepsilon,p}(B_{R})}^{p}dt\leq K_{2}\int_{0}^{T}\|\widetilde{\Xi}_{0\ell}(t)\|_{W^{-\varepsilon,p}(B_{R})}^{p}dt\end{aligned}

(4.17)

and

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\int_{0}^{T}\big\|:\!\Phi_{1}^{j}(t)\!:(\,\Xi_{0\ell}(t)-\widetilde{\Xi}_{0\ell}(t))\|_{W^{-2(k+1)\varepsilon,p}(B_{R})}^{p}dt\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\leq K_{3}\int_{0}^{T}\|\Xi_{0\ell}(t)-\widetilde{\Xi}_{0\ell}(t)\|_{W^{-\varepsilon,p}(B_{R})}^{p}dt\end{aligned}

(4.18)

for any
0<T\leq R
.
Then, we set

\|\Xi_{1}\|_{\Xi_{0},\widetilde{\Xi}_{0},R}=\max(K_{1},K_{2},K_{3})
, where
K_{1}
is
as in Part (ii).

In the remaining part of this paper, we fix small
\varepsilon>0
, finite
p=p(\varepsilon)\gg 1
,
25
25

25

The condition

p=p(\varepsilon)\gg 1
is needed in Propositions
4.2
,
4.3
,
and
4.9
.

and
\mu>0

(to be chosen later),
and often drop dependence on these parameters.
For simplicity of notation, we set

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{k}=2(k+1)\varepsilon

(4.19)

in the remaining part of this section.
This
\varepsilon_{k}
comes from the condition
appearing in the proof of Proposition
4.9
(i).

4.1.
Local well-posedness and stability of SdNLW

In this subsection,
we study
local well-posedness and stability of SdNLW (
4.1
),
where we view

u_{0}
,
u_{1}
, and
\xi
in (
4.1
) as given deterministic spatial / space-time distributions
26
26

26

As
for
\xi
, we should really view
\Psi=\Psi(\xi)
as a given space-time distribution.

such that the enhanced data sets
\Xi_{0}(u_{0})
and
\Xi_{1}(u_{1},\xi)

in (
4.12
) and (
4.13
) make sense.
In order to emphasize the dependence on
u_{0}
,
u_{1}
, and
\xi
,
we may write
u=u(u_{0},u_{1},\xi)
.
By writing
u
as in (
4.8
),
we study the equation (
4.11
)
for the remainder term

\displaystyle\begin{split}v&=u-\mathcal{S}(t)u_{0}-\mathcal{D}(t)u_{1}-\Psi\\
&=u-\Phi_{0}(u_{0})-\Phi_{1}(u_{1},\xi).\end{split}

(4.20)

Given
\Xi_{0}=(\Xi_{01},\dots,\Xi_{0k})
,
we consider (
4.11
), starting from
t=t_{0}

with
(v,\partial_{t}v)|_{t=t_{0}}=(v_{0},v_{1})
,
where
we replace
:\!\Phi_{0}^{m}(u_{0})\!:
by
\Xi_{0m}
.:

\displaystyle\begin{split}v(t)&=\mathcal{S}(t-t_{0})v_{0}+\mathcal{D}(t-t_{0})v_{1}\\
&\quad-\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\sum_{m=0}^{\ell}{k\choose\ell}{\ell\choose m}\\
&\hphantom{XXX}\times\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\mathcal{D}(t-t^{\prime})\big(\Xi_{0m}(t^{\prime}):\!\Phi_{1}^{\ell}(u_{1},\xi)(t^{\prime})\!:v^{k-\ell}(t^{\prime})\big)dt^{\prime}.\end{split}

(4.21)

Given
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
\tau>0
, we set

\displaystyle X_{t_{0},R}^{s}(\tau)

\displaystyle=L^{\infty}([t_{0},\max(t_{0}+\tau,R)];H^{s}(B_{R-t})),

\displaystyle\vec{X}_{t_{0},R}^{s}(\tau)

\displaystyle=X_{t_{0},R}^{s}(\tau)\times X_{t_{0},R}^{s-1}(\tau).

Proposition 4.2

(local well-posedness)
.

Let

R>0
,
0\leq t_{0}<R
, and
(v_{0},v_{1})\in\vec{H}^{1-\varepsilon_{k}}(B_{R-t_{0}})
,
where
\varepsilon_{k}=2(k+1)\varepsilon
is as in (
4.19
).
Suppose that there exist
M_{0},M_{1}\geq 1
such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\Xi_{0}\|_{\mathbb{W}(R)}\leq M_{0}<\infty,\\
\|\Xi_{1}(u_{1},\xi)\|_{\Xi_{0},R}\leq M_{1}<\infty,\end{split}

(4.22)

where

\mathbb{W}(R)
is as in (
4.15
)
and

\|\Xi_{1}(u_{1},\xi)\|_{\Xi_{0},R}
is as in Definition
4.1
(ii).
Then, there exist small
\tau=\tau\big(\|(v_{0},v_{1})\|_{\vec{H}^{1-\varepsilon_{k}}(B_{R-t_{0}})},M_{0},M_{1}\big)>0

and a unique solution
v
to (
4.21
)
on
\mathbf{C}_{R}\cap\big\{t_{0}\leq t\leq\min(t_{0}+\tau,R)\big\}

such that

\displaystyle\|(v,\partial_{t}v)\|_{\vec{X}_{t_{0},R}^{1-\varepsilon_{k}}(\tau)}\leq C_{0}\|(v_{0},v_{1})\|_{\vec{H}^{1-\varepsilon_{k}}(B_{R-t_{0}})}

(4.23)

for some absolute constant
C_{0}>0
.

Proof.

The proof of Proposition
4.2

follows from a slight modification of Proposition 4.1 in
[
40
]

together with Definition
4.1
(ii).
Denote the right-hand side of (
4.21
) by

\Gamma(v)=\Gamma_{\Xi_{0},\Xi_{1}(u_{1},\xi)}(v)
,
and set

\vec{\Gamma}(v)=(\Gamma(v),\partial_{t}\Gamma(v))
.
Then, by the finite speed of propagation,
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Gamma(v)(t)&=\mathcal{S}(t-t_{0})v_{0}+\mathcal{D}(t-t_{0})v_{1}-\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\sum_{m=0}^{\ell}{k\choose\ell}{\ell\choose m}\\
&\hphantom{X}\times\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\mathcal{D}(t-t^{\prime})\big(\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{C}_{R}}\cdot\Xi_{0m}(t^{\prime}):\!\Phi_{1}^{\ell-m}(u_{1},\xi)(t^{\prime})\!:v^{k-\ell}(t^{\prime})\big)dt^{\prime}\end{split}

(4.24)

on the cone
\mathbf{C}_{R}
.

Let
({\bf v}_{0},{\bf v}_{1})
be an extension of

(v_{0},v_{1})
onto
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Given
\vec{v}=(v,\partial_{t}v)
on

\big\{(x,t)\in\mathbf{C}_{R}:t\in[t_{0},\max(t_{0}+\tau,R)]\big\}
,
let
\vec{\bf v}=({\bf v},{\bf v}^{\prime})
be an extension onto
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
Similarly, let
\Phi^{m,\ell-m}_{0,1}

be an extension of

\Xi_{0m}:\!\Phi_{1}^{\ell-m}\!:
from
B_{R}\times[0,R]
to
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,R]
.
Then, proceeding as in the proof of
Proposition 4.1 in
[
40
]
,
it follows
from (
4.24
), Lemma
2.8
, and Sobolev’s inequality that

\displaystyle\|\vec{\Gamma}(v)\|_{\vec{X}_{t_{0},R}^{1-\varepsilon_{k}}(\tau)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|({\bf v}_{0},{\bf v}_{1})\|_{\vec{H}^{1-\varepsilon_{k}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

\displaystyle\quad+\tau^{\theta}\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\sum_{m=0}^{\ell}c_{k,\ell,m}\|\Phi^{m,\ell-m}_{0,1}\|_{L^{p}_{[t_{0},t_{0}+\tau]}W^{-\varepsilon_{k},p}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\|{\bf v}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[t_{0},t_{0}+\tau]}H^{1-\varepsilon_{k}}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{k-\ell}

for some
\theta>0
and
large
p\gg 1
,
where
\varepsilon_{k}=2(k+1)\varepsilon
is as in (
4.19
)
with sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
.
Then, by taking infima over all the extensions

({\bf v}_{0},{\bf v}_{1})
,
\vec{\bf v}
, and
\Phi^{m,\ell-m}_{0,1}

and then using
Definition
4.1
(ii) with (
4.22
), we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\vec{\Gamma}(v)\|_{\vec{X}_{t_{0},R}^{1-\varepsilon_{k}}(\tau)}&\lesssim\|(v_{0},v_{1})\|_{\vec{H}^{1-\varepsilon_{k}}(B_{R-t_{0}})}\\
&\quad+\tau^{\theta}M_{1}\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\sum_{m=0}^{\ell}c_{k,\ell,m}\|\Xi_{0m}\|_{L^{p}_{\tau}W^{-\varepsilon,p}_{x}(B_{R})}\|\vec{v}\|_{\vec{X}_{t_{0},R}^{1-\varepsilon_{k}}(\tau)}^{k-\ell}\\
&\lesssim\|(v_{0},v_{1})\|_{\vec{H}^{1-\varepsilon_{k}}(B_{R-t_{0}})}+\tau^{\theta}M_{0}M_{1}\Big(1+\|\vec{v}\|_{X_{t_{0},R}^{1-\varepsilon_{k}}(\tau)}\Big)^{k}.\end{split}

(4.25)

A similar argument yields the following difference estimate:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\|\vec{\Gamma}(v_{1})-\vec{\Gamma}(v_{2})\|_{\vec{X}^{1-\varepsilon_{k}}_{t_{0},R}(\tau)}\\
&\quad\lesssim\tau^{\theta}M_{0}M_{1}\Big(1+\|\vec{v}_{1}\|_{X_{t_{0},R}^{1-\varepsilon_{k}}(\tau)}^{k-1}+\|\vec{v}_{2}\|_{\vec{X}_{t_{0},R}^{1-\varepsilon_{k}}(\tau)}^{k-1}\Big)\|\vec{v}_{1}-\vec{v}_{2}\|_{\vec{X}_{t_{0},R}^{1-\varepsilon_{k}}(\tau)},\end{split}

(4.26)

where
\vec{v}_{j}=(v_{j},\partial_{t}v_{j})
,
j=1,2
.
Hence, by taking
\tau=\tau\big(\|(v_{0},v_{1})\|_{\vec{H}^{1-\varepsilon_{k}}(B_{R-t_{0}})},M_{0},M_{1}\big)>0

sufficiently small,
the desired claim follows from a standard contraction argument
with (
4.25
) and (
4.26
).
∎

Proposition 4.3

(stability)
.

Suppose that

\Xi_{0}
,
u_{1}
, and
\xi
satisfy (
4.22
)
and that

v=v(\Xi_{0},u_{1},\xi)
is
a solution to (
4.21
)
(
with
t_{0}=0
)

on the cone
\mathbf{C}_{R}
such that

\displaystyle\|\vec{v}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,R];\vec{H}^{1-\varepsilon_{k}}(B_{R-t}))}

\displaystyle\leq M_{2}

(4.27)

for some
M_{2}\geq 1
.
Then, there exists
\delta_{*}=\delta_{*}(R,M_{0},M_{1},M_{2})>0
such that
for any
\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}
satisfying

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\Xi_{0}-\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{W}(R)}&<\delta_{*},\\
\|\Xi_{1}(u_{1},\xi)\|_{\Xi_{0},\widetilde{\Xi}_{0},R}\leq M_{1}&<\infty,\end{split}

(4.28)

where

\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}=(\widetilde{\Xi}_{01},\dots,\widetilde{\Xi}_{0k})

and

\mathbb{W}(R)
is as in (
4.15
),
there exists

\widetilde{v}=\widetilde{v}(\widetilde{\Xi}_{0},u_{1},\xi)\in L^{\infty}([0,R];H^{s}(B_{R-t}))
,
satisfying

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{v}(t)&=-\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\sum_{m=0}^{\ell}{k\choose\ell}{\ell\choose m}\\
&\hphantom{XXX}\times\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{D}(t-t^{\prime})\big(\widetilde{\Xi}_{0m}(t^{\prime}):\!\Phi_{1}^{\ell-m}(t^{\prime})\!:\widetilde{v}^{k-\ell}(t^{\prime})\big)dt^{\prime}\end{split}

(4.29)

such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sup_{0\leq t\leq R}&\|\vec{v}(t)-\vec{\widetilde{v}}(t)\|_{\vec{H}^{1-\varepsilon_{k}}(B_{R-t})}\\
&\leq C_{0}(R,M_{0},M_{1},M_{2})\|\Xi_{0}-\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{W}(R)},\end{split}

(4.30)

where
\vec{v}=(v,\partial_{t}v)
,
\vec{\widetilde{v}}=(\widetilde{v},\partial_{t}\widetilde{v})
, and
\varepsilon_{k}=2(k+1)\varepsilon
is as in (
4.19
).

Proof.

Note that Proposition
4.2
with (
4.22
) and (
4.28
)
guarantees existence of a solution

\widetilde{v}=\widetilde{v}(\widetilde{\Xi}_{0},u_{1},\xi)

to (
4.29
)
on a short time interval.
Namely, it
satisfies

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{v}(t)&=-\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\sum_{m=0}^{\ell}{k\choose\ell}{\ell\choose m}\\
&\hphantom{XXX}\times\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{D}(t-t^{\prime})\big(\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{C}_{R}}\cdot\widetilde{\Xi}_{0m}(t^{\prime}):\!\Phi_{1}^{\ell-m}(t^{\prime})\!:\widetilde{v}^{k-\ell}(t^{\prime})\big)dt^{\prime}\end{split}

(4.31)

on
\mathbf{C}_{R}\cap\{0\leq t\leq\tau\}

for some
\tau>0
,
where

\Phi_{1}=\Phi_{1}(u_{1},\xi)

is as in (
4.10
).
From (
4.23
), we
have

\displaystyle\|\vec{\widetilde{v}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\tau}\vec{H}^{1-\varepsilon_{k}}(B_{R-t})}\lesssim 1.

(4.32)

As for
v
,
it satisfies (
4.21
) (with
t_{0}=0
)
on
\mathbf{C}_{R}
(with an additional cutoff function
\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{C}_{R}}

as in (
4.31
)).

Given an interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}
, let

\displaystyle B_{I}\big(\vec{v},\vec{\widetilde{v}}\,\big)=\|\vec{v}\|_{L^{\infty}(I;\vec{H}^{1-\varepsilon_{k}}(B_{R-t}))}+\|\vec{\widetilde{v}}\|_{L^{\infty}(I;\vec{H}^{1-\varepsilon_{k}}(B_{R-t}))}

(4.33)

Then,
it follows
from (
4.27
) and (
4.32
) that

\displaystyle B_{[0,\tau]}\big(\vec{v},\vec{\widetilde{v}}\,\big)\leq M_{2}+C_{0}^{\prime}\lesssim M_{2}

(4.34)

for
I=[0,\tau]
.
Then, proceeding as in the proof of Proposition
4.2

with
(
4.21
), (
4.31
), Lemma
2.8
, and Sobolev’s inequality
with (
4.22
), (
4.28
), and (
4.34
)
we have

\displaystyle\|\vec{v}-\vec{\widetilde{v}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\tau_{0}}\vec{H}^{1-\varepsilon_{k}}(B_{R-t})}

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim\tau_{0}^{\theta}M_{1}\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\sum_{m=0}^{\ell}c_{k,\ell,m}B_{[0,\tau]}\big(\vec{v},\vec{\widetilde{v}}\,\big)^{k-\ell-1}

\displaystyle\hphantom{XXXX}\times\Big\{B_{[0,\tau]}\big(\vec{v},\vec{\widetilde{v}}\,\big)\|\Xi_{0}-\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{W}(R)}+M_{0}\|\vec{v}-\vec{\widetilde{v}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\tau_{0}}\vec{H}^{1-\varepsilon_{k}}(B_{R-t})}\Big\}

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim\tau_{0}^{\theta}M_{1}M_{2}^{k-1}\Big\{M_{2}\|\Xi_{0}-\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{W}(R)}+M_{0}\|\vec{v}-\vec{\widetilde{v}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\tau_{0}}\vec{H}^{1-\varepsilon_{k}}(B_{R-t})}\Big\}

for any
0<\tau_{0}\leq\tau
.
Hence, by taking
\tau_{0}=\tau_{0}(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2})>0
sufficiently small,
we obtain

\displaystyle\|\vec{v}-\vec{\widetilde{v}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\tau_{0}}\vec{H}^{1-\varepsilon_{k}}(B_{R-t})}\leq C_{1}\|\Xi_{0}-\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{W}(R)}.

By repeating the computation on the second interval
I_{2}=[\tau_{0},2\tau_{0})
,
using (
4.21
) with
t_{0}=\tau_{0}
and an analogous formulation for
\widetilde{v}
,
we have

\displaystyle\|\vec{v}-\vec{\widetilde{v}}\|_{L^{\infty}(I_{2};\vec{H}^{1-\varepsilon_{k}}(B_{R-t}))}\leq C_{1}(1+C_{2})\|\Xi_{0}-\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{W}(R)},

provided that
\delta_{*}=\delta_{*}(M_{2})>0
in (
4.28
) is sufficiently small that

\displaystyle B_{I}\big(\vec{v},\vec{\widetilde{v}}\,\big)\lesssim M_{2}

(4.35)

holds for
I=I_{j}
,
j=1,2
,
where
B_{I}\big(\vec{v},\vec{\widetilde{v}}\,\big)
is as in (
4.33
).
Then, by iterating the computation on the
j
th interval
I_{j}=[(j-1)\tau_{0},j\tau_{0})
,
we obtain

\displaystyle\|\vec{v}-\vec{\widetilde{v}}\|_{L^{\infty}(I_{j};\vec{H}^{1-\varepsilon_{k}}(B_{R-t}))}\leq C_{1}\sum_{i=0}^{j-1}C_{2}^{i}\,\|\Xi_{0}-\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}\|_{\mathbb{W}(R)},

provided that
\delta_{*}=\delta_{*}(j,M_{2})>0
in (
4.28
) is sufficiently small that
(
4.35
) holds for
I=I_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,j
.

Note that our choice of
\tau_{0}=\tau_{0}(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2})
does not depend on
\delta_{*}

in (
4.28
).
Thus, by iterating this argument
\sim\frac{R}{\tau_{0}}
many times
(which requires us to choose

\delta_{*}=\delta_{*}\big(\frac{R}{\tau_{0}},M_{2}\big)=\delta_{*}(R,M_{0},M_{1},M_{2})>0
sufficiently small),
we obtain (
4.30
).
This concludes the proof of Proposition
4.3
.
∎

4.2.
Convergence of the enhanced Gibbs measures

Let
\Xi_{0}(\phi)
be the first enhanced data set defined in (
4.12
).
By viewing
\Xi_{0}
as a map sending
\phi
to
\Xi_{0}(\phi)
,
we consider the pushforward measure
\nu_{L}
of
the
L
-periodic
\Phi^{k+1}_{2}
-measure
\rho_{L}
under the map
\Xi_{0}
.
Namely,
\nu_{L}
is given by

\displaystyle\nu_{L}=(\Xi_{0})_{\#}\rho_{L}.

(4.36)

See also (
1.36
).
In the following, we refer to
\nu_{L}
as the
enhanced Gibbs measure
.
In this subsection, we study convergence properties of the enhanced Gibbs measure
\nu_{L}
,
which
plays an essential role in establishing global well-posedness
of the hyperbolic
\Phi^{k+1}_{2}
-model (
4.1
) on
\mathbb{R}^{2}

(as a limit of the
L
-periodic
hyperbolic
\Phi^{k+1}_{2}
-model (
4.2
))
and invariance of the Gibbs measure
\vec{\rho}_{\infty}

constructed in Theorem
1.2
(i).

Proposition 4.4
.

As probability measures on

\mathbb{W}=\big(C(\mathbb{R}_{+};W^{-\varepsilon,p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\big)^{\otimes k}
,
the family
\{\nu_{L}\}_{L\geq 1}

is tight, and thus
there exists a sequence
\{\nu_{L_{j}}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}

of the
L_{j}
-periodic enhanced Gibbs measures
that converges weakly to some limit
\nu_{\infty}
.
Moreover,

\nu_{L_{j}}
also converges
to the same limit
\nu_{\infty}
in the Wasserstein-1 metric.

Proof.

By the definition (
4.36
),
we have
\nu_{L}=\Law(\Xi_{0}(\phi))

with
\Law(\phi)=\rho_{L}
.
Let
X_{L}
be the solution to the
L
-periodic SNLH (
3.42
)
with
\Law(X_{0,L})=\rho_{L}
.
Then, by the invariance (
3.47
) of
\rho_{L}
under (
3.42
),
we have
\Law(X_{L}(1))=\rho_{L}
, and thus

\nu_{L}=\Law(\Xi_{0}(X_{L}(1))
,
where

\displaystyle\Xi_{0}(X_{L}(1))=\big(\mathcal{S}(t)X_{L}(1),\,:\!(\mathcal{S}(t)X_{L}(1))^{2}\!:\,,\dots,\,:\!(\mathcal{S}(t)X_{L}(1))^{k}\!:\big).

(4.37)

Hence, tightness of
\{\nu_{L}\}_{L\geq 1}

follows once we prove tightness of
\big\{\Law(\Xi_{0}(X_{L}(1))\big\}_{L\geq 1}
.
Once we have tightness of

\{\nu_{L}\}_{L\geq 1}
,
the Prokhorov theorem (Lemma
2.18
)
implies that there exists
a subsequence
of
\{\nu_{L}\}_{L\geq 1}
converging weakly to some limit
\nu_{\infty}
.
Moreover, noting that the metric
d_{\mathbb{W}}
on
\mathbb{W}
defined in (
4.14
) is bounded,
it follows from
Lemma
2.15
and
Remark
2.16

that the same subsequence of

\{\nu_{L}\}_{L\geq 1}
converges to the same limit
\nu_{\infty}

in the Wasserstein-1 metric,
which shows
the second claim in Proposition
4.4
.
Therefore,
we focus on
proving
tightness of
\big\{\Law(\Xi_{0}(X_{L}(1))\big\}_{L\geq 1}

in the following.

By the decomposition (
3.43
),
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(t)X_{L}(1)=\mathcal{S}(t)Y_{L}(1)+\mathcal{S}(t)Z_{L}(1).

(4.38)

Then, from (
2.57
),
we have

\displaystyle:\!(\mathcal{S}(t)X_{L}(1))^{\ell}\!:\,=\sum_{m=0}^{\ell}\binom{\ell}{m}(\mathcal{S}(t)Y_{L}(1))^{\ell-m}:\!(\mathcal{S}(t)Z_{L}(1))^{m}\!:

(4.39)

for
\ell=1,\dots,k
.
When
m\neq 0
, Lemma
2.11
(ii) yields

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\|(\mathcal{S}(t)Y_{L}(1))^{\ell-m}:\!(\mathcal{S}(t)Z_{L}(1))^{m}\!:\|_{W^{-\varepsilon,p}_{\mu}}\\
&\quad\lesssim\|:\!(\mathcal{S}(t)Z_{L}(1))^{m}\!:\|_{W^{-\varepsilon,p_{1}}_{\mu}}\|(\mathcal{S}(t)Y_{L}(1))^{\ell-m}\|_{W^{\varepsilon,p_{2}}_{\frac{\mu}{2^{\delta}}}}\end{split}

(4.40)

for
1<p_{1},p_{2}<\infty

with
p_{1}\gg 1
,
satisfying
\frac{1}{p}\leq\frac{1}{p_{1}}+\frac{1}{p_{2}}\leq\frac{1}{p}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
.
When
m=0
, we simply use the bound:

\displaystyle\|(\mathcal{S}(t)Y_{L}(1))^{\ell}\|_{W^{-\varepsilon,p}_{\mu}}\lesssim\|(\mathcal{S}(t)Y_{L}(1))^{\ell}\|_{W^{\varepsilon,p_{2}}_{\frac{\mu}{2^{\delta}}}}

for
p_{2}\geq p

and apply
the fractional Leibniz rule (Lemma
2.11
(i));
see (
4.58
) below.

\bullet

Part 1:

We first estimate
:\!(\mathcal{S}(t)Z_{L}(1))^{m}\!:\,
,

m=1,\dots,k
.
Recall from (
2.5
) with (
1.23
) that

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(t)

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}\mathcal{D}(t)+\mathcal{D}(t)=e^{-\frac{t}{2}}\cos(t[\hskip-1.70717pt[\nabla]\hskip-1.70717pt])+\frac{1}{2}e^{-\frac{t}{2}}\frac{\sin(t[\hskip-1.70717pt[\nabla]\hskip-1.70717pt])}{[\hskip-1.70717pt[\nabla]\hskip-1.70717pt]},

where

\displaystyle[\hskip-1.70717pt[\nabla]\hskip-1.70717pt]=\sqrt{\tfrac{3}{4}-\Delta}.

(4.41)

Given
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{L}
, we set

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{S}(t)}(\lambda)=e^{-\frac{t}{2}}\cos(t[\hskip-1.70717pt[2\pi\lambda]\hskip-1.70717pt])+\frac{1}{2}e^{-\frac{t}{2}}\frac{\sin(t[\hskip-1.70717pt[2\pi\lambda]\hskip-1.70717pt])}{[\hskip-1.70717pt[2\pi\lambda]\hskip-1.70717pt]},

(4.42)

where
[\hskip-1.70717pt[x]\hskip-1.70717pt]=\sqrt{\frac{3}{4}+|x|^{2}}
.
From (
3.44
), we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(t)Z_{L}(1)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{S}(t)P(1-t^{\prime})\xi_{L}(dt^{\prime}),

(4.43)

where
\xi_{L}
is as in (
4.4
) and (
4.5
).

We first consider the case
m=1
.
From (
4.43
) with (
4.4
) and (
4.5
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathbb{E}&\Big[\big|\langle\nabla\rangle^{-\varepsilon}\big(\phi_{j}\mathcal{S}(t)Z_{L}(1)\big)(x)\big|^{2}\Big]\\
&=\frac{2}{L^{2}}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\bigg|\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{1}{\langle\eta\rangle^{\varepsilon}}\widehat{\mathcal{S}(t)}\big(\tfrac{n}{L}\big)\\
&\hphantom{XXXX}\times\int_{0}^{1}e^{-(1-t^{\prime})\langle\frac{2\pi n}{L}\rangle^{2}}\widehat{\xi}_{L}\big(\tfrac{n}{L},dt^{\prime}\big)\widehat{\phi}_{j}\big(\eta-\tfrac{n}{L}\big)e^{2\pi i\eta\cdot x}d\eta\bigg|^{2}\Bigg].\end{aligned}

(4.44)

Recall that
\big\{\widehat{\xi}_{L}\big(\cdot,\tfrac{n}{L}\big)\big\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}

is a family of independent
temporal white noises
conditioned that
\widehat{\xi}_{L}\big(-\tfrac{n}{L}\big)=\overline{\widehat{\xi}_{L}\big(\tfrac{n}{L}\big)}
,

n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
,
and thus the temporal stochastic integrals in (
4.44
)
are standard Wiener integrals.
Hence, we have

(
4.44
)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{e^{-t}}{L^{2}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\frac{1}{\langle\frac{n}{L}\rangle^{2}}\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{1}{\langle\eta\rangle^{\varepsilon}}\widehat{\phi}_{j}\big(\eta-\tfrac{n}{L}\big)e^{2\pi i\eta\cdot x}d\eta\bigg|^{2}.

(4.45)

From
the triangle inequality

\langle\frac{n}{L}\rangle^{\varepsilon}\lesssim\langle\eta\rangle^{\varepsilon}\langle\eta-\tfrac{n}{L}\rangle^{\varepsilon}

with
(
2.47
) and (
2.48
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bigg|\int\frac{1}{\langle\eta\rangle^{\varepsilon}}\widehat{\phi}_{j}\big(\eta-\tfrac{n}{L}\big)e^{2\pi i\eta\cdot x}d\eta\bigg|&\lesssim\frac{1}{\langle\frac{n}{L}\rangle^{\varepsilon}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big|\langle\eta-\tfrac{n}{L}\rangle^{\varepsilon}\widehat{\phi}_{j}\big(\eta-\tfrac{n}{L}\big)\big|d\eta\\
&\lesssim\frac{1}{\langle\frac{n}{L}\rangle^{\varepsilon}}.\end{split}

(4.46)

On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{1}{\langle\eta\rangle^{\varepsilon}}\widehat{\phi}_{j}\big(\eta-\tfrac{n}{L}\big)e^{2\pi i\eta\cdot x}d\eta=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}G_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\phi_{j}(y)e^{2\pi i\frac{n}{L}\cdot y}dy,

(4.47)

where
G_{\varepsilon}
is the kernel of the Bessel potential
\langle\nabla\rangle^{-\varepsilon}

of order
\varepsilon
.
Recall the following bound on the kernel
G_{\varepsilon}(x)
(see
[
34
, Proposition 1.2.5]
):

\displaystyle|G_{\varepsilon}(x)|\lesssim\begin{cases}e^{-\frac{|x|}{2}},&\text{for }|x|\geq 2,\\
|x|^{\varepsilon-2},&\text{for }|x|<2,\end{cases}

(4.48)

since
0<\varepsilon<2
. Thus, it follows from (
4.47
) and (
4.48
) with (
2.16
) that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bigg|&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{1}{\langle\eta\rangle^{\varepsilon}}\widehat{\phi}_{j}\big(\eta-\tfrac{n}{L}\big)e^{2\pi i\eta\cdot x}d\eta\bigg|\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|G_{\varepsilon}(x-y)||\phi_{j}(y)|dy\\
&=\int_{|x-y|\geq 2}|G_{\varepsilon}(x-y)||\phi_{j}(y)|dy+\int_{|x-y|<2}|G_{\varepsilon}(x-y)||\phi_{j}(y)|dy\\
&\lesssim\int_{|x-y|\geq 2}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{M+2}}\frac{1}{\langle\frac{y}{2^{j}}\rangle^{M}}dy+\int_{|x-y|<2}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{2-\varepsilon}}\frac{1}{\langle\frac{x-y}{2^{j}}\rangle^{M}}\frac{1}{\langle\frac{y}{2^{j}}\rangle^{M}}dy\\
&\lesssim\frac{1}{\langle\frac{x}{2^{j}}\rangle^{M}}\end{split}

(4.49)

for any
M\gg 1
, where, in the third step,
we used the fact that
\langle\frac{x-y}{2^{j}}\rangle\sim 1
for
|x-y|\leq 2
.
Hence, by interpolating (
4.46
) and (
4.49
),
we obtain from (
4.44
), (
4.45
),
and a Riemann sum approximation that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathbb{E}\Big[\big|&\langle\nabla\rangle^{-\varepsilon}\big(\phi_{j}\mathcal{S}(t)Z_{L}(1)\big)(x)\big|^{2}\Big]\lesssim\frac{e^{-t}}{L^{2}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\frac{1}{\langle\frac{n}{L}\rangle^{2+2\varepsilon-}}\frac{1}{\langle\frac{x}{2^{j}}\rangle^{M}}\\
&\lesssim\frac{e^{-t}}{\langle\frac{x}{2^{j}}\rangle^{M}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{1}{\langle y\rangle^{2+2\varepsilon-}}\lesssim\frac{e^{-t}}{\langle\frac{x}{2^{j}}\rangle^{M}}\end{aligned}

(4.50)

for any
M\gg 1
, uniformly in
L\geq 1
.

Given any finite
q\geq p\geq 2
,
it follows from
(
2.22
), Minkowski’s integral inequality,
the Wiener chaos estimate (Lemma
2.12
),
and (
4.50
) that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\big\|\|\mathcal{S}(t)&Z_{L}(1)\|_{W^{-\varepsilon,p}_{\mu}}\big\|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}\leq\Big\|w_{\frac{\mu}{p}}(2^{j})\big\|\langle\nabla\rangle^{-\varepsilon}\big(\phi_{j}\mathcal{S}(t)Z_{L}(1)\big)(x)\big\|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}\Big\|_{\ell^{p}_{j}L^{p}_{x}}\\
&\lesssim q^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big\|w_{\frac{\mu}{p}}(2^{j})\big\|\langle\nabla\rangle^{-\varepsilon}\big(\phi_{j}\mathcal{S}(t)Z_{L}(1)\big)(x)\big\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\Big\|_{\ell^{p}_{j}L^{p}_{x}}\\
&\lesssim q^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{-\frac{t}{2}}\bigg\|w_{\frac{\mu}{p}}(2^{j})\frac{1}{\langle\frac{x}{2^{j}}\rangle^{\frac{M}{2}}}\bigg\|_{\ell^{p}_{j}L^{p}_{x}}\lesssim q^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{-\frac{t}{2}}\|w_{\frac{\mu}{p}}(2^{j})2^{\frac{2}{p}j}\|_{\ell^{p}_{j}}\\
&\lesssim q^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{-\frac{t}{2}},\end{split}

(4.51)

uniformly in
L\geq 1
.

From (
4.42
) and the mean value theorem, we have

\displaystyle|\widehat{\mathcal{S}(t+h)}(\lambda)-\widehat{\mathcal{S}(t)}(\lambda)|\lesssim e^{-\frac{t}{2}}\min\big(1,|h|\langle\lambda\rangle\big)^{\kappa}

(4.52)

for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{L}
and
0\leq\kappa\leq 1
.
Then, by repeating an analogous computation with (
4.52
),
we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\big\|\|\mathcal{S}(t+h)Z_{L}(1)-\mathcal{S}(t)Z_{L}(1)\|_{W^{-\varepsilon,p}_{\mu}}\big\|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}&\lesssim q^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{-\frac{t}{2}}|h|^{\kappa}\end{split}

(4.53)

for any
q\geq 1

and
0<\kappa<\varepsilon
,
uniformly in
L\geq 1
.

Let
T>0
.
In view of (
4.51
)
and (
4.52
), by applying Kolmogorov’s continuity criterion
(
[
5
, Theorem 8.2]
),
we see that

\mathcal{S}(t)Z_{L}(1)\in C^{\alpha}([0,T];W^{-\varepsilon,p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
for some small
\alpha>0
.
Moreover,
the
\alpha
-Hölder semi-norms of
\{\mathcal{S}(t)Z_{L}(1):t\in[0,T]\}

are uniformly (in
L
)
bounded in
L^{q}(\Omega)
for any finite
q\geq 1
.
Therefore,
by noting that the discussion above holds
for any small
\varepsilon>0
and any
\mu>0
,
it follows from
the Arzelà-Ascoli theorem with Lemma
2.2

that
the family
\{\mathcal{S}(t)Z_{L}(1)\}_{L\geq 1}

in
C([0,T];W^{-\varepsilon,p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))

is tight
27
27

27

Namely, the laws
of
\{\mathcal{S}(t)Z_{L}(1):t\in[0,T]\}_{L\geq 1}

are tight as probability measures on

C([0,T];W^{-\varepsilon,p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
.

for any finite
T
, and hence

\{\mathcal{S}(t)Z_{L}(1)\}_{L\geq 1}

in
C(\mathbb{R}_{+};W^{-\varepsilon,p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))

is tight.

Remark 4.5
.

By repeating the argument above
for
e^{\frac{t}{2}}\mathcal{S}(t)Z_{L}(1)
,
we see that

e^{\frac{t}{2}}\mathcal{S}(t)Z_{L}(1)

is almost surely

\alpha
-Hölder continuous in time.
In particular, it grows at most polynomially in time.
From this observation,
we conclude that

\mathcal{S}(t)Z_{L}(1)

is almost surely bounded on
\mathbb{R}_{+}

with values in
W^{-\varepsilon,p}_{\mu}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

We now study the higher powers

:\!(\mathcal{S}(t)Z_{L}(1))^{m}\!:\,
,
m=2,\dots,k
.
From Lemma
2.13
and (
4.43
) with (
4.4
) and (
4.5
), we have
28
28

28

Strictly speaking, in applying Lemma
2.13
, we need to go back to
the frequency truncated version of the Wick power, just as in (
1.25
) for the damped wave equation,
and remove the frequency truncation.

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}\big[:\!(\mathcal{S}(t)Z_{L}(1))^{m}(y)\!:\,:\!(\mathcal{S}(t)Z_{L}(1))^{m}(y^{\prime})\!:\big]\\
&\quad=m!\Big\{\mathbb{E}\big[(\mathcal{S}(t)Z_{L}(1))(y)\cdot(\mathcal{S}(t)Z_{L}(1))(y^{\prime})\big]\Big\}^{m}\\
&\quad\sim\frac{1}{L^{2m}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}e^{2\pi i\frac{n}{L}\cdot(y-y^{\prime})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},\dots,n_{m}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\\
n=n_{1}+\cdots+n_{m}\end{subarray}}\prod_{j=1}^{m}\bigg\{\big(\widehat{\mathcal{S}(t)}\big(\tfrac{n_{j}}{L}\big)\big)^{2}\frac{1-e^{-2\langle\frac{2\pi n_{j}}{L}\rangle^{2}}}{2\langle\frac{2\pi n_{j}}{L}\rangle^{2}}\bigg\}.\end{split}

(4.54)

Then, by (
4.54
)
and interpolating (
4.46
) and (
4.49
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}

\displaystyle\Big[\big|\langle\nabla\rangle^{-\varepsilon}\big(\phi_{j}:\!(\mathcal{S}(t)Z_{L}(1))^{m}\!:\big)(x)\big|^{2}\Big]
